================================================================================
  ABOUT THIS DOCUMENT
================================================================================
  This is the GOLD-STANDARD authoring specification for the CKA learning guide
  (cka.html) — the single source of truth for its content, structure, visual
  language and quality bar.

  Its PURPOSE is to drive an ABSOLUTE REVISION of the material: take the large,
  already-written content of the Edition-1 guide (cka_1.html) — its diagrams,
  explanations, code, tables and exam questions — HARVEST it, PREPOPULATE the
  matching cka.html sections, then AUGMENT, EXPAND and RE-ILLUSTRATE every
  chapter up to the standard defined here. cka.txt owns the structure and the
  bar; cka_1.html supplies a content head-start that is merged in and lifted.

  This document defines:
    1. ART DIRECTION ...... the symbol legend, visual-component catalog, layout.
    2. CONTENT MAP ........ every Part, Chapter, Section & sub-section, in order.
    3. SCAFFOLD ........... the fixed anatomy every Chapter and Part follows.
    4. REFERENCE PROJECT .. how easypj/jobpost grounds each concept end-to-end.
    5. SOURCE HARVEST ..... what to extract from cka_1.html and where it lands.

  MISSION — take a complete beginner from zero → CKA-certified → confident,
  working administrator, WITHOUT ever needing official docs, blogs, books, or
  videos again.

  INTENDED AUDIENCE
  -----------------
    • Primary ... newcomers preparing for the CKA exam (no prior Kubernetes assumed).
    • Secondary . practitioners wanting one deep, exam-AND-production reference for
                  day-to-day Kubernetes administration.

  SCOPE
  -----
    IN  ... 100% of the official 2025 CKA exam domains, PLUS production "Day-2"
            administration (HA, cloud, GitOps, hardening, policy, observability, SRE).
    OUT ... application-developer patterns (CKAD) and deep security specialism
            (CKS) are referenced where useful but are not the focus.

  CONVENTIONS
  -----------
    Difficulty, exam-relevance and content types use a fixed symbol system — see
    SECTION 1 (Symbol & Icon Legend) and SECTION 2 (Visual Component Catalog).
    Reading guidance is in SECTION 0.

  SOURCE MATERIAL & METHOD  (how cka_1.html merges in)
  ----------------------------------------------------
    cka_1.html is the Edition-1 rendered guide — a large, already-populated CKA
    document. It is NOT discarded; it is the primary CONTENT SOURCE, merged in by:
      • EXTRACT ........ pull each chapter's existing content from cka_1.html
                         (concepts, diagrams, tables, code blocks, exam Q&A,
                         takeaways).
      • REMAP .......... place it under THIS document's Part/Chapter/Section
                         structure (Edition-1's 37 chapters redistribute across
                         Edition-2's 45 — see the Content Harvest Map in §8).
                         Rename anihpj → easypj throughout.
      • PREPOPULATE .... seed the matching cka.html sections with that content.
      • AUGMENT/EXPAND/RE-ILLUSTRATE — raise every section to this guide's
                         scaffold, depth and visual standard (more diagrams,
                         🔬 DEEPER blocks, the 2025 syllabus, easypj grounding).
    GOLD-STANDARD RULE — where cka_1.html and this spec disagree, THIS SPEC WINS.
    To prevent a two-spec conflict, cka.txt is the ONLY blueprint in use;
    cka_1.txt (Edition-1's outline) is retired and should not be referenced.

================================================================================
  SECTION 0 — HOW TO READ THIS GUIDE  (the design philosophy)
================================================================================

    THE 5 PROMISES OF THIS GUIDE
    ────────────────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  1. ZERO-ASSUMPTION       Every term is defined the first time it      │
    │                           appears. No "you should already know X."     │
    │  2. SHOW, DON'T TELL       Never 3 paragraphs in a row without a        │
    │                           diagram, table, card, or code block.         │
    │  3. ONE REAL PROJECT       Every concept is demonstrated on easypj/     │
    │                           jobpost — the SAME app, start to finish.     │
    │  4. EXAM + BEYOND          Each topic has an "Exam lens" (what's        │
    │                           tested) AND a "Day-2 lens" (real ops use).   │
    │  5. SELF-CONTAINED         Internals included ("Under the Hood") so you │
    │                           never need to leave this document.           │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    THE LEARNER'S LADDER  (how difficulty is signposted)
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────
       🟢 BEGINNER      Concepts, vocabulary, "what & why"
       🟡 INTERMEDIATE  Hands-on, YAML, kubectl, "how"
       🔴 ADVANCED      Internals, HA, failure modes, "what if it breaks"
       🎯 EXAM TOPIC    Directly graded on the CKA performance exam
       🏢 DAY-2 / BEYOND Real-world administration the exam doesn't test
       🧪 LAB           A hands-on, timed, exam-style task

    PREREQUISITES & YOUR TOOLKIT  (what you need before Chapter 1)
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  💻 Linux CLI comfort: bash, systemctl, journalctl, vi/nano, ssh      │
    │  🐳 Docker basics — Ch 2 teaches exactly what you need                │
    │  🐍 Any programming literacy (examples use Python/Django — easypj)    │
    │  💾 A machine with 8GB+ RAM (16GB for multi-node kubeadm practice)    │
    │  🧰 Tools (installed in Ch 5): minikube/kind, kubectl, helm,          │
    │     kustomize (built into kubectl), + a kubeadm lab (VMs or cloud)    │
    │  🌐 Internet — to pull images and, on the OPEN-BOOK exam, kubernetes.io│
    │  ⏱️ Budget ~50-70 hours; Appendix G has a 12-week plan (8-12h/week).  │
    │  🐍 The example app lives in Dem/easypj (Django 5.2.5 / Python 3.13). │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  SECTION 1 — THE SYMBOL & ICON LEGEND  (used consistently everywhere)
================================================================================
    Every icon below carries a FIXED meaning across the whole guide so your
    eye learns to scan. The HTML render must keep these consistent.

    NAVIGATION & STATUS
    ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  🎯       │ CKA exam topic (graded competency)                        │
    │  🏢       │ Beyond-the-exam / production Day-2 knowledge              │
    │  🧪       │ Hands-on lab / practice task                              │
    │  ⏱️       │ Time budget / exam-speed note                             │
    │  🟢 🟡 🔴 │ Beginner / Intermediate / Advanced difficulty             │
    └───────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    TEACHING CUES
    ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  💡       │ Key insight / "aha" moment                                │
    │  🧠       │ Mental model / analogy                                    │
    │  📖       │ Definition of a term (Key Term box)                       │
    │  🔧       │ Under the Hood (internals / how it really works)          │
    │  ⚠️       │ Gotcha / common mistake / trap                            │
    │  ⚡       │ Speed tip (imperative shortcut, exam time-saver)          │
    │  🔑       │ Must-remember command / fact                              │
    │  ✅ ❌     │ Do / Don't · Correct / Wrong · Pass / Fail               │
    │  🧭       │ Decision guide ("which one do I use?")                    │
    │  🩺       │ Troubleshooting / diagnosis flow                          │
    └───────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    KUBERNETES OBJECT ICONS  (reused in every architecture diagram)
    ┌───────────┬──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │  ☸️  K8s   │ 🧠 Control plane │ 💪 Worker node │ 📦 Pod / Container    │
    │  📡 API srv│ 🗄️  etcd         │ 📋 Scheduler   │ 🔄 Controller mgr    │
    │  🤖 kubelet│ 🌐 kube-proxy    │ 🐋 containerd  │ 🔌 Service           │
    │  🌍 Ingress│ 🚪 Gateway API   │ ⚙️ ConfigMap   │ 🔒 Secret            │
    │  💾 PV/PVC │ 🧱 StorageClass  │ 🛡️ RBAC/SA     │ 🧯 NetworkPolicy     │
    │  🐍 easypj │ 💼 jobpost       │ 🐘 PostgreSQL  │ 📈 HPA / autoscaler  │
    └───────────┴──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘


================================================================================
  SECTION 2 — THE VISUAL COMPONENT CATALOG  (the art direction)
================================================================================
    These are the ONLY building blocks used to render content. The rule:
    "NEVER a wall of text." Every subsection must contain >=1 of these.
    Each entry = [WHAT it is] + [WHEN to use it] + an ASCII mock for the
    HTML generator to follow. cka_1.html's diagrams are a good base — REUSE
    the good ones, but AUGMENT (color, labels, legend, captions) and EXPAND.

    2.1  CONCEPT CARD GRID          — 2-4 cards, icon + title + 1-line idea.
         WHEN: introduce a set of sibling concepts (e.g. 4 Service types).
         [ 📦 Pod ][ 🔄 Deploy ][ 🔌 Service ][ 🌍 Ingress ]

    2.2  ANATOMY CALLOUT            — label every field of a YAML/command.
         WHEN: first time a manifest or command is shown.
         kubectl  get   pods   easypj-web   -n easypj
         └cmd──┘ └verb┘ └kind┘ └──name───┘ └namespace┘

    2.3  COMPARISON TABLE          — side-by-side feature matrix.
         WHEN: "X vs Y" decisions (VM vs container, RWO vs RWX).

    2.4  DECISION TREE 🧭          — branching "which one do I use?" guide.
         WHEN: choosing among options (Deployment vs StatefulSet vs DaemonSet).

    2.5  SEQUENCE / FLOW DIAGRAM   — numbered steps with arrows (left->right
         or top->down). WHEN: a process over time (pod creation, TLS handshake).

    2.6  ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM      — boxed components + connections + legend.
         WHEN: showing how parts of the system relate spatially.

    2.7  MENTAL-MODEL / ANALOGY 🧠 — relate K8s to everyday life.
         WHEN: a concept is abstract (etcd = the cluster's memory/brain).

    2.8  COMMAND CHEAT-CARD 🔑     — grouped, copy-paste commands with notes.
         WHEN: end of every chapter ("most-used commands to remember").

    2.9  YAML SKELETON (annotated) — minimal manifest, every line commented.
         WHEN: teaching the shape of a resource; also used inside lab answers.

    2.10 UNDER THE HOOD 🔧         — collapsible deep-dive (internals, etcd
         keys, controller loops, packet paths). WHEN: once per chapter.

    2.11 GOTCHA BOX ⚠️            — the trap + why it happens + the fix.
         WHEN: a place learners reliably fail (indent, wrong ns, deprecated API).

    2.12 SPEED TIP ⚡             — exam time-saver (dry-run, aliases, $do).
         WHEN: an imperative shortcut beats writing YAML.

    2.13 DAILY-LIFE OPS 🏢        — "in a real job you would..." scenario.
         WHEN: connecting a tested skill to actual administration.

    2.14 TROUBLESHOOTING FLOW 🩺  — symptom -> checks -> cause -> fix tree.
         WHEN: any failure mode (CrashLoopBackOff, NotReady, Pending).

    2.15 BEFORE / AFTER SPLIT     — "without K8s" vs "with K8s" two-panel.
         WHEN: motivating why a feature exists.

    2.16 LIFECYCLE PILLS          — state badges joined by arrows
         (Pending -> Running -> Succeeded/Failed). WHEN: object states.

    2.17 STAT / PROGRESS BARS     — weights, adoption %, exam-domain %.
         WHEN: quantitative emphasis.

    2.18 RELATIONSHIP / MIND MAP  — central node with labeled spokes.
         WHEN: "how everything connects" (object relationship map).

    2.19 KEY TAKEAWAYS BANNER     — ✅ bullet recap + 🎯 exam relevance +
         🔑 commands. WHEN: end of every chapter AND every part.

    2.20 VISUAL SUMMARY           — one big picture that re-tells the whole
         chapter/part in a single diagram. WHEN: closing every chapter/part.

    2.21 PRACTICE DRILL 🧪        — short timed checklist task (do-it-now).
         WHEN: mid/after a chapter, before the formal lab questions.

    2.22 LAB Q&A CARD 🧪          — exam-style question + Show Answer +
         Show Explanation (with YAML/diagram). WHEN: 10+ per chapter, end.

    2.23 EXAM-TOPIC BADGE 🎯      — inline tag marking graded competencies.

    2.24 KEY TERM / GLOSSARY POP 📖 — bold term + tight definition box.

    HTML RENDER RULES (carried from cka_1.html, augmented):
      • Dark GitHub theme; collapsible 3-level sidebar (Part>Chapter>Section).
      • Prism.js syntax highlighting for yaml/bash/python/docker/json.
      • Mac-style code window header (red/yellow/green dots + lang label).
      • Responsive: Desktop >=1000px sidebar pinned (body margin-left);
        Tablet 768-999px sidebar = overlay; Mobile <768px slide-over,
        touch targets >=44px.
      • Smooth-scroll + scrollspy active-link highlight.
      • Every anchor id must exist and be linked from the sidebar.


================================================================================
  SECTION 3 — THE PER-CHAPTER SCAFFOLD  (fixed anatomy of EVERY chapter)
================================================================================
    Every chapter is assembled from these blocks IN THIS ORDER. This is the
    contract the HTML must satisfy for each chapter.

      [A] CHAPTER HEADER      Title + difficulty meta (🟢/🟡/🔴, ⏱️ est time,
                              🎯 domain %, tags).
      [B] WHY THIS MATTERS    1-paragraph hook framed on easypj + a Before/
                              After or analogy visual.
      [C] LEARNING OBJECTIVES "🎯 What you'll be able to DO" — action bullets.
      [D] CKA RELEVANCE       Which exact official competencies this maps to.
      [E] SECTION-MAP DIAGRAM A single diagram showing how all sections of
                              THIS chapter relate (the "you are here" map).
      [F] CONCEPT SECTIONS    The numbered sections (x.1, x.2, ...). Each one:
                                · 📖 key terms · 🧠 analogy where useful
                                · core explanation broken by visuals
                                · annotated YAML / kubectl as relevant
                                · ⚠️ gotcha · ⚡ speed tip where relevant
                                · 🏢 Day-2 note where relevant
      [G] UNDER THE HOOD 🔧   One internals deep-dive for the chapter.
      [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS       ✅ recap + 🎯 exam relevance.
      [I] EXAM GOTCHAS ⚠️     The top traps for this chapter, listed.
      [J] SPEED TIPS ⚡       Imperative shortcuts / dry-run patterns.
      [K] COMMANDS TO REMEMBER 🔑  Cheat-card of must-know commands.
      [L] PRACTICE DRILL 🧪   A short, timed do-it-now checklist.
      [M] LAB QUESTIONS 🧪    >=10 exam-style Q&A. EACH has: Question,
                              Show Answer (commands/steps), Show Explanation
                              (the WHY) + at least one YAML/diagram/table.
      [N] VISUAL SUMMARY      One closing big-picture diagram of the chapter.


================================================================================
  SECTION 4 — THE PER-PART SCAFFOLD  (fixed anatomy of EVERY part)
================================================================================
      [A] PART COVER          Title, difficulty band, exam-domain weight,
                              chapter range, "what you'll build" line.
      [B] PART INTRO          What this part is, why it exists, what it
                              unlocks — framed on the easypj journey.
      [C] "HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT" DIAGRAM   A map showing each chapter in the
                              part and the dependency/flow between them.
      [D] SECTION SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION  (placed BEFORE the chapters) — a
                              concise summary of the whole part and HOW it is
                              implemented end-to-end on easypj.
      [E] CHAPTERS            (each following the Section 3 scaffold)
      [F] PART KEY TAKEAWAYS  ✅ + 🎯 + 🔑 across the whole part.
      [G] PART VISUAL SUMMARY One diagram tying the whole part together.


================================================================================
  SECTION 5 — THE RUNNING PROJECT: easypj / jobpost
================================================================================
    Everything in this guide is taught on ONE real application so concepts
    compound instead of resetting each chapter.

    5.1  WHAT IT IS
         📖 easypj   = the Django PROJECT (the deployable unit / repo root).
         📖 jobpost  = the Django APP inside it (a job-board: post & browse
                       jobs, skills, employers, locations).
         Real code (Dem/easypj): Python 3.13, Django 5.2.5.

    5.2  THE DATA MODEL  (real models from jobpost/models.py)
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  User (django.contrib.auth)                                    │
         │     │ 1─1                                                       │
         │     ▼                                                          │
         │  Author(company, designation)                                  │
         │     │ 1─many (jobpost)                                          │
         │     ▼                                                          │
         │  JobPost(title, description, salary, type, slug, expiry, date) │
         │     ▲ 1─1            ▲ many─many                                │
         │     │                │                                         │
         │  Location(...)     Skills(name)                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         API surface (jobpost/urls.py + views.py):
           GET /jobs/             -> jobpost_list   (JSON list of jobs)
           GET /jobs/<int:id>/    -> jobpost_detail (one job, 404 if missing)
           GET /skills/           -> skills_list    (JSON list of skills)

    5.3  THE CONTAINERIZATION STORY  (the through-line)
         🐳 Dev:    runserver + SQLite (db.sqlite3) — what the repo ships.
         🐳 Build:  multi-stage Dockerfile -> small gunicorn image easypj:vN.
         ☸️ Deploy: Pods (Deployment) + Service + Ingress/Gateway, config via
                    ConfigMap/Secret, data via PostgreSQL (StatefulSet + PVC).
         📖 Why swap SQLite -> PostgreSQL? SQLite is a single file on local
            disk — it cannot be shared by multiple replicas across nodes.
            Kubernetes scales easypj horizontally, so the database must be a
            networked, concurrent server: PostgreSQL (🐘). This swap is the
            single most important "why" that motivates Services, PV/PVC,
            StatefulSets, Secrets, and NetworkPolicy later in the guide.

    5.4  TARGET ARCHITECTURE  (what we build by the end)
         ┌─────────────────────── NAMESPACE: easypj ───────────────────────┐
         │  🌍 Ingress / 🚪 Gateway  (easypj.example.com)                    │
         │              │                                                    │
         │       🔌 Service: easypj-web (ClusterIP)                          │
         │        ┌─────┼───────────────┐                                   │
         │     📦 web-0  📦 web-1  📦 web-2   (Deployment, replicas=3, 📈HPA) │
         │        │  envFrom ⚙️ ConfigMap + 🔒 Secret                        │
         │        ▼                                                          │
         │       🔌 Service: easypj-db (headless)                            │
         │        │                                                         │
         │     🐘 postgres-0  (StatefulSet) ── 💾 PVC ── 🧱 StorageClass     │
         │                                                                   │
         │  🛡️ RBAC(SA+Role)  🧯 NetworkPolicy  PDB  CronJob(db-backup)      │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    5.5  WHERE THE PROJECT APPEARS (mapping)  — every part advances easypj:
         P1 understand it · P2 deploy it · P3 run it on a real cluster ·
         P4 scale/schedule it · P5 connect/expose it · P6 give it storage ·
         P7 secure it · P8 maintain it · P9 troubleshoot it · P10 observe it ·
         P11 ship it (CI/CD) · P12 extend/harden it · P13 prove it (exam).


================================================================================
  SECTION 6 — CKA EXAM AT A GLANCE (2025)  &  OFFICIAL COMPETENCY MAP
================================================================================

    6.1  THE FACTS
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Duration: 2 hours    Format: performance-based (live CLI)     │
         │  ~15-20 hands-on tasks   Pass: 66%   Open book: kubernetes.io  │
         │  + helm.sh & Gateway API docs   1 free retake   Valid: 3 yrs   │
         │  Version: tracks current k8s (this guide targets v1.31+/2025)  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    6.2  DOMAIN WEIGHTS  (official, 2025)
         🔴 Troubleshooting .......................... 30%  ████████████
         🟡 Cluster Architecture, Install & Config ... 25%  ██████████
         🟡 Services & Networking .................... 20%  ████████
         🟢 Workloads & Scheduling ................... 15%  ██████
         🟢 Storage .................................. 10%  ████

    6.3  OFFICIAL COMPETENCY -> CHAPTER MAP  (so nothing is missed)
         CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE, INSTALL & CONFIG (25%)
           • Manage RBAC ................................ Ch 24, App-RBAC
           • Prepare infra for install ................. Ch 9
           • Create/manage clusters with kubeadm ....... Ch 9, 12
           • Manage cluster lifecycle (upgrades) ....... Ch 28

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  Chapters 1-4  ·  Difficulty: Beginner  ·  "What is all this, and why?"
  What you'll build by the end: a clear mental model of a cluster and the

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 1 INTRO]
  Before you can administer a cluster you must be able to SEE it in your head.
  Part 1 builds that picture from nothing: why Kubernetes exists, what a
  container actually is, the machines and brains that make up a cluster, and
  the handful of objects you'll use every single day. We anchor it all on
  easypj so the abstractions have a face.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 1 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Ch1 WHY ───────▶ Ch2 HOW (containers) ───────▶ Ch3 WHERE (architecture)│
  │   the problem        the unit K8s runs            the machines & brains  │
  │      │                                                   │               │
  │      └───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┘               │
  │                              ▼                                            │
  │                 Ch4 WHAT (the core objects you declare)                   │
  │            Pod · ReplicaSet · Deployment · Service · Namespace            │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 1 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION  — read before the chapters]
  By the end you will have: installed nothing yet, but you can explain the
  control-plane/worker split, draw how "kubectl apply" turns YAML into a
  running easypj pod, and identify every object in the target architecture
  (Section 5.4). Implementation thread: we take easypj from "an app that runs
  on my laptop" to "an app described as desired state that a cluster keeps
  alive." VISUAL: reuse cka_1's WITHOUT-vs-WITH-Kubernetes split (augment
  with the 🐍 easypj icon and a caption).


  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 1: What Is Kubernetes & Why Administrators Need It           🟢 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟢 Beginner · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Domain: Cluster Architecture (foundation)
  [B] WHY THIS MATTERS
      easypj runs fine on your laptop with `python manage.py runserver`. The
      moment real users arrive it must survive a 3 AM crash, a traffic spike, a
      bad deploy and a dead server — AUTOMATICALLY, without you waking up. That
      automation IS Kubernetes, and building & operating the platform that
      provides it is the CKA's job. This chapter installs the mental model the
      rest of the guide is built on.  VISUAL: Before/After split (below).
  [C] OBJECTIVES — after this chapter you can:
      ✅ define "container orchestration" in one sentence;
      ✅ give the one-line definition of Kubernetes + the desired/actual model;
      ✅ distinguish CKA (administrator) from CKAD (developer) work;
      ✅ name & place the 5 core concepts (cluster, control plane, nodes, pod,
         desired state);
      ✅ identify the major ecosystem tools (CNCF, runtimes, CNI, Helm, GitOps);
      ✅ describe what a Kubernetes administrator actually does day to day.
  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: not a graded task by itself, but every Architecture (25%)
      and Troubleshooting (30%) question assumes this model — get it wrong and
      nothing else lands.
  [E] SECTION MAP (you are here):
      1.1 the problem → 1.2 the definition → 1.3 admin vs dev → 1.4 the 5 core
      concepts → 1.5 the ecosystem → 1.6 real-world usage → 1.7 the object model
      & the Kubernetes API.

  ── 1.1  The Problem Before Kubernetes ──────────────────────────────  🟢
     Before orchestration: one app, one server. To run easypj for real you'd
     hand-install Python, Django, gunicorn, nginx and PostgreSQL on a box and
     pray. The pain:
       • 💀 Single point of failure — the box dies, easypj is down, full stop.
       • 😱 3 AM pager — a crashed process restarts only when YOU ssh in.
       • 📈 Scaling = capex — more traffic means buying & configuring another
         server by hand.
       • 🚀 Risky deploys — copy files, restart, hope; no safe rollback.
     📖 KEY TERM — Container orchestration: automated management of containerized
        apps across many machines (placement, scaling, healing, networking,
        rollout) done by software, not humans.
     🧠 ANALOGY: a lone server is a solo musician; Kubernetes is the conductor of
        an orchestra — it decides who plays what and covers anyone who drops out.

  ── 1.2  What Is Kubernetes? (the simple answer) ────────────────────  🟢
     📖 DEFINITION: "an open-source system for automating deployment, scaling and
        management of containerized applications."
     The heart is DECLARATIVE control: you describe the END STATE you want
     ("3 copies of easypj:v2, always up") and Kubernetes continuously makes
     reality match it — restarting, rescheduling and scaling on its own.
        💡 Desired State  →  [control loops]  →  Actual State  (reconciliation)
     Sticky trivia: name = Greek κυβερνήτης ("helmsman"); descends from Google's
     "Borg"; open-sourced 2014; donated to the CNCF; written in Go. K8s = K+8+s.

  ── 1.3  The CKA Perspective: Administrator vs Developer ────────────  🟢 🎯
     Two Linux Foundation certs, two jobs:
       👩‍💻 CKAD (Developer) — "I deploy & configure APPS on a cluster someone
          else runs" (Pods, Deployments, Services, ConfigMaps, probes).
       🔧 CKA (Administrator) — "I BUILD, OPERATE & TROUBLESHOOT the CLUSTER
          ITSELF" ← this guide (kubeadm install, certs/PKI, etcd backup,
          upgrades, node mgmt, RBAC, CNI/DNS, storage, outages, HA/DR).
     🧠 ANALOGY: CKAD furnishes the rooms; CKA builds & maintains the house —
        foundations, plumbing and wiring included.

  ── 1.4  The 5 Core Concepts — the big picture ──────────────────────  🟢
     Everything else is built from five ideas:
       1. 🗂️ CLUSTER — a set of machines K8s manages as one.
       2. 🧠 CONTROL PLANE — the "brain": API, state, scheduling, reconciliation.
       3. 💪 WORKER NODES — the "muscle": actually run your containers.
       4. 📦 POD — smallest deployable unit (1+ containers sharing IP & storage).
       5. 🔄 DESIRED vs ACTUAL STATE — you declare desired; controllers drive
          actual toward it, forever.

  ── 1.5  The Kubernetes Ecosystem ──────────────────────────────────  🟢 🏢
     A core that everything plugs into (all governed by the CNCF):
       • 🐋 Runtimes — containerd, CRI-O (via the CRI).
       • 🌐 CNI plugins — Calico, Cilium, Flannel (pod networking).
       • 💾 CSI drivers — cloud / Ceph / NFS storage.
       • 📦 Packaging — Helm (charts), Kustomize (overlays).
       • 🔄 GitOps — ArgoCD, FluxCD (git as source of truth).
       • 🕸️ Service mesh — Istio, Linkerd.
       • 📊 Observability — Prometheus, Grafana, OpenTelemetry.

  ── 1.6  How Companies Use Kubernetes (a day in an admin's life) ────  🟢 🏢
     Scale changes the job, not the fundamentals:
       🏠 Startup (5–20) — 1 small cluster (minikube → managed); one admin
          deploys easypj, monitors, basic troubleshooting.
       🏢 Mid-size (50–200) — dev/staging/prod; CI/CD, RBAC, upgrades, cost
          control; 2–4 admins.
       🏙️ Enterprise (500+) — many HA, multi-region clusters; DR, compliance,
          GitOps, service mesh; a dedicated platform team.
     🏢 A CKA admin's day: check cluster/node health → read events & logs →
        ship changes via GitOps/PRs → drain-patch-uncordon a node → verify etcd
        backups → handle an incident → capacity-plan.

  ── 1.7  The Object Model & the Kubernetes API ──────────────────────  🟢 🎯
     Everything you create is a Kubernetes OBJECT — a persisted record of intent.
     Four ideas make the whole API click:
     • THE 4 REQUIRED FIELDS (every manifest, no exceptions):
         apiVersion — which API group+version defines it (e.g. apps/v1)
         kind       — the object type (e.g. Deployment); immutable
         metadata   — identity: name (required), namespace, labels, annotations,
                      and the server-set uid
         spec       — your DESIRED state (shape varies per kind)
       Omit one (or mis-case `kind`) → the apiserver rejects it before etcd.
     • SPEC vs STATUS: spec = what YOU declared; status = what the cluster
       observes & writes (read-only). The reconcile loop just keeps status → spec.
     • NAMING RULES (validation, not taste):
         DNS Subdomain (most objects): ≤253 chars, lowercase a–z 0–9 - . ;
            start/end alphanumeric (may start with a digit).   e.g. easypj-web
         DNS Label (namespaces, nodes): ≤63 chars, lowercase a–z 0–9 - (NO dots);
            MUST START WITH A LETTER, end alphanumeric.  ❌ easypj_web · EasyPJ · 9web
         Service names also follow RFC 1035 (must start with a letter).
         UID — cluster-unique, auto-generated, never user-set.
     • API GROUPS, VERSIONS & STABILITY:
         core group  → /api/v1            (Pod, Service, ConfigMap, Namespace, Node)
         named groups → /apis/<grp>/<ver> (apps/v1, batch/v1, rbac.../v1 …)
         alpha (v1alpha1, off by default, may vanish) → beta (v1beta1, may change)
            → stable/GA (v1, backward-compatible).
         Deprecation policy: a GA API is NOT removed within a major version; a
            deprecated BETA is dropped after 9 months or 3 releases (whichever is
            longer); alpha can change any release. No LTS — each minor release is
            maintained ~14 months (≈12 months + a ~2-month maintenance window).
         `kubectl api-resources` lists every kind → its group, version, namespaced?
     • OBJECT MANAGEMENT — 3 approaches (the exam loves `apply`):
         imperative cmd  → kubectl create deploy easypj-web --image=easypj:v2
         imperative cfg  → kubectl create -f f.yaml / replace -f  (NOT idempotent)
         declarative     → kubectl apply -f f.yaml  (idempotent — the default)
         Server-Side Apply (`--server-side`) tracks field ownership in
            managedFields so several controllers co-own one object cleanly.
     • ADD-ONS a fresh cluster still needs: CoreDNS (in-cluster DNS, usually
       installed) · metrics-server (powers `kubectl top` + HPA, NOT default) ·
       an Ingress controller (you pick & install) · Dashboard (optional UI).
     📖 KEY TERM — Object: a persisted etcd entry expressing intent (spec) and
        observed reality (status), reconciled by a controller.
     🧠 ANALOGY: a manifest is a work order — apiVersion/kind name the shop, the
        metadata is the label, the spec is the instructions; status is the shop's
        progress note you read back.

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 1
  [WITHOUT vs WITH KUBERNETES — the easypj story]
  ┌──────────────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────────────┐
  │ ❌ WITHOUT K8s                │   │ ✅ WITH K8s                   │
  │  ┌────────────────────────┐  │   │  ┌────┐ ┌────┐ ┌────┐  ← auto │
  │  │ 🖥️ ONE server          │  │   │  │📦web│ │📦web│ │📦web│   heal│
  │  │  🐍 easypj             │  │   │  └────┘ └────┘ └────┘  & scale │
  │  │  🐘 postgres           │  │   │   spread across nodes          │
  │  │  🌐 nginx              │  │   │  ┌──────┐   ┌──────┐           │
  │  │  💀 crash = ALL down   │  │   │  │Node 1│   │Node 2│ 🐘 db on  │
  │  │  😱 manual restart 3AM │  │   │  └──────┘   └──────┘  its PVC  │
  │  └────────────────────────┘  │   │  Self-healing 24/7 🎉          │
  └──────────────────────────────┘   └──────────────────────────────┘

  [DECLARATIVE — you DECLARE, Kubernetes DELIVERS]
   📝 YOU WRITE (YAML)            ⚙️ KUBERNETES GUARANTEES
   ┌───────────────────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────────┐
   │ kind: Deployment      │     │ ✅ 3 easypj pods always up    │
   │ replicas: 3           │ ──► │ ✅ all on image easypj:v2     │
   │ image: easypj:v2      │     │ ✅ crash? restarted in secs   │
   │ resources: 256Mi      │     │ ✅ node dies? rescheduled     │
   └───────────────────────┘     │ ✅ new version? 0-downtime    │
       "DESIRED state"           └──────────────────────────────┘
                                    "ACTUAL state" (reconciled)
   💡 You never say "start a container on node 2" — you declare the END state
      and the control loop makes reality match it, continuously, forever.

  [THE 5 CORE CONCEPTS — one cluster at a glance]
  ┌──────────────────────── 🗂️ CLUSTER ─────────────────────────┐
  │  🧠 CONTROL PLANE  ("the brain" — decides)                   │
  │     📡 API server · 🗄️ etcd · 📋 scheduler · 🔄 controllers   │
  │            │   desired vs actual state reconciled            │
  │  ──────────┼───────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  💪 WORKER NODES  ("the muscle" — run)                       │
  │   ┌────────────────┐        ┌────────────────┐              │
  │   │ 📦 easypj pod   │        │ 📦 easypj pod   │  you said    │
  │   │ 📦 easypj pod   │        │ 🐘 db pod       │  "3 web";    │
  │   └────────────────┘        └────────────────┘  K8s keeps 3 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [CKA vs CKAD — who owns what]
   👩‍💻 CKAD owns ▸ Pods · Deployments · Services · ConfigMaps · probes
   ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
   🔧 CKA owns  ▸ ⚙️ install (kubeadm) · 🔐 certs/PKI · 🗄️ etcd backup ·
                  ⬆️ upgrades · 🖥️ node mgmt · 🛡️ RBAC · 🌐 CNI/DNS ·
                  💾 storage · 🩺 troubleshooting · 🏗️ HA/DR
   💡 The administrator owns EVERYTHING BELOW the app.

  [THE KUBERNETES ECOSYSTEM — a core everything plugs into]
                       📦 Helm / Kustomize
                              │
          🔄 ArgoCD ───┐      │      ┌─── 🌐 Calico / Cilium
                       ╲      │      ╱
                    ┌──────────────────┐
        🐋 containerd │   ☸️  KUBERNETES  │ 📊 Prometheus
                    │      (CNCF)       │
                    └──────────────────┘
                       ╱      │      ╲
          🕸️ Istio ────┘      │      └─── 💾 CSI drivers
                              │
                       📈 Grafana / OTel

  [COMPANY SIZE → K8s USAGE → ADMIN LOAD]
   ┌────────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
   │ 🏠 Startup  │ 1 cluster, few nodes  │ deploy + monitor; 1 admin  │
   │ 🏢 Mid-size │ dev / staging / prod  │ CI/CD, RBAC, upgrades; 2–4 │
   │ 🏙️ Enterprise│ many HA, multi-region │ DR, compliance, GitOps;   │
   │            │                       │ a platform team            │
   └────────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

  [G] 🔧 UNDER THE HOOD: the reconciliation loop (observe -> diff -> act);
      level-triggered vs edge-triggered (why K8s self-heals reliably).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the closed-loop control model (idempotent,
      eventually consistent); EVERYTHING is an API object reconciled by a
      controller; why that makes K8s a universal control plane.
      • API-server WRITE PIPELINE: authN → authZ(RBAC) → mutating admission →
        schema validation → validating admission → persist to etcd.
      • Controllers scale via SHARED INFORMERS: one LIST+WATCH per resource type,
        cached in memory and shared by all controllers → load is O(resource
        types), not O(controllers). Failed reconciles re-queue with exponential
        backoff (≈5ms, doubling, capped ~1000s), deduped, reset on success.
      • WATCH machinery: resourceVersion is an optimistic-concurrency cursor —
        write with a stale rv → 409 Conflict → re-read & retry; periodic BOOKMARK
        events prove a watch is alive; a too-old rv → 410 Gone → client re-LISTs.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: orchestration automates ops; declarative not imperative;
      admin owns everything below the app; API server is the only path to etcd.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: "K8s replaces Docker" (no — it orchestrates containers);
      thinking imperatively ("start a container") instead of declaratively.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: think in desired state; `kubectl explain` is your in-exam
      manual; learn to read events first.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: kubectl cluster-info · get nodes -o wide ·
      get componentstatuses · api-resources · version.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: in 5 min, sketch the cluster from memory and label each box.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — each rendered with a full answer + explanation + a small
      diagram/table:
      Q1  Define "container orchestration" in one sentence.
          → automated deploy/scale/heal/connect of containers across machines.
      Q2  easypj's pod crashes at 3 AM — what does K8s do, and why?
          → a controller sees actual≠desired and recreates it (level-triggered
            reconciliation); no human needed.
      Q3  Give the one-line definition of Kubernetes.
      Q4  Explain desired-vs-actual state using a `replicas: 3` example.
      Q5  List three tasks that belong to CKA but NOT CKAD.
      Q6  Name the four control-plane components and the one that stores state.
      Q7  What is a Pod, and what do containers inside it share?
      Q8  Why is `kubectl apply` idempotent? (declarative + reconciliation)
      Q9  Match each tool to its job: Calico · Helm · ArgoCD · Prometheus ·
          containerd.
      Q10 Sort "admin-owns vs dev-owns": etcd backup · a Deployment · RBAC · a
          ConfigMap · a node upgrade.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: one diagram — "From your YAML to a running easypj pod."

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 2: Containers & Docker — The Unit Kubernetes Runs            🟢 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟢 Beginner · ⏱️ ~2h · Foundation for Cluster Architecture (CRI).
  [B] WHY: K8s schedules CONTAINERS. To debug a pod that won't start you must
      understand images, layers, and runtimes. VISUAL: "works on my machine"
      problem panel grounded on easypj (missing psycopg2, wrong Python).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain containers vs VMs; read/write a Dockerfile; build the
      easypj image; use core docker/crictl commands; explain CRI & why Docker
      was removed in v1.24.
  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: 🎯 CRI is an "extension interface" (Cluster Arch 25%);
      runtime-level debugging appears in Troubleshooting (30%).

  ── 2.1  Why Containers? the "works on my machine" problem ──────────  🟢
     easypj works on your laptop and explodes in production: a different Python,
     a missing psycopg2, no env vars. A CONTAINER fixes this by packaging the app
     WITH everything it needs — code + Python 3.13 + Django 5.2.5 + gunicorn +
     psycopg2 + config — into one immutable image that runs identically anywhere.
     🧠 ANALOGY: a shipping container — the same sealed box rides any ship, truck
        or crane. (See the "works on my machine" panel below.)

  ── 2.2  Containers vs Virtual Machines ─────────────────────────────  🟢
     A VM virtualizes HARDWARE (a full guest OS per app → GB, boots in minutes).
     A container virtualizes the OS — all containers SHARE the host kernel → MB,
     starts in milliseconds.
     ┌───────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
     │           │ Virtual Machine   │ Container            │
     │ Size      │ Gigabytes         │ Megabytes            │
     │ Start     │ minutes           │ seconds / ms         │
     │ OS        │ full guest OS     │ shares host kernel   │
     │ Isolation │ hardware-level    │ process-level        │
     └───────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  ── 2.3  Docker Basics for Admins ───────────────────────────────────  🟢
     📖 Dockerfile (recipe) → `docker build` → IMAGE (read-only template) →
        `docker run` → CONTAINER (running instance) → `docker push` → REGISTRY
        (Docker Hub / ECR / GCR). An image is built from stacked, cached LAYERS.

  ── 2.4  Building the easypj Image ──────────────────────────────────  🟡 🧪
     A production multi-stage Dockerfile keeps the final image small & safe:
     Dockerfile.production:
       # ── stage 1: builder ──
       FROM python:3.13-slim AS builder
       WORKDIR /app
       COPY requirements.txt .
       RUN pip install --no-cache-dir --prefix=/install -r requirements.txt
       # ── stage 2: slim runtime ──
       FROM python:3.13-slim
       RUN useradd -m app                       # run as non-root
       WORKDIR /app
       COPY --from=builder /install /usr/local
       COPY . .
       RUN python manage.py collectstatic --noinput
       USER app
       EXPOSE 8000
       CMD ["gunicorn","easypj.wsgi:application","-b","0.0.0.0:8000"]
     .dockerignore → .git  __pycache__  *.pyc  db.sqlite3  .env  venv/
     🏢 Multi-stage = smaller image, fewer CVEs, faster pulls (builder discarded).

  ── 2.5  Essential Container Commands ───────────────────────────────  🟡
     Build/run with Docker; on a NODE (where kubectl/Docker may be absent) use
     crictl against containerd:
     ┌──────────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
     │ Docker (build host)      │ crictl / ctr (on the node)  │
     │ docker ps                │ crictl ps                   │
     │ docker logs <id>         │ crictl logs <id>            │
     │ docker exec -it <id> sh  │ crictl exec -it <id> sh     │
     │ docker inspect <id>      │ crictl inspect <id>         │
     │ docker images            │ crictl images / ctr images  │
     └──────────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────┘
     ⚡ crictl is your lifeline when the kubelet/apiserver is down.

  ── 2.6  Container Runtimes Beyond Docker (CRI) ─────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     📖 CRI (Container Runtime Interface) is the socket the kubelet talks to.
        Since v1.24 the "dockershim" is gone — nodes RUN containers via
        containerd or CRI-O. You still BUILD images with Docker/buildkit; the
        node just doesn't need the Docker daemon. (See runtime-evolution diagram.)

  ── 2.7  Images in Kubernetes — Pull Policy, Registries & Failures ──  🟡 🎯
     Building easypj:v1 was the laptop side; here's how the CLUSTER names, pulls,
     authenticates and garbage-collects images — and the pull errors you'll debug.
     • IMAGE REFERENCE: [REGISTRY[:PORT]/]NAME[:TAG][@DIGEST].
         bare `easypj` → docker.io/library/easypj:latest (default registry + tag!)
         `easypj@sha256:…` → pinned by immutable digest (exact bytes, ignores tags)
     • TAG vs DIGEST: a tag is MUTABLE (someone can re-push :v1) → two nodes may
        run different builds; a DIGEST is immutable & tamper-evident — pin prod.
     • imagePullPolicy DEFAULTS (inferred from the tag — exam favourite):
         :latest or no tag → Always (pull every start)
         a fixed tag (:v1) → IfNotPresent (use the node cache)
         @sha256:… digest  → IfNotPresent
         Override: Always | IfNotPresent | Never  (Never = must already be on node)
     • PRIVATE REGISTRIES: `kubectl create secret docker-registry regcred …`, then
        spec.imagePullSecrets (or attach it to the ServiceAccount so every pod
        inherits it). Missing/bad secret → ImagePullBackOff with "unauthorized".
     • PULL FAILURE STATES: ErrImagePull (a pull just failed) → ImagePullBackOff
        (retried, backing off ≤ ~5min) · InvalidImageName (malformed ref).
        Diagnose: `kubectl describe pod` → Events names the exact error.
     • SERIAL PULLS: the kubelet pulls one image at a time per node by default
        (serializeImagePulls / maxParallelImagePulls) → big images leave pods in
        ContainerCreating for a while.
     • IMAGE GARBAGE COLLECTION: the kubelet deletes unused images when node disk
        crosses imageGCHighThresholdPercent (default 85%), down to Low (80%);
        images in use are never removed; staying high → DiskPressure → eviction.
     • RuntimeClass: maps a name → a runtime handler the node supports; a pod opts
        in via spec.runtimeClassName to run in a stronger sandbox (gVisor / Kata).
     📖 KEY TERM — imagePullPolicy: when the kubelet (re)pulls an image; its
        DEFAULT is inferred from the tag, not fixed.
     🧠 ANALOGY: a tag is a sticky note on a box (can be moved to another box); a
        digest is the box's tamper-proof serial number.

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 2
  [CONTAINERS vs VMs — shared kernel = light & fast]
   VIRTUAL MACHINES                CONTAINERS
   ┌─────┐ ┌─────┐                 ┌──────┐ ┌─────┐
   │App A│ │App B│                 │easypj│ │nginx│
   │Libs │ │Libs │                 │Libs  │ │Libs │
   │Guest│ │Guest│                 └──┬───┘ └──┬──┘
   │ OS  │ │ OS  │                 ┌──▼────────▼──┐
   ├─────┴─┴─────┤                 │ container rt  │ (containerd)
   │ Hypervisor  │                 ├───────────────┤
   ├─────────────┤                 │  Host kernel  │ (SHARED)
   │   Host OS   │                 ├───────────────┤
   └─────────────┘                 │   Hardware    │
   GB · minutes                    MB · seconds   └───────────────┘

  [IMAGE LAYERS — easypj built in stages, cached]
   FROM python:3.13-slim        ◄ base layer (shared / cached)
   COPY requirements + pip install ◄ deps layer (reused until reqs change)
   COPY . (easypj source)       ◄ app layer (changes most -> put it LAST)
   CMD gunicorn ...             ► image easypj:v1 ─► a running 📦 container
   💡 multi-stage build discards the builder -> small, fewer-CVE final image.

  ["WORKS ON MY MACHINE" — why containers exist]
   👩‍💻 laptop:   easypj runs ✅            🖥️ server:   ModuleNotFoundError 💀
   WHY: different Python · missing psycopg2 · no env vars · different OS
   FIX: 📦 package EVERYTHING into one image →
        ┌──────────────────────────────┐
        │ 📦 easypj:v1                  │  Python 3.13 · Django 5.2.5 ·
        │  gunicorn · psycopg2 · code   │  runs IDENTICALLY everywhere
        └──────────────────────────────┘

  [CONTAINER RUNTIME EVOLUTION — Docker out of the data path (v1.24)]
   BEFORE v1.24             AFTER v1.24
   🤖 kubelet               🤖 kubelet
      │                        │  CRI
   dockershim (removed)     ┌──┴───┐
      │                     ▼      ▼
   Docker daemon         containerd  CRI-O
      │                     │        │
   containerd              📦        📦   containers (no Docker daemon)
   💡 You still BUILD images with Docker; nodes RUN them via containerd.

  [G] 🔧 UNDER THE HOOD: namespaces + cgroups + union FS = the 3 pillars of
      isolation; how an image layer becomes a running rootfs.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): OCI image/runtime specs; content-addressable
      digests (@sha256); the pause/sandbox container; rootless & user
      namespaces; node image GC; buildkit/kaniko/buildah; imagePullSecrets.
      • The 8 Linux namespaces (pid·net·mnt·uts·ipc·user·cgroup·time); inside a
        pod NET + IPC are SHARED (why containers reach each other on localhost),
        held open by the pause container; PID optional (shareProcessNamespace).
      • cgroups v1 vs v2: v2 is one unified tree (enables PSI, MemoryQoS); v1 is
        DEPRECATED as of v1.35 (kubelet won't start on a v1 node by default).
        kubelet & runtime MUST share the same cgroup driver (systemd).
      • overlayfs copy-on-write: reads fall through the layers; a write copies the
        file UP into the ephemeral upper layer; a delete writes a "whiteout".
      • Exit codes: 0 clean · 1–128 app error · 137 = SIGKILL/OOMKilled ·
        143 = SIGTERM · 139 = SIGSEGV.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: containers share the host kernel (light & fast); an image is
      cached layers; K8s runs containers via the CRI (containerd), not Docker.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: `:latest` tag (non-reproducible); running as root; forgetting
      the CRI socket; cgroup-driver mismatch (must be systemd) → kubelet won't start.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: pin image digests; order Dockerfile layers cheap→expensive
      (deps before source) for cache hits; reach for `crictl` when kubectl is dead.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: docker build/run/ps/logs/exec/push · crictl ps/logs/inspect ·
      ctr images · nerdctl.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: build easypj:v1 locally, run it, curl /jobs/.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation + diagram each:
      Q1  State two differences between a VM and a container.
      Q2  A pod is ImagePullBackOff — give 3 likely causes and how to confirm.
      Q3  Why does a multi-stage build produce a smaller, safer image?
      Q4  kubectl is down on a node — how do you list & log containers? (crictl)
      Q5  What is the CRI, and what replaced dockershim in v1.24?
      Q6  Reorder a Dockerfile so `pip install` stays cached across code edits.
      Q7  Name three ways to shrink the easypj image.
      Q8  Why run as non-root, and how? (Dockerfile USER + securityContext)
      Q9  Why is `:latest` dangerous; what to use instead? (pinned tags/digests)
      Q10 A node's kubelet won't start citing cgroups — what's the fix?
          (set containerd SystemdCgroup=true to match the kubelet)
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: "easypj source -> image -> pod" pipeline.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 3: Kubernetes Architecture — The Admin's Blueprint          🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Architecture (25%).
  [B] WHY: every troubleshooting question is "which component failed?" You
      can't fix what you can't name. VISUAL: full annotated cluster diagram.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: name & explain every control-plane + node component; trace
      a pod's creation end-to-end; describe the 3 network layers; explain
      static pods.
  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: 🎯 core of the 25% domain; prerequisite for all of
      Troubleshooting (30%).
  [E] SECTION-MAP: control plane -> nodes -> pod-creation journey -> network
      -> static pods -> node health & control-plane↔node communication.

  ── 3.1  Cluster Architecture Overview ──────────────────────────────  🟡
     A cluster = CONTROL-PLANE node(s) that make every decision + WORKER nodes
     that run the workloads. As a CKA admin you install, configure and repair
     BOTH. (See the complete-cluster diagram below.)

  ── 3.2  Control-Plane Components — deep dive ───────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     a) 📡 kube-apiserver — the ONLY front door. Every kubectl call, component
        and controller talks to it; it does authN → authZ(RBAC) → admission →
        then reads/writes etcd. Stateless & horizontally scalable.
     b) 🗄️ etcd — the distributed Raft key-value store holding ALL cluster state
        (/registry/...). Ports 2379 (client) / 2380 (peer). Lose it without a
        backup and the cluster is gone — backup is the #1 admin task.
     c) 📋 kube-scheduler — watches for unbound Pods and picks a node:
        FILTER (which nodes CAN run it?) → SCORE (which is best?) → BIND.
     d) 🔄 kube-controller-manager — runs the reconciliation loops in one
        process: Node, ReplicaSet, Endpoint/EndpointSlice, ServiceAccount and
        Job controllers, etc. Each watches one thing and corrects drift.
     e) ☁️ cloud-controller-manager (optional) — integrates a cloud provider
        (LoadBalancers, routes, node lifecycle); only in cloud clusters.

  ── 3.3  Worker-Node Components — deep dive ─────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     • 🤖 kubelet — the node agent. NOT a pod — a SYSTEMD service. Registers the
       node, watches for Pods assigned to it, drives the runtime to start/stop
       containers, runs probes, reports status. Config:
       /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml.
     • 🌐 kube-proxy — programs Service → Pod routing (iptables / IPVS / nftables).
     • 🐋 container runtime — containerd / CRI-O (via the CRI) actually runs the
       containers.

  ── 3.4  How a Pod Gets Created — the full journey ──────────────────  🟡 🎯
     The single most important sequence to know cold (assumed in every
     troubleshooting task): kubectl → apiserver (authN/Z/admission) → etcd →
     controller-manager (Deployment→ReplicaSet→Pod) → scheduler (bind to a node)
     → kubelet (pull image, start container via the runtime) → kube-proxy
     (program Service routing) → Running. (See the 8-step "how a pod is born"
     diagram, grounded on easypj-deploy.yaml.)

  ── 3.5  Cluster Networking Overview ────────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     Three rules: every Pod gets a unique IP; all Pods reach all Pods WITHOUT
     NAT (a flat network); a CNI plugin implements it. Three layers to keep
     straight — POD network (pod↔pod) · SERVICE network (stable VIPs via
     kube-proxy) · EXTERNAL (NodePort/LoadBalancer/Ingress/Gateway). Detail in
     Part 5.

  ── 3.6  Static Pods — an admin-only concept ───────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     📖 Static pods are managed DIRECTLY by the kubelet from YAML in
        /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ — not by the scheduler/apiserver. The whole
        control plane runs AS static pods. The kubelet creates a read-only
        "mirror pod" in the API so you can see them; their names carry a
        "-<nodename>" suffix. Drop a file in that dir → pod appears; remove it →
        pod gone.

  ── 3.7  Node Health & Control-Plane ↔ Node Communication ───────────  🟡 🎯
     How the control plane KNOWS a node is alive, how much it can schedule onto
     it, and how the two halves talk — the backbone of "node NotReady" debugging.
     • NODE CONDITIONS (kubectl describe node): Ready (True/False/Unknown) plus
       the pressure flags MemoryPressure · DiskPressure · PIDPressure ·
       NetworkUnavailable.
     • HEARTBEATS: the kubelet renews a lightweight Lease in the kube-node-lease
       namespace (~every 10s) AND posts a fuller .status on change. If heartbeats
       stop: ~50s (node-monitor-grace-period) → Ready=Unknown + taint
       node.kubernetes.io/unreachable (or …/not-ready):NoExecute; ~5min → pods
       without a matching toleration are evicted (rate-limited).
     • CAPACITY vs ALLOCATABLE: Allocatable = Capacity − kube-reserved −
       system-reserved − eviction-threshold; the scheduler only fills
       Allocatable, so a pod can sit Pending though the node "looks" half-empty.
     • NODE REGISTRATION: the kubelet self-registers by default (--register-node);
       `kubectl cordon` marks a node unschedulable without evicting its pods.
     • CP↔NODE COMMS (trust asymmetry): node→apiserver :6443 is TLS+RBAC by
       default (secure); apiserver→kubelet :10250 is HTTPS but doesn't verify the
       kubelet cert unless --kubelet-certificate-authority is set; apiserver→
       nodes/pods/services is plain HTTP by default → use the Konnectivity service
       for a secured tunnel. Mental model: everything DIALS the apiserver.
     📖 KEY TERM — Allocatable: the slice of a node's resources the scheduler may
        actually place pods into (Capacity minus reservations).
     🧠 ANALOGY: the node Lease is a heartbeat monitor — miss a few beeps and the
        controller calls a code (Unknown), then moves the patients (eviction).

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 3
  [THE COMPLETE CLUSTER — brain (control plane) + muscle (workers)]
  ┌───────────────────────────── ☸️ CLUSTER ────────────────────────────┐
  │  🧠 CONTROL PLANE                                                     │
  │   ┌────────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────────┐ ┌────────────────────┐   │
  │   │📡 apiserver│ │🗄️ etcd │ │📋 scheduler│ │🔄 controller-mgr   │   │
  │   │ front door │ │ ALL    │ │ place pods │ │ reconcile loops    │   │
  │   │ :6443      │ │ state  │ │            │ │ (node/RS/endpoint) │   │
  │   └─────┬──────┘ └────────┘ └────────────┘ └────────────────────┘   │
  │         │  every request & watch flows through the apiserver         │
  │  ───────┼──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
  │  💪 WORKER NODES                                                      │
  │   ┌─────▼────────────────┐        ┌──────────────────────┐          │
  │   │ Node 1               │        │ Node 2               │          │
  │   │ 🤖 kubelet  🌐 proxy │        │ 🤖 kubelet  🌐 proxy │          │
  │   │ 🐋 containerd        │        │ 🐋 containerd        │          │
  │   │ ┌────┐ ┌────┐        │        │ ┌────┐ ┌────┐        │          │
  │   │ │📦  │ │📦  │ easypj  │        │ │📦  │ │🐘  │        │          │
  │   │ │web │ │web │ pods    │        │ │web │ │ db │        │          │
  │   │ └────┘ └────┘        │        │ └────┘ └────┘        │          │
  │   └──────────────────────┘        └──────────────────────┘          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [HOW A POD IS BORN — kubectl apply -f easypj-deploy.yaml]
   1  kubectl ─►📡 apiserver         (HTTPS + client cert)
   2  apiserver ─► authN ─► authZ(RBAC) ─► admission   (who? allowed? valid?)
   3  apiserver ─►🗄️ etcd            (Deployment object persisted)
   4  🔄 controller-mgr sees Deployment ─► creates ReplicaSet ─► creates Pod(s)
   5  📋 scheduler sees an unbound Pod ─► filter ─► score ─► binds to Node 2
   6  🤖 kubelet(Node 2) sees its Pod ─►🐋 containerd pulls easypj:v2 ─► starts
   7  🌐 kube-proxy programs Service routing to the new Pod IP
   8  ✅ Pod Running ─► kubelet reports status ─► apiserver ─► etcd
   💡 Each actor only reconciles its OWN slice — no central script.

  [KUBERNETES IS PLUGGABLE — the 3 extension interfaces]
        🤖 kubelet ──CRI──► 🐋 containerd / CRI-O        (RUN containers)
        pod network ──CNI──► 🌐 Calico / Cilium / Flannel (WIRE pods)
        volumes ──CSI──► 🧱 CSI driver (EBS/PD/Ceph/NFS)   (ATTACH storage)
   📖 CRI=runtime · CNI=network · CSI=storage — the three "sockets" of K8s.

  [G] 🔧 UNDER THE HOOD: the watch/list mechanism; how informers & the
      reconcile queue keep actual==desired; leader election via Lease.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): API machinery (REST + watch + resourceVersion);
      the Binding subresource; API Priority & Fairness (APF); the aggregation
      layer; controller-runtime informers & workqueues.
      • Full pod-creation trace (internals beneath §3.4): admission chain
        (Mutating → schema validation → Validating: LimitRanger / PodSecurity /
        webhooks) → scheduler plugins (NodeResourcesFit[LeastAllocated],
        NodeResourcesBalancedAllocation, ImageLocality, NodeAffinity) → kubelet
        CRI calls RunPodSandbox → PullImage → CreateContainer → StartContainer →
        containerd → runc.
      • Static-pod BOOTSTRAP paradox: the kubelet runs the apiserver itself from a
        manifest BEFORE any apiserver exists, then registers a read-only mirror
        pod; restart one by moving its manifest out of /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.
      • Lease fields: holderIdentity · leaseDurationSeconds (~15s for sched/c-m) ·
        renewTime. APF rejects with 429 (queued/over-limit, retry) vs 503 (no
        queue / overloaded); admission failurePolicy Fail (default) vs Ignore;
        mutating runs before validating so validators judge the FINAL object.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: control plane decides, nodes run; only the apiserver talks
      to etcd; the kubelet is a systemd service (not a pod); the control plane
      itself is static pods; CRI/CNI/CSI are the 3 plug points.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: kubelet is systemd, not a pod; editing a static-pod manifest
      auto-restarts that pod; the apiserver is the single choke point — if it's
      down, kubectl is useless (use crictl/journalctl).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: apiserver down? debug from the node with `crictl ps -a`,
      `crictl logs`, and `journalctl -u kubelet`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: get --raw='/readyz?verbose' (componentstatuses is deprecated) ·
      get pods -n kube-system · get lease -n kube-node-lease · crictl ps -a ·
      journalctl -u kubelet -f · ls /etc/kubernetes/manifests.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: list the control-plane static pods; tail apiserver logs.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation + diagram each:
      Q1  Which component is the ONLY one that reads/writes etcd? (apiserver)
      Q2  A node shows NotReady — which agent do you check first, and how?
      Q3  Trace what happens after `kubectl apply -f easypj-deploy.yaml`.
      Q4  The scheduler is down — what symptom appears, on which objects?
      Q5  Create a static pod on a worker, then delete it. Which files/commands?
      Q6  Name the 3 network layers and what routes each.
      Q7  Which ports does etcd listen on, and for what? (2379 client/2380 peer)
      Q8  Why is the apiserver the cluster's single choke point?
      Q9  Explain a controller's reconcile loop with the ReplicaSet example.
      Q10 What is a "mirror pod" and why does its name end in -<nodename>?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the full annotated cluster, one picture.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 4: Core Kubernetes Objects — The Admin's Toolkit            🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 Intermediate · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Workloads + foundation.
  [B] WHY: these are the nouns of every exam task and every easypj manifest.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain & relate Pod, ReplicaSet, Deployment, Service,
      Namespace, Labels/Selectors/Annotations; trace the pod lifecycle (phases,
      states, probes); explain rolling-update mechanics & resource scope; read
      the relationship map.
  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: 🎯 underpins Workloads (15%) & Services (20%).

  ── 4.1  Pods — the smallest unit ───────────────────────────────────  🟡
     A Pod is one or more containers that share a network namespace (one IP,
     reachable over localhost) and storage volumes. Pods are EPHEMERAL — when one
     dies it is replaced, never repaired, so never rely on a pod's identity or
     IP. Multi-container pods follow patterns like the sidecar (e.g. easypj + an
     nginx static-file sidecar). (See the pod-anatomy diagram below.)

  ── 4.2  ReplicaSets — maintaining the count ────────────────────────  🟡
     A ReplicaSet keeps a desired number of identical Pods running, using a
     label SELECTOR to know which pods are "its". If a pod dies it creates a
     replacement (self-healing). You rarely create one directly — a Deployment
     manages it for you.

  ── 4.3  Deployments — application lifecycle ────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     The standard way to run a stateless app. A Deployment manages ReplicaSets,
     which manage Pods, and adds rolling updates, rollbacks and revision
     history: Deployment → ReplicaSet(v_current) → Pods, with old ReplicaSets
     kept at 0 replicas for instant rollback.

  ── 4.4  Services — stable network endpoints ───────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     Pods get random IPs that change on restart; a Service gives a STABLE virtual
     IP + DNS name and load-balances across the Pods its selector matches (its
     Endpoints). Four types: ClusterIP (internal, default), NodePort (node
     IP:port), LoadBalancer (cloud LB), ExternalName (DNS alias).

  ── 4.5  Namespaces — virtual clusters ──────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     Logical partitions of one cluster, used to isolate teams/environments. The
     built-ins: default, kube-system (control-plane add-ons), kube-public,
     kube-node-lease. Each namespace can carry its own ResourceQuota, RBAC and
     NetworkPolicy (e.g. easypj-dev vs easypj-prod). ⚠️ Namespaces do NOT
     isolate nodes or cluster-scoped objects (PV, Node, StorageClass).

  ── 4.6  Labels, Selectors & Annotations ────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     📖 Labels — key/value tags used to SELECT objects (matchLabels). They wire
        Services→Pods, Deployments→Pods, NetworkPolicies and RBAC. 📖 Annotations
        — non-identifying metadata for tools/humans; NEVER used for selection.
     easypj convention: app=easypj · tier=web|db · version=v2.

  ── 4.7  The Object Relationship Map ────────────────────────────────  🟡
     How it all connects: Deployment→ReplicaSet→Pods; Service finds Pods by
     label; ConfigMap/Secret feed env via envFrom; Ingress/Gateway routes to a
     Service; PVC→PV→StorageClass for storage; a Namespace contains it all and
     RBAC governs access. (See the relationship map below — THE picture to
     memorise.)

  ── 4.8  Pod Lifecycle & Health Probes ──────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     A pod isn't just up/down — it has phases, per-container states, and probes
     that gate traffic.
     • PHASES (.status.phase): Pending → Running → Succeeded / Failed / Unknown.
     • CONTAINER STATES (.status.containerStatuses[].state): Waiting (reason:
       ContainerCreating / ImagePullBackOff / CrashLoopBackOff) · Running
       (startedAt) · Terminated (exitCode: 137 = OOM/SIGKILL, 0 = clean).
     • restartPolicy: Always (default; Deployments) · OnFailure (Jobs) · Never.
       Restarts back off exponentially (10s,20s,40s… capped 5min) → CrashLoopBackOff.
     • POD CONDITIONS flip in order: PodScheduled → Initialized → ContainersReady
       → Ready. Only a Ready pod joins its Service endpoints (the answer to
       "Running but no traffic").
     • TERMINATION: delete → deletionTimestamp (Terminating) → removed from
       endpoints → SIGTERM → grace (terminationGracePeriodSeconds, default 30s) →
       SIGKILL.
     • PROBES (concept here; config in Part 4): startup (gate slow boots),
       liveness (fail → RESTART the container), readiness (fail → REMOVE from
       endpoints); each via httpGet / tcpSocket / exec / grpc.
     📖 KEY TERM — readiness probe: decides if a pod can serve traffic right now;
        failing it removes the pod from Service endpoints without restarting it.
     🧠 ANALOGY: liveness = "are you alive?" (if not, restart); readiness = "are
        you open for business?" (if not, stop sending customers).

  ── 4.9  Rollouts, Service Ports, Label Rules & Resource Scope ──────  🟡 🎯
     The exam-critical details that make §4.3–4.6 actually behave.
     • DEPLOYMENT ROLLOUT: strategy RollingUpdate (default) vs Recreate (downtime).
       maxSurge 25% (extra above desired) · maxUnavailable 25% (down during roll) ·
       revisionHistoryLimit 10 (old ReplicaSets kept at 0 for rollback). The
       .spec.selector is IMMUTABLE. Commands: kubectl rollout status/history/undo
       [--to-revision=N]/pause/resume ; set image deploy/easypj-web easypj=easypj:v2.
     • SERVICE PORTS: port (the Service's own) → targetPort (the container's, 8000
       for easypj); NodePort adds <nodeIP>:nodePort, range 30000–32767. Wrong
       targetPort = "connection refused"; port defaults to targetPort if omitted.
     • CROSS-NAMESPACE DNS: same ns → short name (easypj-db); across namespaces →
       FQDN service.namespace.svc.cluster.local.
     • LABEL GRAMMAR: key = [prefix/]name, name ≤63 chars of [a-z A-Z 0-9 - _ .],
       start/end alphanumeric; value same ≤63 (or empty). Selectors: equality
       (app=easypj,tier=web = AND) and set-based (in, notin, exists / !key).
     • RESOURCE SCOPE: namespaced (Pod, Deployment, Service, ConfigMap, Secret,
       PVC, Role, RoleBinding, Ingress, NetworkPolicy) vs cluster-scoped (Node,
       PersistentVolume, StorageClass, Namespace, ClusterRole, CRD). List with
       `kubectl api-resources --namespaced=true|false`. Deleting a namespace
       deletes everything namespaced in it; cluster-scoped objects survive.
     📖 KEY TERM — maxSurge / maxUnavailable: the two knobs that pace a rolling
        update (both default 25%).
     🧠 ANALOGY: a rolling update is changing tires while driving — maxSurge bolts
        on a spare first, maxUnavailable is how many you'll briefly take off.

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 4
  [POD ANATOMY — containers share one network + storage]
  ┌──────────── 📦 POD  (IP 10.244.1.5) ────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────────┐      ┌──────────────┐          │
  │  │📦 easypj      │      │📦 nginx       │ sidecar  │
  │  │ Django :8000 │      │ static :80   │          │
  │  └──────┬───────┘      └──────┬───────┘          │
  │         │  localhost   share  │                   │
  │         └────────┬────────────┘                   │
  │              ┌───▼────┐  shared 💾 volume          │
  │              │ volume │  (e.g. emptyDir)           │
  │              └────────┘                            │
  │  localhost:8000 = Django   ·   localhost:80 = nginx │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [OBJECT RELATIONSHIP MAP — how easypj wires together]
   🌍 Ingress / 🚪 Gateway
        │ routes host/path
        ▼
   🔌 Service ──selector(app=easypj)──► 📦 Pods
        ▲                                  ▲ owned by
   🔄 Deployment ──manages──► ReplicaSet ──┘ creates
        │ pod template references
        ├─ ⚙️ ConfigMap (envFrom)   ┌─ 💾 PVC ──► PV ──► 🧱 StorageClass
        ├─ 🔒 Secret   (envFrom)    │
        └─ 🛡️ ServiceAccount ◄─ RBAC (Role+Binding) controls access
   🗂️ Namespace contains ALL of the above   ·   🧯 NetworkPolicy firewalls it
   💡 Labels are the glue: a Service finds Pods only by matching labels.

  [G] 🔧 UNDER THE HOOD: ownerReferences & cascading deletion; how the
      endpoints/EndpointSlice controller wires a Service to live pods.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): GVK/GVR & the RESTMapper; the spec/status split &
      subresources; server-side apply & managedFields; GC modes (foreground/
      background/orphan); generateName.
      • Cascading deletion via `kubectl delete --cascade=background(default)|
        foreground|orphan`. ownerReferences CANNOT cross namespaces (only a
        cluster-scoped owner may own across them). EndpointSlices shard endpoints
        ≤100/slice (the legacy Endpoints API is deprecated); only Ready pods land
        in a slice. finalizers + deletionTimestamp pause deletion until cleared —
        the classic "stuck Terminating" namespace.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: Pod < ReplicaSet < Deployment; Services give stable
      networking; Namespaces isolate; LABELS are the glue that wires everything.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: a Service/Deployment selector MUST match the pod-template
      labels (else empty endpoints / orphaned pods); deleting a Pod under a
      Deployment just respawns it; annotations ≠ labels (not selectable).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: generate every object with `--dry-run=client -o yaml` then
      edit; `kubectl label`/`annotate` to change live objects; `-l` to filter.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: get all -n easypj · get po -l app=easypj · label/annotate ·
      get ep · explain <kind> · rollout status/undo deploy/easypj-web ·
      api-resources --namespaced=true|false.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: label nodes/pods, select with -l, watch a self-heal.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 5: Setting Up Your Environment                               🟢 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟢 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Foundation for everything hands-on.
  [B] WHY: you need a sandbox to practice; the exam is a live terminal, so
      muscle memory starts here. VISUAL: tool-choice decision tree 🧭.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: pick a local tool; install Minikube + kubectl; read a
      kubeconfig; switch contexts/namespaces; verify cluster health; enable
      addons.  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: 🎯 kubeconfig/context switching is used in
      EVERY exam question.
  ── 5.1  Choosing a Local Kubernetes Tool 🧭 ────────────────────────  🟢
     ┌─────────────┬───────────────┬─────────┬──────────────────────────┐
     │ Tool        │ Best for      │ Nodes   │ Notes                    │
     │ Minikube    │ learning      │ 1–multi │ easiest single-node start│
     │ Kind        │ CI / testing  │ multi   │ K8s in Docker, fast      │
     │ k3s         │ edge / light  │ multi   │ tiny footprint           │
     │ kubeadm     │ REAL clusters │ multi   │ you MUST learn this (CKA)│
     │ Docker Desk │ Win/Mac dev   │ 1       │ one-click                │
     └─────────────┴───────────────┴─────────┴──────────────────────────┘
     💡 CKA path: Minikube to learn fast → kubeadm because the exam tests it.

  ── 5.2  Installing Minikube ────────────────────────────────────────  🟢
     Per-OS (choco/winget · brew · curl binary). Start & verify:
       minikube start --driver=docker --cpus=2 --memory=4096 --nodes=3
       minikube status   ·   minikube stop / delete
     💡 --nodes=3 gives a multi-node sandbox for scheduling/drain practice.

  ── 5.3  Installing kubectl ─────────────────────────────────────────  🟢
     The CLI for ANY cluster. Install per-OS; verify `kubectl version --client`.
     It connects using ~/.kube/config (next).

  ── 5.4  Understanding kubeconfig ───────────────────────────────────  🟢 🎯
     📖 ~/.kube/config (or $KUBECONFIG) has three sections: CLUSTERS (where: API
        URL + CA), USERS (who: cert/token), CONTEXTS (a cluster+user+namespace
        combo). current-context is the active one. (See the kubeconfig diagram.)

  ── 5.5  Managing Clusters & Contexts ───────────────────────────────  🟢 🎯
       kubectl config get-contexts                              # list
       kubectl config use-context prod                          # switch
       kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=easypj  # default ns
     Merge several files via KUBECONFIG=a:b:c. ⚠️ ALWAYS verify context AND
     namespace before acting — the #1 way to lose exam points.

  ── 5.6  Verifying Your Cluster ─────────────────────────────────────  🟢
     🩺 `cluster-info` (API reachable?), `get nodes` (all Ready?), `get pods -n
     kube-system` (CoreDNS/proxy Running?), and a DNS smoke test from a temp pod.
     NotReady/CrashLoop here = fix before deploying anything.

  ── 5.7  Enabling Essential Addons ──────────────────────────────────  🟢
     `minikube addons enable …` for metrics-server (kubectl top/HPA), ingress
     (nginx), storage-provisioner (dynamic PVCs), dashboard.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 5
  [KUBECONFIG — one file, many clusters; a CONTEXT picks the combo]
   ~/.kube/config
   ┌─ clusters: WHERE  minikube · easypj-dev · easypj-prod   (server + CA)
   ├─ users:    WHO    admin (cert) · dev (token)
   └─ contexts: combo  dev  = easypj-dev  + dev   + ns:easypj
                       prod = easypj-prod + admin + ns:easypj
   current-context: dev   ◄ kubectl acts HERE
   🔀 switch: kubectl config use-context prod
   📌 set default ns: kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=easypj
   ⚠️ ALWAYS verify context + namespace before acting — wrong cluster = 0 points.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how kubectl finds & merges kubeconfig (flag > $KUBECONFIG > ~/.kube).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): exec credential plugins (cloud auth); client-go
      loading order; insecure-skip-tls-verify risks; impersonation flags
      (--as / --as-group); using KUBECONFIG in CI; kubectx/kubens.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: kubeconfig = clusters+users+contexts; a context binds all
      three; confirm context + namespace first; addons unlock top/ingress.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: acting on the wrong context/cluster = 0 points; a stale default
      namespace silently sends objects to the wrong place.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: `alias k=kubectl`, a `kns` namespace helper, and
      `kubectl config use-context` as the FIRST move of every exam question.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: config get-contexts/use-context/set-context · cluster-info ·
      get nodes · minikube start/status/addons.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create 2 contexts, switch between them, set default ns to easypj.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Name the 3 sections of a kubeconfig and what each holds.
      Q2  Switch to context "prod" (command).
      Q3  Set the current context's default namespace to easypj.
      Q4  Merge two kubeconfig files for one kubectl session.
      Q5  Verify a freshly-built cluster is healthy (3 commands).
      Q6  What exactly is a "context"?
      Q7  Enable metrics-server and prove `kubectl top` works.
      Q8  Start a 3-node local cluster (command).
      Q9  Print the current context in one command.
      Q10 KUBECONFIG precedence: flag vs env vs ~/.kube/config — which wins?
