  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 6: kubectl — The Administrator's Command Center              🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Used in 100% of exam tasks. SPEED CHAPTER.
  [B] WHY: kubectl fluency is the single biggest score multiplier. VISUAL:
      command anatomy callout.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: master the verb/resource/name/flags pattern; the daily
      command set; `kubectl explain`; imperative vs declarative; output
      formatting (jsonpath/custom-columns/sort/label/field selectors); debugging
      (logs/exec/ephemeral containers via `kubectl debug`).
  ── 6.1  The kubectl Syntax Pattern ─────────────────────────────────  🟡
     Every command is verb + resource + name + flags (see the anatomy callout).
     Learn the short names (po svc deploy ns cm rs sts ds pvc sa netpol ing) —
     they save real seconds under the clock.

  ── 6.2  Essential Admin Commands ───────────────────────────────────  🟡
     Grouped for muscle memory:
       GET    : get all -n easypj · get po -o wide · get events --sort-by=…
       INSPECT: describe <kind> <name> · explain <path>
       CREATE : create / apply / run / expose / scale  (imperative = fastest)
       MODIFY : edit · set image · label · annotate · patch
       DELETE : delete <kind> <name> [$now]
       DEBUG  : logs [-p][-c] · exec -it · port-forward · debug · cp · top

  ── 6.3  kubectl explain ────────────────────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     💡 Your in-exam manual. `kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.resources`
        gives the exact YAML structure; add `--recursive` for the whole tree.
        Forgot a field name? explain it instead of guessing.

  ── 6.4  Imperative vs Declarative ──────────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     Imperative (`create/run/expose`) = fastest for one-off objects.
     Declarative (`apply -f`) = repeatable, version-controlled, GitOps-ready.
     💡 THE HYBRID (exam gold): generate imperatively then apply —
        `k run x --image=nginx $do > pod.yaml` → edit → `k apply -f pod.yaml`.

  ── 6.5  Output Formatting & Filtering ──────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     -o wide | yaml | json · -o jsonpath='{.items[*].status.podIP}' ·
     -o custom-columns=NAME:.metadata.name,IP:.status.podIP ·
     --sort-by=.metadata.creationTimestamp · -l app=easypj ·
     --field-selector status.phase=Running.

  ── 6.6  Debugging Pods — logs, exec & ephemeral containers ─────────  🟡 🎯
     • logs: `kubectl logs <pod> [-c <ctr>] [-p|--previous] [-f] [--since=1h]`
       (--previous = the LAST crashed container's logs — vital for CrashLoopBackOff).
     • exec: `kubectl exec -it <pod> -- sh` (needs a shell in the image).
     • EPHEMERAL CONTAINERS via `kubectl debug` — the only way to "get a shell"
       into a CRASHED pod or a DISTROLESS image (no shell). They share the pod
       (optionally its PID namespace) and CANNOT be removed/restarted; they take
       no ports/probes/resources:
         kubectl debug -it <pod> --image=busybox --target=<ctr>      # attach to a running pod
         kubectl debug <pod> -it --image=busybox --copy-to=dbg --share-processes
         kubectl debug node/<node> -it --image=busybox               # node (host fs at /host)
     • `kubectl wait --for=condition=Ready pod/<x> --timeout=60s` blocks in scripts.
     📖 KEY TERM — ephemeral container: a temporary debug container injected into a
        running pod; not part of pod.spec, never restarted, can't be removed.

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 6
  [kubectl COMMAND ANATOMY]
   kubectl   get    pods   easypj-web-x9k   -n easypj   -o wide
   └binary─┘ └verb┘ └kind┘ └────name─────┘ └namespace┘ └output┘
   verbs: get·describe·logs·exec·apply·create·delete·edit·scale·rollout·top
   short names: po svc deploy ns cm rs sts ds pvc sa netpol ing

  [THE FAST PATH — generate, don't hand-write]
   k run nginx --image=nginx $do > pod.yaml   ($do = --dry-run=client -o yaml)
        ▼ edit (add volumes / probes / resources)
   k apply -f pod.yaml ─► validated by the apiserver ─► created
   🔍 forgot a field? kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.livenessProbe

  [G] 🔧 UTH: kubectl is a REST client — `kubectl get po -v=8` shows the raw
      API calls; how server-side vs client-side dry-run differ.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): -v=6..9 to see raw API calls; server-side apply;
      go-template output; kubectl wait & --raw; krew plugins; explain
      --recursive; custom-columns & label/field selectors.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: verb+resource+name+flags; generate YAML with --dry-run, never
      hand-write; `explain` is your manual; output flags extract anything fast.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: forgetting `-n`; confusing client vs server dry-run; jsonpath
      quoting (single quotes); editing live objects instead of the manifest.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED CARD: alias k=kubectl · kgp='k get po' · export do='--dry-run=
      client -o yaml' · export now='--force --grace-period 0' · bash completion.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: get/describe/logs/exec/apply/create/run/expose/scale/rollout ·
      explain · -o jsonpath/custom-columns · debug · wait · apply --server-side.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: generate a Deployment YAML with $do, edit it, apply it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Generate a Pod manifest without creating it (command).
      Q2  Show the YAML path for a container's liveness probe via explain.
      Q3  Extract every pod's IP with jsonpath.
      Q4  List pods sorted by start time.
      Q5  Show only pods labelled app=easypj.
      Q6  Show only Running pods (field selector).
      Q7  Get logs from a container that already crashed. (--previous)
      Q8  Open a shell inside a running easypj pod.
      Q9  Port-forward the easypj Service to localhost:8000.
      Q10 Print NAME + node + IP as custom columns.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: "one command, four parts, infinite power."

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 7: YAML & Kubernetes Manifests                               🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · The language of every manifest & exam answer.
  [B] WHY: one wrong indent = a broken task. VISUAL: YAML building-blocks card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: write valid YAML; the 4 required fields; find the right
      apiVersion; multi-doc files; validate with dry-run/diff; object management
      (imperative vs declarative) & server-side apply.
  ── 7.1  YAML Basics ────────────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
     📖 YAML = key/value pairs, lists (`- item`), and nested maps shown by
        INDENTATION (2 spaces per level). Rules that bite: spaces NOT tabs; a
        space after every colon (`key: value`); strings usually unquoted; `---`
        separates documents in one file. (See the YAML cheat-sheet card.)

  ── 7.2  The 4 Required Fields ──────────────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     EVERY manifest has apiVersion · kind · metadata · spec. (See the annotated
     easypj Deployment skeleton below — note selector.matchLabels MUST equal
     template.metadata.labels.)

  ── 7.3  Finding the Right apiVersion ───────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     `kubectl api-resources` (kind → group + short name) and `kubectl
     api-versions` (all versions). Common ones:
       Pod · Service · ConfigMap · Secret · PV ..... v1
       Deployment · ReplicaSet · StatefulSet · DaemonSet  apps/v1
       Ingress · NetworkPolicy ..................... networking.k8s.io/v1
       Role · RoleBinding ......................... rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
       Job · CronJob .............................. batch/v1

  ── 7.4  Multi-Resource YAML ────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
     Separate multiple objects with `---` in one file; apply a whole directory
     with `kubectl apply -f k8s/`; overlay-customise with Kustomize
     (`kubectl apply -k`). (Kustomize in depth: Ch 37.)

  ── 7.5  Validation & Dry Run ───────────────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     `--dry-run=client` (local shape check) vs `--dry-run=server` (full apiserver
     validation incl. admission) · `kubectl diff -f x.yaml` previews the change.
     Validate BEFORE applying in anger.

  ── 7.6  Object Management & Server-Side Apply ──────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     THREE ways to manage objects (don't mix them on one object):
       imperative cmd → kubectl create/run/expose/scale/label  (fast, keeps no file)
       imperative cfg → kubectl create -f / replace -f          (whole-object)
       declarative    → kubectl apply -f / -k                   (the default)
     HOW `apply` KNOWS WHAT CHANGED — a client-side 3-WAY MERGE of: (1) the
       last-applied-configuration annotation
       (kubectl.kubernetes.io/last-applied-configuration), (2) the live object,
       (3) your new file → it SETS fields you set, CLEARS fields you removed, and
       LEAVES fields others wrote (e.g. a replica count set by `kubectl scale`).
     SERVER-SIDE APPLY (`--server-side`): the apiserver does the merge and records
       FIELD OWNERSHIP in metadata.managedFields; two managers writing the same
       field → conflict (resolve with --force-conflicts). Preferred for
       controllers/GitOps and any object with several writers.
     ⚠️ Don't `kubectl edit`/`scale` an object you also manage with `apply` — the
        next apply may revert your imperative change.
     📖 KEY TERM — managedFields: the server-side record of WHICH manager owns each
        field, so multiple actors co-own one object without clobbering.

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 7
  [EVERY MANIFEST = 4 REQUIRED FIELDS]
   apiVersion: apps/v1     ◄ which API group/version? (api-resources to find)
   kind: Deployment        ◄ what TYPE of object?
   metadata:               ◄ identity: name, namespace, labels
     name: easypj-web
   spec:                   ◄ DESIRED STATE (the meat)
     selector.matchLabels:   {app: easypj}  ── MUST match ──┐
     template.metadata.labels:{app: easypj} ◄───────────────┘
     template.spec.containers:[{name, image: easypj:v1, ports}]
   ⚠️ spaces not tabs · a space after every colon · selector == template labels.
   ✅ validate: kubectl apply -f x.yaml --dry-run=server  ·  kubectl diff -f x.yaml

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the apiserver validates a manifest (schema -> defaulting ->
      admission); why server dry-run catches more than client.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): multi-doc streams; anchors/aliases; strategic-
      merge vs JSON6902 patches; null/~/empty pitfalls; pruning & defaulting;
      kubeconform validation; managedFields.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: 4 required fields; spaces not tabs; selector == template
      labels; generate then validate with dry-run; find apiVersion via
      api-resources.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: tabs; missing space after a colon; selector ≠ template labels;
      deprecated apiVersion (extensions/v1beta1 is gone); unquoted "yes"/numbers.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: never hand-write — generate with $do then edit; `kubectl
      diff` before apply; `explain --recursive` for unfamiliar fields.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: api-resources · api-versions · apply --dry-run=server · diff ·
      explain.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: break a YAML's indent, let `--dry-run=server` catch it, fix it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Fix a manifest that uses tabs / a bad indent.
      Q2  Add the missing required field to a manifest.
      Q3  Find the apiVersion for an Ingress (command).
      Q4  Convert `kubectl create deploy …` into a YAML manifest.
      Q5  Put a Deployment + Service in one file and apply it.
      Q6  Validate a manifest against the apiserver WITHOUT creating it.
      Q7  Preview what `apply` would change. (diff)
      Q8  Why does a label typo leave a Service with no endpoints?
      Q9  Fix an "expected map, got list" error.
      Q10 Force the string "true" / a numeric port to be quoted — why care?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the manifest skeleton, color-coded by the 4 fields.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 8: Deploying easypj — Your First Kubernetes Application      🟡 🧪
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · The payoff: a real app on a real cluster.
  [B] WHY: this is where Parts 1-2 fuse into a running system you can see.
      VISUAL: easypj target architecture (Section 5.4, scoped to dev).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: deploy easypj step-by-step; inject config via ConfigMap &
      Secret; expose with a Service; access via port-forward; verify & explain
      what K8s did.
  ── 8.1  easypj Application Overview ─────────────────────────────────  🟡
     We deploy the Django web tier now (PostgreSQL comes in Part 6). Target:
     Namespace → ConfigMap → Secret → Deployment → Service → reachable on /jobs/.
     (See the easypj-dev target diagram.)

  ── 8.2  Step 1 — Namespace ─────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
       kubectl create namespace easypj-dev
     (or a 3-line Namespace manifest). Everything else sets namespace: easypj-dev.

  ── 8.3  Step 2 — ConfigMap (non-secret config) ─────────────────────  🟡
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: ConfigMap
       metadata: { name: easypj-config, namespace: easypj-dev }
       data:
         DEBUG: "False"
         ALLOWED_HOSTS: "*"
         DB_HOST: "easypj-db"
         DJANGO_SETTINGS_MODULE: "easypj.settings"

  ── 8.4  Step 3 — Secret (sensitive config) ─────────────────────────  🟡
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: Secret
       metadata: { name: easypj-secret, namespace: easypj-dev }
       type: Opaque
       stringData:                  # plaintext in → base64 at rest
         SECRET_KEY: "change-me-in-prod"
         DB_PASSWORD: "supersecret"
     ⚠️ Never commit real Secrets to git; base64 is ENCODING, not encryption.

  ── 8.5  Step 4 — Deployment ────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
       apiVersion: apps/v1
       kind: Deployment
       metadata: { name: easypj-web, namespace: easypj-dev }
       spec:
         replicas: 1
         selector: { matchLabels: { app: easypj, tier: web } }
         template:
           metadata: { labels: { app: easypj, tier: web } }
           spec:
             containers:
             - name: web
               image: easypj:v1
               ports: [{ containerPort: 8000 }]
               envFrom:
               - configMapRef: { name: easypj-config }
               - secretRef:    { name: easypj-secret }
               resources:
                 requests: { cpu: 100m, memory: 128Mi }
                 limits:   { cpu: 500m, memory: 256Mi }
               readinessProbe: { httpGet: { path: /jobs/, port: 8000 } }
               livenessProbe:  { httpGet: { path: /jobs/, port: 8000 },
                                 initialDelaySeconds: 10 }

  ── 8.6  Step 5 — Service ───────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: Service
       metadata: { name: easypj-web, namespace: easypj-dev }
       spec:
         selector: { app: easypj, tier: web }    # MUST match pod labels
         ports: [{ port: 80, targetPort: 8000 }]
     Verify: `kubectl get svc,ep -n easypj-dev` (endpoints should list pod IPs).

  ── 8.7  Step 6 — Access It ─────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
       kubectl -n easypj-dev port-forward svc/easypj-web 8000:80
     → open http://localhost:8000/jobs/  ✅

  ── 8.8  Step 7 — Verify Everything ─────────────────────────────────  🟡
     `get all -n easypj-dev` · `describe deploy/easypj-web` · `logs deploy/
     easypj-web`. Checklist: pod Running & READY 1/1, Service has endpoints,
     /jobs/ returns 200.

  ── 8.9  What Just Happened? ────────────────────────────────────────  🟡
     apply → apiserver → etcd → controller-mgr (RS→Pod) → scheduler (bind) →
     kubelet (pull+run) → kube-proxy (route) → port-forward → 🎉. (See the
     apply-order diagram.)

  ── 8.10  ConfigMaps & Secrets — the full picture ──────────────────  🟡 🎯
     CONSUMPTION (both): env (one key via configMapKeyRef/secretKeyRef) · envFrom
       (all keys → env vars) · VOLUME mount (each key → a file) · command/args.
     LIVE UPDATES: env vars are FIXED at start (need a pod restart); a VOLUME-
       mounted ConfigMap/Secret updates automatically (≈ kubelet sync period) —
       BUT a `subPath` mount does NOT get updates.
     LIMITS: each ConfigMap/Secret ≤ 1 MiB. Both support `immutable: true` (v1.19+)
       — blocks edits and cuts apiserver/etcd watch load.
     SECRETS specifics: base64 is ENCODING, not encryption — Secrets sit
       UNENCRYPTED in etcd by default → protect with encryption-at-rest + RBAC.
       `data` (base64) vs `stringData` (plaintext in, encoded for you).
       Built-in TYPES: Opaque · kubernetes.io/service-account-token ·
       kubernetes.io/dockerconfigjson · kubernetes.io/basic-auth ·
       kubernetes.io/ssh-auth · kubernetes.io/tls · bootstrap.kubernetes.io/token.
     📖 KEY TERM — immutable ConfigMap/Secret: marked immutable:true so it can only
        be deleted+recreated, never edited — safer and lighter on the control plane.
     🧠 ANALOGY: env-injected config is printed in the startup banner (fixed); a
        mounted volume is a live noticeboard (updates while the app runs).

  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 8
  [DEPLOY easypj — apply order & what each object does]
   1  ns easypj-dev          the namespace that holds everything
   2  ⚙️ ConfigMap easypj-config   DB_HOST · ALLOWED_HOSTS · DEBUG
   3  🔒 Secret easypj-secret      SECRET_KEY · DB_PASSWORD (base64)
   4  🔄 Deployment easypj-web     image easypj:v1, envFrom CM+Secret, probes
   5  🔌 Service easypj-web        ClusterIP 80 -> targetPort 8000
   6  ▶ kubectl port-forward svc/easypj-web 8000:80  -> open /jobs/  ✅
   apply ─►📡 apiserver ─►🗄️ etcd ─►🔄 ctrl-mgr (RS->Pod) ─►📋 scheduler ─►
   🤖 kubelet (pull + run) ─►🌐 kube-proxy (route) ─► 🎉 easypj LIVE
   ⚠️ image not built/pushed -> ImagePullBackOff · secret not base64 -> error

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how envFrom injects ConfigMap/Secret into the container env; how
      readiness gates traffic via endpoints.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): initContainers for DB migrations; readiness-gated
      rollouts; env-over-envFrom precedence; the downward API; minReadySeconds;
      recreate vs rolling for schema migrations.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: ns → ConfigMap → Secret → Deployment → Service →
      port-forward; envFrom injects config; readiness gates traffic via endpoints.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: image not built/pushed → ImagePullBackOff; targetPort ≠
      containerPort; Secret values not base64 (use stringData); selector ≠ labels
      → empty endpoints.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: scaffold with `kubectl create deploy easypj-web
      --image=easypj:v1 $do`; `kubectl expose` for the Service; verify endpoints.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: create ns · create cm/secret · apply -f · expose · port-forward
      · get all/ep · describe · logs.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: deploy easypj from scratch in under 10 minutes.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Create namespace + Deployment + Service for easypj (imperative).
      Q2  Inject DB_HOST into the pod from a ConfigMap.
      Q3  Expose SECRET_KEY from a Secret as an env var.
      Q4  The pod is ImagePullBackOff — diagnose and fix.
      Q5  Port-forward the Service and hit /jobs/.
      Q6  Scale easypj-web to 3 replicas (command).
      Q7  Add a readiness probe so traffic flows only when /jobs/ is up.
      Q8  Confirm the Service has endpoints; what if it doesn't?
      Q9  Roll the image to easypj:v2 and watch the rollout.
      Q10 A pod is Pending — which command shows why, and where do you look?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: easypj-dev running, traffic path drawn.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 2 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ kubeconfig (clusters/users/contexts) controls WHICH cluster you hit;  │

  │ ✅ kubectl = verb+resource+name+flags; `explain` is your manual;         │

PART 3: CLUSTER ARCHITECTURE, INSTALLATION & CONFIGURATION              🟡 🎯
  Chapters 9-12  ·  CKA DOMAIN WEIGHT: 25% (MAJOR)  ·  "Build the house"
  What you'll build: a real multi-node kubeadm cluster, configured by hand,
  with etcd backups you can restore from — running easypj on it.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 3 INTRO]
  This is the heart of the administrator's job and a quarter of the exam.
  Minikube hid the hard parts; now you build a cluster from bare machines with
  kubeadm, configure the control plane by editing its static-pod manifests,
  and master the single most important admin skill — etcd backup & restore.
  ⚠️ This part is mostly done over SSH on the nodes, not just via kubectl.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 3 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch9 INSTALL (kubeadm) ─▶ Ch10 CONFIGURE (apiserver/kubelet/admission)   │
  │        │                          │                                     │
  │        ▼                          ▼                                     │
  │ Ch11 etcd (backup/restore) ◀─▶ Ch12 MANAGE control plane (static pods)  │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 3 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: prep nodes (swap off, kernel modules, containerd) ->
  kubeadm init -> CNI -> join workers -> configure apiserver/kubelet/admission
  -> back up etcd (and prove you can restore) -> redeploy easypj on YOUR
  cluster. VISUAL: the kubeadm cluster-creation flow (reuse+augment cka_1).
  📖 EXTENSION INTERFACES (official competency) introduced here and revisited:
     CRI (Ch 2/3) · CNI (Ch 9/17) · CSI (Ch 21) — "the three plug points."

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 9: Installing Kubernetes with kubeadm                        🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~3h · HEAVILY TESTED. Domain: Cluster Arch 25%.
  [B] WHY: the exam may hand you a half-built or broken cluster; you must know
      every install step to finish or fix it. VISUAL: kubeadm does/doesn't card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: prepare infra; init a control plane; install a CNI; join
      workers; verify; recover a lost join token.
  [D] CKA RELEVANCE: 🎯 "prepare infra," "create clusters with kubeadm."
  9.1  What is kubeadm? 🎯 — bootstraps a cluster (certs, control plane, join).
       VISUAL: reuse+augment "YOU provide vs kubeadm handles vs NOT kubeadm."
  ── 9.2  Pre-requisites for All Nodes ───────────────────────────────  🟡 🎯
     Run on EVERY node before init/join:
       # 1) swap off — kubeadm refuses to run with swap on
       sudo swapoff -a && sudo sed -i '/ swap / s/^/#/' /etc/fstab
       # 2) kernel modules + sysctl for bridged pod traffic
       sudo modprobe overlay && sudo modprobe br_netfilter
       cat <<EOF | sudo tee /etc/sysctl.d/k8s.conf
       net.bridge.bridge-nf-call-iptables = 1
       net.ipv4.ip_forward                = 1
       EOF
       sudo sysctl --system
       # 3) containerd with the SYSTEMD cgroup driver (must match the kubelet):
       #    /etc/containerd/config.toml → SystemdCgroup = true ; restart containerd
       # 4) install + HOLD the toolchain (an apt upgrade must not break the node)
       sudo apt-get install -y kubeadm kubelet kubectl
       sudo apt-mark hold kubeadm kubelet kubectl
  9.3  Initialize the Control Plane 🎯 — `kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr=...
       --apiserver-advertise-address=...`; copy admin.conf; SAVE the join cmd.
  9.4  Install a CNI Plugin 🎯 — 📖 CNI = pod networking plug point; Calico/
       Flannel/Cilium/Weave table; pods stay NotReady until CNI is up.
  9.5  Join Worker Nodes 🎯 — `kubeadm join ... --token ... --discovery-token-
       ca-cert-hash ...`; recover: `kubeadm token create --print-join-command`.
       VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 cluster-setup flow (prereq->init->kubeconfig
       ->CNI->join->Ready).
  9.6  Verifying the Cluster — get nodes (all Ready); kube-system pods; deploy
       easypj as the smoke test.
  9.7  🧪 PRACTICE EXERCISES — build a 3-node cluster; token recovery; add a
       control-plane node; fix a NotReady worker.
  ── 9.8  Required Ports (open these between nodes) ──────────────────  🟡 🎯
     CONTROL-PLANE node (inbound):
       6443        kube-apiserver — every client & node dials this
       2379-2380   etcd client (2379) + peer/Raft (2380)
       10259       kube-scheduler        10257  kube-controller-manager
       10250       kubelet API (control plane → kubelet)
     WORKER node (inbound):
       10250       kubelet API           10256  kube-proxy health
       30000-32767 NodePort Service range
     💡 Blocked 6443 → joins & kubectl fail · blocked 10250 → `kubectl logs/exec`
        + metrics fail · blocked 2379-2380 → etcd can't form quorum.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 9
  [BUILD A CLUSTER WITH kubeadm — the order that matters]
   ALL NODES (prep):  swapoff -a · load overlay+br_netfilter · sysctl
                      bridge-nf-call+ip_forward · install containerd
                      (SystemdCgroup=true) · install kubeadm/kubelet/kubectl + hold
        │
        ▼  CONTROL PLANE only
   kubeadm init --pod-network-cidr=10.244.0.0/16  ─► generates certs+manifests
        │  then: mkdir ~/.kube; cp admin.conf; chown   (+ SAVE the join cmd!)
        ▼
   kubectl apply -f <CNI>   (Calico/Cilium) ─► nodes go Ready (NOT before!)
        │
        ▼  EACH WORKER
   kubeadm join <cp>:6443 --token <t> --discovery-token-ca-cert-hash sha256:<h>
        │   (lost it? kubeadm token create --print-join-command)
        ▼
   ✅ kubectl get nodes  ─ all Ready  ─► deploy easypj as the smoke test
   ⚠️ NotReady everywhere = no CNI · token expired = regenerate · swap on = fail

  [G] 🔧 UTH: the 14-phase kubeadm init sequence (preflight->certs->kubeconfig
      ->static-pod manifests->etcd->bootstrap-token->addons).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the kubeadm-config ConfigMap; declarative
      `--config` init; TLS bootstrapping & bootstrap tokens; --upload-certs for
      HA; air-gapped installs; --ignore-preflight-errors; custom CRI socket.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: prep every node (swap off, modules, sysctl, containerd) →
      `kubeadm init` on the CP → install a CNI (nodes stay NotReady until then)
      → `kubeadm join` the workers. Save the join command.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: swap on (kubeadm refuses); no CNI → NotReady; expired join
      token; cgroup-driver mismatch; pod-CIDR overlap; blocked ports (6443,
      10250, 2379-2380); forgetting apt-mark hold.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: `kubeadm token create --print-join-command` regenerates a
      lost join; `--upload-certs` for HA CP joins; `kubeadm reset` to undo.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: kubeadm init/join/token/reset · kubeadm config images pull ·
      systemctl status kubelet · journalctl -u kubelet.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: rebuild a cluster from scratch in under 30 minutes.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Disable swap permanently on a node (commands).
      Q2  Name the 3 commands you run right after `kubeadm init`.
      Q3  All nodes are NotReady after init — most likely cause and fix.
      Q4  The join token expired — add a new worker anyway (commands).
      Q5  Why must --pod-network-cidr match the CNI's expectation?
      Q6  The kubelet is crash-looping on a fresh node — where do you look?
      Q7  Which kernel modules & sysctls are required, and why?
      Q8  Cleanly remove a worker (drain + delete + reset).
      Q9  Which ports must be open between control plane and workers?
      Q10 Pin the toolchain so an apt upgrade can't break the node.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the full kubeadm build, one flow.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 10: Cluster Configuration & kubeconfig                       🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25%.
  [B] WHY: configuring the apiserver/kubelet/admission is how you bend the
      cluster to requirements — and how you accidentally break it. VISUAL:
      admission pipeline diagram.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: deep-read kubeconfig; configure apiserver flags; configure
      kubelet; enable/understand admission controllers; navigate API groups.
  10.1 kubeconfig Deep Dive 🎯 — clusters/users/contexts; merge files. VISUAL:
       full annotated kubeconfig.
  10.2 Managing Multiple Clusters 🎯 — view/get-contexts/use-context/set-context;
       KUBECONFIG merge precedence. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 multi-cluster.
  10.3 API Server Configuration 🎯 — /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml;
       key flags (--authorization-mode, --enable-admission-plugins,
       --etcd-servers, --service-cluster-ip-range); edit -> kubelet restarts it.
       ⚠️ bad edit -> apiserver won't start (recovery: fix the YAML).
  10.4 kubelet Configuration 🎯 — /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml; systemd unit;
       clusterDNS/clusterDomain/cgroupDriver; journalctl -u kubelet.
  10.5 Admission Controllers 🎯 — 📖 intercept requests after authZ, before
       etcd; Mutating then Validating. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 admission
       pipeline (authN->authZ->mutating->validating->persist). Key ones:
       NamespaceLifecycle, LimitRanger, ResourceQuota, PodSecurity,
       NodeRestriction, DefaultStorageClass.
  10.6 API Groups & Versioning 🎯 — core (/api/v1) vs named (/apis/group/ver);
       api-resources / api-versions. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 API tree.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 10
  [THE API REQUEST PIPELINE — every kubectl apply travels this]
   kubectl apply -f pod.yaml
        ▼
   🔐 AUTHENTICATION   who are you?   (client cert / token / OIDC)
        ▼
   🛂 AUTHORIZATION    allowed?       (RBAC: verb + resource)
        ▼
   🧪 ADMISSION  ── MUTATING webhooks (add defaults, e.g. LimitRanger)
        │         ── schema validation
        │         ── VALIDATING webhooks / PodSecurity (reject if invalid)
        ▼
   🗄️ PERSIST in etcd  ─► controllers + scheduler take over
   💡 Mutating runs BEFORE validating. Enable plugins via --enable-admission-
      plugins on the kube-apiserver static-pod manifest.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the kubelet watches the manifests dir & reconciles static
      pods; how an apiserver flag change propagates (file -> kubelet -> new pod).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): API Priority & Fairness; audit webhook backends;
      KMS v2 encryption providers; --feature-gates; the front-proxy/aggregation
      certs; OIDC flags; admission-plugin ordering.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: configure the control plane by editing its static-pod
      manifests (kubelet auto-restarts on save); admission runs after authZ
      (mutating then validating); enable plugins via --enable-admission-plugins.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: a manifest typo crashes the apiserver (kubectl dies — recover
      by fixing the file); `systemctl daemon-reload` before restarting kubelet
      after a unit change; don't confuse --cert-file with --peer-cert-file.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: ALWAYS copy a manifest before editing (`cp x.yaml /tmp`);
      watch it come back with `crictl ps` / `get pods -n kube-system -w`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: edit /etc/kubernetes/manifests/* · api-resources · api-versions
      · config view · journalctl -u kubelet.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: enable an admission plugin, verify it took, then revert.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Name the 3 kubeconfig sections and what each holds.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 11: etcd — The Cluster Database (Backup & Restore)           🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · ALMOST-GUARANTEED EXAM TASK. Domain: 25%.
  [B] WHY: etcd is the cluster's entire memory. Lose it without a backup and
      the cluster is gone. This is THE highest-value admin skill. VISUAL: "etcd
      = the cluster's memory" (reuse+augment cka_1).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain etcd & Raft; take a verified snapshot; restore to a
      new data-dir and repoint the static pod; secure etcd (TLS + encryption
      at rest).
  11.1 What is etcd? 🎯 — distributed KV store; /registry/* keys; Raft; ⚠️
       Secrets stored UNENCRYPTED by default. VISUAL: /registry key listing
       (easypj objects).
  11.2 etcd Architecture & Raft 🎯 — leader/follower; quorum = N/2+1; odd member
       counts; 3 tolerate 1, 5 tolerate 2. VISUAL: reuse+augment 3-node Raft.
  11.3 etcd Backup — the snapshot 🎯 — `ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl snapshot save
       /opt/etcd-backup.db --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 --cacert=...
       --cert=... --key=...`; verify with `snapshot status --write-table`.
       VISUAL: the 4 required flags anatomy callout; where to find cert paths
       (etcd.yaml / describe etcd pod).
  11.4 etcd Restore 🎯 — `snapshot restore ... --data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-restored`;
       then edit etcd.yaml: change --data-dir AND the hostPath volume; kubelet
       restarts etcd. ⚠️ restore to a NEW dir; update BOTH places. VISUAL:
       reuse+augment cka_1 backup->disaster->restore flow.
  11.5 etcd Security & Encryption at Rest 🎯🏢 — TLS (default); EncryptionConfig
       (aescbc) + --encryption-provider-config on apiserver; re-encrypt existing
       secrets (`get secrets -A -o json | kubectl replace -f -`).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 11
  [etcd RAFT — quorum keeps the cluster's memory consistent]
   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐
   │ etcd-0   │◄─►│ etcd-1   │◄─►│ etcd-2   │   3 members
   │ LEADER   │   │ follower │   │ follower │   quorum = N/2+1 = 2
   │ :2379/80 │   │          │   │          │   tolerate 1 failure
   └──────────┘   └──────────┘   └──────────┘
   WRITE: client ─► LEADER ─► replicate ─► commit once a MAJORITY ack ─► ✅
   ⚠️ Always ODD members (3,5,7). 5 ⇒ tolerate 2. Lose quorum ⇒ read-only/down.

  [BACKUP ▶ DISASTER ▶ RESTORE — the #1 admin skill]
   snapshot save ──► 💾 /opt/etcd-backup.db  (needs --cacert --cert --key)
        │
   ─ ─ ─│─ ─ ─ ─ 💥 DISASTER ─ ─ ─ ─
        ▼
   snapshot restore ──data-dir=/var/lib/etcd-RESTORED  (a NEW dir!)
        ▼
   edit /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml:
        change --data-dir  AND  the hostPath volume  ──► both!
        ▼
   🤖 kubelet auto-restarts etcd ──► ✅ cluster back at the snapshot point

  [G] 🔧 UTH: BoltDB + WAL; MVCC revisions; compaction & defragmentation; the
      write path (leader -> replicate -> commit on majority).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): --auto-compaction-mode/retention tuning; scheduled
      defrag; member add/remove & learner members; quorum-loss & split-brain
      recovery; NOSPACE alarms & disarm; lease/txn semantics; perf tuning
      (--heartbeat-interval/--election-timeout); external-etcd topology.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: etcd holds ALL state; snapshot save needs the 3 TLS flags +
      endpoint; restore goes to a NEW data-dir and you repoint etcd.yaml (data-dir
      AND hostPath); odd member count, quorum = N/2+1.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: missing --cacert/--cert/--key; restoring into the LIVE data-dir;
      forgetting the hostPath update; not stopping the apiserver for external etcd;
      assuming etcd backs up PV data (it doesn't).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: alias the etcdctl TLS flags; ALWAYS take a fresh snapshot
      before any restore; cert paths live in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/etcd.yaml.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: etcdctl snapshot save/status/restore · etcdctl member list ·
      etcdctl endpoint health · ETCDCTL_API=3.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: back up etcd, delete a Deployment, restore, confirm it's back.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Write the full `etcdctl snapshot save` command with TLS flags.
      Q2  Restore a snapshot and repoint the static pod — every step.
      Q3  Where do you find the 3 cert paths etcdctl needs?
      Q4  Verify a snapshot file before trusting it. (snapshot status)
      Q5  Quorum math: how many failures does a 5-member cluster tolerate?
      Q6  A restore failed with "data-dir not empty" — why, and the fix.
      Q7  Enable encryption at rest for Secrets (EncryptionConfiguration).
      Q8  External vs stacked etcd — one operational difference.
      Q9  Check etcd cluster health from the command line.
      Q10 Automate a nightly etcd backup with a CronJob.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: backup -> disaster -> restore, one timeline.

  Chapter 12: Managing the Control Plane                               🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% + Troubleshooting 30%.
  [B] WHY: when the control plane misbehaves you fix it by reading logs and
      editing static-pod manifests — often with the apiserver DOWN. VISUAL:
      "what breaks when each component fails" matrix.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: manage control-plane static pods; inspect health (with &
      without kubectl); safely modify components; recover from bad config;
      understand the CNI/CSI/CRI extension interfaces as a set.
  12.1 Control Plane as Static Pods 🎯 — manifests dir listing (etcd, apiserver,
       controller-manager, scheduler .yaml); kubelet owns their lifecycle.

       component logs; component health endpoints; when apiserver is DOWN use
       `crictl ps/logs` + `journalctl -u kubelet`.

       workflow (back up -> edit -> wait -> verify -> recover). ⚠️ typo = no
       apiserver.

       PriorityClass; node-monitor-grace-period / pod-eviction-timeout.

       (storage): the 3 plug points that make K8s pluggable. VISUAL: 3-socket
       diagram (kubelet->CRI, pod net->CNI, volumes->CSI).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 12
  [CONTROL PLANE = STATIC PODS — edit a file, kubelet restarts the pod]
   /etc/kubernetes/manifests/
     ├ etcd.yaml                     ┐
     ├ kube-apiserver.yaml           ├─ 🤖 kubelet WATCHES this dir
     ├ kube-controller-manager.yaml  │   edit -> auto-restart (~30-60s)
     └ kube-scheduler.yaml           ┘   remove file -> pod deleted
   ⚠️ a typo here = apiserver won't start. Fix the YAML -> kubelet recovers it.
   kubectl dead? ─► crictl ps -a · crictl logs <id> · journalctl -u kubelet
   📖 plug points: kubelet─CRI─runtime · pods─CNI─net · volumes─CSI─storage

PART 4: WORKLOADS & SCHEDULING                                          🟡 🎯
  Chapters 13-16  ·  CKA DOMAIN WEIGHT: 15%  ·  "Run apps the right way"
  What you'll build: easypj scaled to 3 self-healing replicas with zero-
  downtime rollouts, autoscaling, smart placement, and the right workload
  type for every job (web, db, batch, per-node agent).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 4 INTRO]
  A Pod alone is fragile. This part is about the controllers and policies that
  make workloads ROBUST and SELF-HEALING: Deployments for stateless apps,
  rolling updates & rollbacks, autoscaling under load, scheduling so the right
  pod lands on the right node, resource governance so no pod starves another,
  and the specialized workload types (Job/CronJob/DaemonSet/StatefulSet).

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 4 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch13 RUN & SCALE ──▶ Ch14 PLACE (scheduling) ──▶ Ch15 GOVERN (resources)│
  │  Deploy/RS/HPA          affinity/taints/topology     requests/limits/QoS │
  │            └────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┘            │
  │                                 ▼                                        │
  │            Ch16 SPECIALIZE: Job · CronJob · DaemonSet · StatefulSet      │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 4 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: turn easypj into a Deployment, roll out v2 with zero
  downtime, autoscale it under load, pin db pods to fast-disk nodes, set
  requests/limits so QoS is sane, then add a CronJob (db-backup) and run the
  postgres tier as a StatefulSet. 📖 NOTE: ConfigMaps & Secrets (an official
  Workloads competency) are taught in depth in Ch 23 (Part 6) and used here.
  VISUAL: workload-type decision tree 🧭 (reuse+augment cka_1).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 13: Deployments, ReplicaSets & Scaling                       🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Workloads 15%.
  [B] WHY: Deployments are how you ship and update easypj safely. VISUAL:
      rolling-update timeline.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: create/scale deployments; perform & watch rolling updates;
      roll back; pause/resume; understand ReplicaSets; autoscale with HPA;
      configure liveness/readiness/startup probes for robust self-healing.
  13.1 Deployments — complete lifecycle 🎯 — (a) create (imperative+YAML);
       (b) scale; (c) rolling update (`set image`; maxSurge/maxUnavailable;
       VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 v1->v2 timeline); (d) rollback (`rollout
       history/undo --to-revision`); (e) pause/resume to batch changes.
  13.2 ReplicaSets — under the hood 🎯 — matchLabels vs matchExpressions;
       revisionHistoryLimit; never edit RS directly.
  13.3 Horizontal Pod Autoscaler (HPA) 🎯 — requires metrics-server; `kubectl
       autoscale deploy easypj --min=3 --max=10 --cpu-percent=70`; HPA v2
       (multiple/custom metrics); scale-up fast / down slow. VISUAL: reuse+
       augment cka_1 HPA flow. 🏢 mention VPA & Cluster Autoscaler (when to use).
  13.4 Deployment Strategies 🎯 — RollingUpdate (default) vs Recreate; tuning
       maxSurge/maxUnavailable; 🏢 blue-green & canary (concept, not built-in).
  13.5 Health Probes & Self-Healing Primitives 🎯 — 📖 liveness (restart if
       dead) / readiness (gate traffic via endpoints) / startup (protect slow
       boots); handlers HTTP/TCP/Exec/gRPC; tuning initialDelaySeconds/
       periodSeconds/timeoutSeconds/failureThreshold/successThreshold. THE
       "robust self-healing" trio (official competency) = ReplicaSet (replace
       lost pods) + Probes (detect sick pods) + PodDisruptionBudget (survive
       voluntary disruption). VISUAL: probe-decision card (which probe, when)
       + the self-healing loop (probe fail -> restart OR drop-from-endpoints).
       # easypj-web container probes (all three):
       startupProbe:                 # protect a slow boot; gates the others
         httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8000 }
         failureThreshold: 30
         periodSeconds: 5            # up to 150s to start before liveness applies
       readinessProbe:               # gate traffic (endpoints) — no restart
         httpGet: { path: /ready, port: 8000 }
         initialDelaySeconds: 5
         periodSeconds: 10
       livenessProbe:                # restart if truly dead
         httpGet: { path: /healthz, port: 8000 }
         initialDelaySeconds: 15
         periodSeconds: 20
         failureThreshold: 3
       ⚠️ a too-aggressive liveness probe causes restart storms; readiness !=
       liveness; a failing readiness empties endpoints (503s) without restarts.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 13
  [ROLLING UPDATE — easypj v1 -> v2 with ZERO downtime]
   maxSurge:1  maxUnavailable:0   (always >= 3 ready)
   t0  [v1][v1][v1]                 all v1
   t1  [v1][v1][v1][v2]+            spin up 1 new (surge)
   t2  [v1][v1][v2][v2]             old one removed once new is READY
   t3  [v1][v2][v2][v2]             repeat...
   t4  [v2][v2][v2]              ✅ all v2, never < 3 serving
   ↩️ broke it? kubectl rollout undo deploy/easypj  (old ReplicaSet kept at 0)

  [SELF-HEALING TRIO — the official "robust deployment" primitives]
   ┌────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────────────────┐
   │ 🔁 ReplicaSet   │ pod/node lost  ─► recreate to keep replica count│
   │ ❤️ Probes       │ liveness fails ─► restart;  readiness fails ─►  │
   │                │ drop from endpoints (no traffic, no restart)    │
   │ 🛡️ PDB          │ drain/upgrade  ─► keep minAvailable up          │
   └────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ⚠️ aggressive liveness ⇒ restart storms · readiness ≠ liveness.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: the rolling-update math (RS scale up/down per step); how rollout
      status reads .status.conditions; how revisions map to old ReplicaSets.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): progressDeadlineSeconds & minReadySeconds;
      Available vs Progressing conditions; HPA v2 behavior (stabilizationWindow/
      policies); the scale subresource; canary via two Deployments.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: Deployment→RS→Pods; rolling update + `rollout undo` = safe
      ship + instant rollback; HPA needs requests + metrics-server; the self-
      healing trio = ReplicaSet + probes + PDB.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: the selector is immutable; HPA does nothing without resource
      requests; a stuck rollout (bad image) → `rollout undo`; an aggressive
      liveness probe causes restart storms; readiness ≠ liveness.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: `kubectl set image deploy/easypj web=easypj:v2`; confirm with
      `rollout status`; `scale --replicas=N`; `rollout undo --to-revision=N`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: create deploy · scale · set image · rollout status/history/
      undo/pause/resume · autoscale · get rs.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: roll easypj v1 → v2 → a bad image → undo.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Update easypj to v2 with zero downtime (command + the fields that control it).
      Q2  A rollout is stuck on a bad image — diagnose and roll back.
      Q3  RollingUpdate vs Recreate — when is each correct?
      Q4  Give 3 ways to scale a Deployment.
      Q5  Name the chain for pod easypj-web-7d4-x9k2 (Deployment→RS→Pod).
      Q6  Create an HPA 3→10 at 70% CPU — what prerequisite is required?
      Q7  maxSurge:1/maxUnavailable:0 with 3 replicas — how many pods peak?
      Q8  Pause a Deployment, make 2 edits, resume as ONE rollout.
      Q9  Add a startup probe so a slow-booting easypj isn't killed early.
      Q10 A failing readiness probe — what happens to traffic vs the pod?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: one Deployment, many ReplicaSets, rollout & rollback.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 14: Scheduling & Node Management                             🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~3h · Domain: Workloads 15% (Pod admission & scheduling).
  [B] WHY: control WHERE pods run (and keep nodes maintainable). VISUAL:
      scheduler filter->score->bind flowchart.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain the scheduler; use nodeSelector/affinity; pod
      (anti)affinity; taints/tolerations; topology spread; priority/preemption;
      cordon/drain/uncordon.
  14.1 How the Scheduler Works 🎯 — filtering then scoring then binding. VISUAL:
       reuse+augment cka_1 3-phase flowchart (easypj 256Mi/250m).
  14.2 Node Selectors 🎯 — label node, select in pod spec.
  14.3 Node Affinity 🎯 — required vs preferred ...DuringScheduling...; operators.
  14.4 Pod (Anti-)Affinity 🎯 — co-locate (easypj near cache) / spread replicas.
  14.5 Taints & Tolerations 🎯 — taint repels; toleration permits; effects
       NoSchedule/PreferNoSchedule/NoExecute; control-plane taint. VISUAL:
       reuse+augment cka_1 taint/toleration card.
  14.6 Node Management 🎯 — cordon -> drain (--ignore-daemonsets
       --delete-emptydir-data [--force for bare pods]) -> maintain -> uncordon.
       A PodDisruptionBudget (spec.minAvailable / maxUnavailable + a selector)
       makes `drain` HONOR availability: eviction proceeds gradually and BLOCKS
       rather than drop below the budget — the official way to "safely drain."
       VISUAL: reuse+augment maintenance flow.
  14.7 Topology Spread Constraints 🎯 — maxSkew/topologyKey/whenUnsatisfiable/
       labelSelector; even spread across zones/nodes.
  14.8 Priority & Preemption 🏢 — PriorityClass; high-pri can evict low-pri.
  14.9 🧪 PRACTICE EXERCISES — taints; node affinity (required+preferred);
       anti-affinity spread; static pod on a worker.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 14
  [THE SCHEDULER — filter ▶ score ▶ bind  (easypj pod: 256Mi, 250m)]
   New Pod (unbound)
        │
        ▼  ── FILTER: which nodes CAN run it? ──
   Node1 (512Mi free) ✅   Node2 (128Mi free) ❌ too small
   Node3 (1Gi free)   ✅   Node4 taint gpu=true ❌ no toleration
        │  (only survivors continue)
        ▼  ── SCORE: which fits BEST? ──
   Node1 = 65        Node3 = 82  ◄ more headroom + affinity + spread
        │
        ▼  ── BIND ──   Pod ─► Node3 ─► 🤖 kubelet starts it
   📖 Levers: nodeSelector < nodeAffinity < taints/tolerations < topologySpread.

  [TAINTS & TOLERATIONS — nodes repel, pods opt-in]
   🚫 Node taint: gpu=true:NoSchedule
        ├─ 📦 easypj pod (no toleration)      ─► ❌ rejected
        └─ 📦 ml-job pod (tolerates gpu=true) ─► ✅ scheduled
   💡 Control-plane nodes carry node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane:NoSchedule
      by default — that's why your pods don't land there.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: predicates vs priorities; how the scheduler caches node state;
      how NoExecute evicts already-running pods with tolerationSeconds.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the scheduling framework (filter/score/bind
      plugins & extension points); multiple schedulers via schedulerName; the
      descheduler; pod overhead (RuntimeClass); nodeName bypass; PreferNoSchedule
      vs NoSchedule nuance; well-known taints (not-ready/unreachable/pressure);
      bin-packing vs spreading score weights.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: scheduler = filter→score→bind; placement levers escalate
      nodeSelector < affinity < taints/tolerations < topology spread; cordon →
      drain → uncordon for node maintenance.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: taint syntax is key=value:Effect; drain is blocked by a PDB or
      bare pods; required vs preferred affinity; the control-plane taint silently
      explains why pods won't land there.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: remove a taint with a trailing dash (`taint nodes n
      key=val:NoSchedule-`); `get po -o wide` to see placement.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: label/taint nodes · cordon/drain/uncordon · get nodes
      --show-labels · describe node · get po -o wide.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: taint a node, prove a pod won't schedule, add a toleration, prove
      it does.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Pin easypj to nodes labelled disk=ssd (nodeSelector).
      Q2  Require pods in a zone using nodeAffinity (required).
      Q3  Spread 3 easypj replicas across nodes with anti-affinity.
      Q4  Taint a node and give one pod a matching toleration.
      Q5  Cordon vs drain — what does each do?
      Q6  Drain a node for maintenance, then bring it back.
      Q7  Even-spread easypj across 3 zones (topologySpreadConstraints).
      Q8  Make easypj higher priority than batch jobs (PriorityClass).
      Q9  A pod is Pending — list the scheduling reasons to check.
      Q10 Remove a taint from a node (command).
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: a pod's path from "unscheduled" to a chosen node.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 15: Resource Management                                      🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Workloads 15% (limits/admission).
  [B] WHY: requests/limits decide scheduling, throttling, and who gets killed
      under pressure. VISUAL: requests-vs-limits card (throttle vs OOMKilled).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: set requests/limits; understand QoS classes; enforce defaults
      with LimitRange; cap namespaces with ResourceQuota.
  15.1 Requests & Limits 🎯 — requests = scheduling floor; limits = hard ceiling;
       CPU millicores (throttle) vs memory (OOMKill). VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1.
  15.2 QoS Classes 🎯 — Guaranteed/Burstable/BestEffort; eviction order. VISUAL:
       QoS table with easypj prod/dev mapping.
  15.3 LimitRange 🎯 — per-container defaults/min/max in a namespace.
  15.4 ResourceQuota 🎯 — namespace-wide totals (cpu/mem/pods/pvcs/objects).
       VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 3-level resource hierarchy.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 15
  [REQUESTS vs LIMITS — and what happens when you cross a limit]
   resources:
     requests: { cpu: 250m, memory: 256Mi }  ◄ scheduling FLOOR (guaranteed)
     limits:   { cpu: 500m, memory: 512Mi }  ◄ hard CEILING (enforced)
   Over CPU limit  ─► ⏳ THROTTLED (slowed, not killed)
   Over MEM limit  ─► 💀 OOMKilled (137)
   No requests ─► scheduler is blind   ·   No limits ─► pod can eat the node

   [QoS & GOVERNANCE NESTING]
   ResourceQuota (namespace TOTAL: 8 cpu / 16Gi / 30 pods)
     └─ LimitRange (per-container default/min/max)
          └─ Pod spec (this easypj: 250m req / 500m lim)
   QoS:  req==lim ⇒ Guaranteed (evicted last) · partial ⇒ Burstable ·
         none ⇒ BestEffort (evicted first)

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how QoS is computed from requests==limits; how the kubelet
      enforces cgroup limits; eviction thresholds & node pressure.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): cgroup v1 vs v2; CPU CFS quota & throttling
      metrics; memory QoS & PSI; ephemeral-storage requests/limits; extended
      resources & device plugins (GPU); node-allocatable & overcommit.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: requests = scheduling floor (guaranteed), limits = hard
      ceiling; CPU over limit → throttled, memory over limit → OOMKilled; QoS
      (Guaranteed/Burstable/BestEffort) sets eviction order.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: with a ResourceQuota every pod MUST set limits (or a LimitRange
      supplies defaults); a too-low memory limit → CrashLoop via OOMKilled; no
      requests → the scheduler is blind.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: set BOTH requests and limits; use a LimitRange to inject
      defaults instead of editing every pod; `describe po` shows the QoS class.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: set resources · create quota · get/describe limitrange/quota ·
      top po · describe po (Last State / QoS).
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: add a LimitRange, create a pod with no limits, watch defaults get
      injected.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Set CPU/memory requests & limits on the easypj container.
      Q2  Determine a given pod's QoS class and justify it.
      Q3  Create a LimitRange with default + max for a namespace.
      Q4  Create a ResourceQuota capping cpu/mem/pods/PVCs.
      Q5  A pod keeps OOMKilling — root cause and two fixes.
      Q6  A pod won't schedule due to requests — what do you check?
      Q7  Why is BestEffort risky in production?
      Q8  A pod create is rejected by the quota — why, and the fix.
      Q9  Prove a LimitRange injected defaults into a no-limits pod.
      Q10 CPU throttling vs OOMKill — which resource causes which?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: ResourceQuota -> LimitRange -> Pod spec, nested.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 16: Jobs, CronJobs, DaemonSets & StatefulSets                🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Workloads 15% (self-healing primitives).
  [B] WHY: not everything is a web server. easypj needs migrations (Job),
      backups (CronJob), and a stateful database (StatefulSet). VISUAL:
      workload-type decision tree 🧭.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: choose & build the right workload type for each need.
  16.1 Jobs — run to completion 🎯 — completions/parallelism/backoffLimit.
       apiVersion: batch/v1
       kind: Job
       metadata: { name: easypj-migrate, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         backoffLimit: 4              # retries before the Job is marked Failed
         template:
           spec:
             restartPolicy: Never
             containers:
             - name: migrate
               image: easypj:v1
               command: ["python","manage.py","migrate","--noinput"]
               envFrom:
               - configMapRef: { name: easypj-config }
               - secretRef:    { name: easypj-secret }
  16.2 CronJobs — scheduled 🎯 — cron syntax; concurrencyPolicy. VISUAL: cron
       expression table.
       apiVersion: batch/v1
       kind: CronJob
       metadata: { name: easypj-db-backup, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         schedule: "0 2 * * *"        # 02:00 daily (min hr dom mon dow)
         concurrencyPolicy: Forbid    # don't overlap runs
         successfulJobsHistoryLimit: 3
         jobTemplate:
           spec:
             template:
               spec:
                 restartPolicy: OnFailure
                 containers:
                 - name: backup
                   image: postgres:16
                   command: ["sh","-c","pg_dump -h easypj-db -U easypj easypj > /backup/easypj-$(date +%F).sql"]
  16.3 DaemonSets — one pod per node 🎯 — log/monitoring/CNI agents; node-add ->
       auto-pod. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 DaemonSet diagram.
  16.4 StatefulSets — stable identity & storage 🎯 — ordered names (db-0,db-1),
       stable DNS, per-pod PVC (volumeClaimTemplates), ordered ops. VISUAL:
       reuse+augment cka_1 StatefulSet.
       apiVersion: apps/v1
       kind: StatefulSet
       metadata: { name: easypj-db, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         serviceName: easypj-db        # the Headless Service (clusterIP: None)
         replicas: 1
         selector: { matchLabels: { app: easypj-db } }
         template:
           metadata: { labels: { app: easypj-db } }
           spec:
             containers:
             - name: postgres
               image: postgres:16
               ports: [{ containerPort: 5432 }]
               envFrom:
               - secretRef: { name: easypj-secret }   # POSTGRES_PASSWORD ...
               volumeMounts:
               - { name: data, mountPath: /var/lib/postgresql/data }
         volumeClaimTemplates:         # each replica gets its OWN durable PVC
         - metadata: { name: data }
           spec:
             accessModes: ["ReadWriteOnce"]
             storageClassName: easypj-fast
             resources: { requests: { storage: 10Gi } }
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 16
  [WHICH WORKLOAD TYPE? — a decision tree for easypj]
   Need stable identity + per-pod storage?
     ├─ YES ─► 🗄️ StatefulSet      (🐘 postgres: db-0, db-1 + PVCs)
     └─ NO
         Need exactly one pod per node?
           ├─ YES ─► 🔁 DaemonSet   (log/monitoring agent)
           └─ NO
               Runs once to completion?
                 ├─ YES ─► ⚡ Job       (easypj manage.py migrate)
                 │         on a schedule? ─► ⏰ CronJob (nightly db-backup)
                 └─ NO ─► 🔄 Deployment  (easypj-web: stateless, scalable)

  [STATEFULSET vs DEPLOYMENT — names & storage]
   Deployment pods: easypj-web-7d4-x9k2  (random, interchangeable, shared/no PVC)
   StatefulSet pods: db-0 ▶ db-1 ▶ db-2  (ordered, stable DNS, OWN PVC each)
   ⚠️ StatefulSet needs a headless Service; scaling down does NOT delete PVCs.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a Job tracks completions; how CronJob spawns Jobs & handles
      missed schedules (startingDeadlineSeconds); StatefulSet ordinal & PVC
      naming (<vct>-<sts>-<ordinal>).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): Job completionMode: Indexed & podFailurePolicy &
      suspend; CronJob concurrencyPolicy/startingDeadlineSeconds; StatefulSet
      podManagementPolicy: Parallel & partitioned rollouts; DaemonSet OnDelete.
  [H] KEY TAKEAWAYS: pick the type — Deployment (stateless), StatefulSet (stable
      id+storage), DaemonSet (one/node), Job (run-to-completion), CronJob
      (scheduled); a StatefulSet needs a headless Service.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS: a Job's restartPolicy must be OnFailure/Never (not Always);
      CronJob timezone & concurrencyPolicy; a StatefulSet needs a headless
      Service; scaling a StatefulSet down does NOT delete its PVCs.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS: trigger a one-off run with `create job easypj-migrate
      --from=cronjob/easypj-backup`; `create cronjob … --schedule`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS: create job/cronjob · get jobs/cronjobs/ds/sts · create job
      --from=cronjob · scale sts.
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create a one-off easypj migrate Job; a nightly db-backup CronJob.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1  Create a Job that runs `manage.py migrate` once.
      Q2  Create a CronJob that backs up the db nightly at 02:00.
      Q3  Run a logging agent on EVERY node (which workload type?).
      Q4  Give the postgres tier stable identity + per-pod storage.
      Q5  Pick the type for: web · db · migration · nightly backup · node agent.
      Q6  Run a Job with 5 completions, 2 in parallel.
      Q7  Trigger a Job immediately from an existing CronJob.
      Q8  Why does a StatefulSet need a headless Service?
      Q9  What are the PVC names for a StatefulSet "db" with 2 replicas?
      Q10 Why do StatefulSet pods start and stop in order?
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the workload-type decision tree, captioned with easypj.

PART 5: SERVICES & NETWORKING                                           🟡 🎯
  Chapters 17-20b  ·  CKA DOMAIN WEIGHT: 20%  ·  "Connect & expose"
  What you'll build: easypj reachable from the internet via Ingress AND the
  modern Gateway API, talking to its database internally, with traffic locked
  down by NetworkPolicies and names resolved by CoreDNS.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 5 INTRO]
  A running pod nobody can reach is useless. This part is the plumbing: how
  pods get IPs and talk (flat network + CNI), how Services give them stable
  names and load-balance, how DNS resolves those names, how external traffic
  enters (Ingress AND the new Gateway API), and how NetworkPolicies firewall
  it all. 🆕 2025: Gateway API is now an official exam competency.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 5 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch17 POD NET (CNI) ─▶ Ch18 SERVICES ─▶ Ch19 DNS (CoreDNS)               │
  │   IPs & flat net        stable VIPs       name -> ClusterIP             │
  │                              │                                          │
  │                              ▼                                          │
  │ Ch20 INGRESS + NETWORKPOLICY ──▶ Ch20b GATEWAY API (modern ingress)     │
  │   L7 routing + firewall            Gateway/HTTPRoute, role-oriented     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 5 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: easypj-web Pods get CNI IPs -> a ClusterIP Service
  fronts them -> CoreDNS resolves easypj-db -> an Ingress (then a Gateway/
  HTTPRoute) exposes easypj.example.com -> a default-deny NetworkPolicy only
  allows web->db on 5432. VISUAL: the full north-south + east-west traffic map.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 17: Kubernetes Networking Fundamentals                       🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Services & Networking 20%.
  [B] WHY: every networking bug traces back to these rules. VISUAL: the 4
      fundamental networking rules card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: state the K8s network model; explain CNI's role; trace pod-
      to-pod (same/cross node); explain kube-proxy modes; basic net debugging.
  17.1 The Networking Model 🎯 — every pod a unique IP; pod-to-pod & node-to-pod
       without NAT; pod sees its real IP. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 4-rules.
  17.2 CNI Plugins 🎯 — 📖 CNI = the network plug point; Calico/Cilium/Flannel/
       Weave; who installs/troubleshoots it (you).
  17.3 Pod-to-Pod Communication 🎯 — same node (bridge) vs cross node (VXLAN/
       BGP). VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 cross-node path (cbr0/eth0/overlay).
  17.4 kube-proxy 🎯 — DaemonSet; iptables (default)/IPVS/nftables; turns
       Service VIP into pod endpoints.
  17.5 Network Troubleshooting 🩺 — check CNI/kube-proxy pods; exec curl to a
       ClusterIP; nslookup; common issues table.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 17
  [POD-TO-POD ACROSS NODES — the flat network, no NAT]
   Node 1                                Node 2
   📦 web (10.244.1.5)                   📦 db (10.244.2.8)
       │                                     ▲
   ┌───▼─────────┐  CNI overlay (VXLAN)  ┌───┴─────────┐
   │ cbr0 bridge │                        │ cbr0 bridge │
   └───┬─────────┘                        └───▲─────────┘
   ┌───▼─────────┐   eth0 ──────────► eth0 ┌──┴──────────┐
   │ 192.168.1.10│ ====== physical net ====│ 192.168.1.11│
   └─────────────┘                         └─────────────┘
   📖 Rules: every pod a unique IP · pod<->pod direct (no NAT) · a pod sees its
      OWN real IP. kube-proxy only matters for Service VIPs, not pod-to-pod.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a packet to a ClusterIP is DNAT'd to a pod IP by iptables;
      IPVS vs iptables at scale; how CNI sets up veth pairs.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): eBPF dataplane (Cilium) vs iptables/IPVS; BGP
      (Calico) vs VXLAN overlay; dual-stack IPv4/IPv6; per-node pod-CIDR
      allocation; conntrack table limits & tuning; MTU/overlay overhead;
      NetworkPolicy enforced at the eBPF layer; service mesh (sidecar vs
      ambient) at a glance.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • The K8s network model has 4 rules: every pod gets a unique IP; pods talk
        to pods WITHOUT NAT; nodes talk to pods without NAT; a pod sees its own
        real IP (the same IP others use to reach it). One flat network.
      • CNI (a plugin you install — Calico/Cilium/Flannel/Weave) implements that
        model. No CNI = nodes stay NotReady and pods never get an IP.
      • kube-proxy is NOT in the pod-to-pod path. It only programs the dataplane
        (iptables/IPVS/nftables) that turns a Service VIP into a real pod IP.
      • Same-node traffic crosses a local bridge (veth → cbr0); cross-node
        traffic is encapsulated (VXLAN) or routed (BGP) by the CNI, then hits
        the peer node's bridge.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • No CNI installed → every node shows NotReady, CoreDNS is Pending. The fix
        is "install a CNI", not "restart kubelet".
      • Flannel does NOT enforce NetworkPolicy — policies silently do nothing.
        Use Calico or Cilium when you need a pod firewall.
      • MTU mismatch (overlay adds ~50 bytes) → large packets hang/drop while
        ping/small requests work. Classic "curl hangs at TLS handshake" symptom.
      • conntrack table full on busy nodes → dropped connections; tune
        net.netfilter.nf_conntrack_max.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Debug with a throwaway pod: `kubectl run tmp --rm -it --image=nicolaka/
        netshoot -- bash` (or busybox for a minimal shell).
      • `kubectl get pods -A -o wide` shows pod IPs and which node — the fastest
        way to confirm the CNI assigned addresses.
      • A NotReady node almost always means the CNI DaemonSet pod on that node is
        crashing — check `kubectl -n kube-system get pods -o wide`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get pods -A -o wide                 # pod IPs + nodes
      kubectl -n kube-system get pods | grep -Ei 'calico|cilium|flannel|kube-proxy'
      kubectl run net --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash
      kubectl exec net -- curl -s http://easypj-web.default.svc.cluster.local
      kubectl exec net -- nslookup easypj-db
      ip route ; ip -d link show flannel.1        # on a node: overlay device/MTU
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: from a temp netshoot pod, curl the easypj-web ClusterIP, then
      curl a pod IP directly; confirm both work (proves the flat network). Scale
      down the CNI DaemonSet on one node and watch that node go NotReady.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. State the 4 rules of the Kubernetes network model.
          A. (1) Every pod has a unique IP. (2) Pods communicate pod-to-pod with
          no NAT. (3) Nodes communicate with pods with no NAT. (4) A pod sees its
          own IP as the same IP others use. Together: one flat L3 network.
      Q2. All nodes are NotReady and pods are stuck Pending/ContainerCreating.
          What is the most likely cause and fix?
          A. No CNI plugin installed (or its DaemonSet is crashing). kubelet can't
          set up pod networking, so it reports NotReady. Fix: install/repair a CNI
          (e.g. `kubectl apply -f calico.yaml`) and confirm its pods are Running.
      Q3. Is kube-proxy involved when pod A curls pod B by pod IP? Explain.
          A. No. Pod-to-pod by IP uses the CNI's flat network directly. kube-proxy
          only matters when you target a Service VIP, which it DNATs to a pod IP.
      Q4. Trace a packet from web on node1 to db on node2 (different nodes).
          A. web's veth → node1 bridge (cbr0) → CNI encapsulates (VXLAN) or routes
          (BGP) out node1 eth0 → physical net → node2 eth0 → decapsulate → node2
          bridge → db's veth → db. No NAT; db sees web's real pod IP.
      Q5. How do you debug "web can't reach db" at the network layer?
          A. Run a netshoot pod; `nslookup easypj-db` (DNS?), `curl <db-podIP>:5432`
          (pod reachable?), `curl easypj-db:5432` (Service path?). Narrow whether
          it's DNS, Service/endpoints, NetworkPolicy, or the CNI.
      Q6. What kube-proxy modes exist and how do you check which is in use?
          A. iptables (default), IPVS (better at scale), nftables (newer). Check
          the kube-proxy ConfigMap `mode:` field or `kubectl -n kube-system logs
          ds/kube-proxy | grep -i proxier`.
      Q7. How does a request to a ClusterIP reach a real pod?
          A. kube-proxy installs iptables/IPVS rules that DNAT the Service VIP:port
          to one of the READY endpoint pod IPs:targetPort, load-balanced.
      Q8. You need NetworkPolicy but it isn't enforcing. Which CNI did you pick?
          A. Probably Flannel, which ignores NetworkPolicy. Switch to a policy-
          aware CNI like Calico or Cilium.
      Q9. ping works but large HTTP responses hang across nodes. Likely cause?
          A. MTU mismatch — the overlay's encapsulation overhead shrinks the
          usable MTU; oversized packets get dropped. Align the CNI/interface MTU.
      Q10. What is a veth pair and where is it used here?
          A. A virtual ethernet cable with two ends: one inside the pod's network
          namespace (eth0), one on the host attached to the bridge. It connects
          the pod to the node's network so the CNI can route its traffic.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: a packet's journey pod->Service->pod, annotated.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 18: Services Deep Dive                                       🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Services & Networking 20%.
  [B] WHY: Services are how everything finds everything. VISUAL: selector ->
      endpoints -> pods wiring.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain Services & endpoints; use ClusterIP/NodePort/
      LoadBalancer/ExternalName/Headless; service discovery via DNS.
  18.1 How Services Work 🎯 — VIP + selector -> Endpoints/EndpointSlice auto-
       updated. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 service routing (easypj selector).
  18.2 ClusterIP 🎯 — internal default; easypj-web internal access.
  18.3 NodePort 🎯 — node IP:30000-32767; dev/testing external access.
  18.4 LoadBalancer 🎯 — cloud external LB.
  18.5 ExternalName 🎯 — DNS alias to an external service (e.g. managed psql).
  18.6 Headless Services 🎯 — clusterIP: None; per-pod DNS; required by
       StatefulSets (postgres-0.easypj-db...).
  18.7 Service Discovery 🎯 — DNS form <svc>.<ns>.svc.cluster.local; endpoints.
       VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 discovery (easypj-web -> easypj-db).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 18
  [HOW A SERVICE FINDS ITS PODS — selector -> endpoints -> load-balance]
   apply Service{ selector: app=easypj, port 80 -> targetPort 8000 }
        │
        ▼  K8s watches for Pods with matching labels
   🔌 Service  ClusterIP 10.96.45.2:80
        │
        ▼  auto-built
   📇 EndpointSlice  ── only READY pods are listed ──┐
        │  10.244.1.5:8000  10.244.2.8:8000  10.244.3.2:8000
        ▼  🌐 kube-proxy load-balances (iptables/IPVS)
   📦 web-0        📦 web-1        📦 web-2
   💡 Pod 2 fails its readiness probe? It is DROPPED from the slice — traffic
      only flows to healthy pods. No endpoints at all = selector/label mismatch.

  [THE 5 SERVICE TYPES — who can reach them]
   ┌──────────────┬───────────────────────────┬──────────────────────┐
   │ ClusterIP    │ inside cluster only (def.) │ easypj-web internal  │
   │ NodePort     │ <anyNodeIP>:30000-32767    │ dev/test external    │
   │ LoadBalancer │ cloud external LB -> nodes  │ prod on cloud        │
   │ ExternalName │ DNS CNAME to external host  │ managed psql alias   │
   │ Headless     │ clusterIP:None -> pod DNS   │ StatefulSet (db-0..) │
   └──────────────┴───────────────────────────┴──────────────────────┘

  [G] 🔧 UTH: EndpointSlice vs Endpoints; how readiness gates endpoint
      membership; sessionAffinity; externalTrafficPolicy Local vs Cluster.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): EndpointSlice scaling & topology-aware routing
      hints; internalTrafficPolicy; dual-stack Services; service IP allocation;
      allocateLoadBalancerNodePorts; headless DNS for StatefulSets.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • A Service is a stable VIP + DNS name in front of a moving set of pods. It
        selects pods by label; K8s auto-maintains an EndpointSlice of their IPs.
      • Only READY pods appear in the endpoints. Readiness probe failing = pod
        silently removed from rotation (graceful, no 500s to clients).
      • Five types: ClusterIP (internal default), NodePort (node IP:30000-32767),
        LoadBalancer (cloud LB), ExternalName (DNS CNAME to an external host),
        Headless (clusterIP:None → returns pod IPs, used by StatefulSets).
      • port is the Service's port; targetPort is the container's port. They are
        independent — port 80 → targetPort 8000 is normal for easypj-web.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Empty endpoints (`<none>`) = the selector matches no pods OR no pod is
        Ready. Check pod labels vs the Service selector and the readiness probe.
      • targetPort must match the containerPort the app actually listens on, not
        the Service port. Mismatch = connection refused.
      • NodePort must be in 30000-32767 (default range). Outside = rejected.
      • LoadBalancer stays `<pending>` with no cloud controller (bare-metal needs
        MetalLB or similar).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Fastest expose: `kubectl expose deploy easypj-web --port=80
        --target-port=8000`. Add `--type=NodePort` for external dev access.
      • `kubectl get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=easypj-web`
        shows exactly which pod IPs back a Service.
      • To debug, curl the ClusterIP from a temp pod — if pod IP works but VIP
        doesn't, it's a kube-proxy/endpoints problem, not the app.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl expose deploy easypj-web --port=80 --target-port=8000
      kubectl get svc easypj-web -o wide
      kubectl get endpoints easypj-web
      kubectl get endpointslices -l kubernetes.io/service-name=easypj-web
      kubectl describe svc easypj-web        # selector + endpoints in one view
      kubectl run t --rm -it --image=busybox -- wget -qO- easypj-web:80/jobs/
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: expose easypj-web, verify the endpoint pod IPs match the running
      pods, then edit the Deployment's pod labels so the selector no longer
      matches — watch endpoints go empty — and fix it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Expose easypj-web (port 80) to its container port 8000 imperatively.
          A. `kubectl expose deploy easypj-web --port=80 --target-port=8000`. This
          creates a ClusterIP Service selecting the deployment's pod labels.
      Q2. `kubectl get endpoints easypj-web` shows <none>. List the two causes.
          A. (1) Selector/label mismatch — no pod carries the labels the Service
          selects. (2) Pods exist but none is Ready (readiness probe failing).
      Q3. When would you choose NodePort over ClusterIP vs LoadBalancer?
          A. ClusterIP for internal-only (default). NodePort to reach a Service
          from outside on <nodeIP>:3xxxx in dev/test/bare-metal. LoadBalancer for
          a real cloud external IP in production.
      Q4. Why do StatefulSets need a Headless Service?
          A. clusterIP:None makes DNS return each pod's IP and gives stable
          per-pod names (postgres-0.easypj-db...), so clients address specific
          replicas — required for ordered, identity-bound workloads.
      Q5. What does ExternalName do and when is it useful?
          A. Maps a Service name to an external DNS name via CNAME (no proxying).
          Use it to alias a managed PostgreSQL host as `easypj-db` so app config
          stays cluster-internal.
      Q6. Give the full DNS name for easypj-db in namespace easypj.
          A. `easypj-db.easypj.svc.cluster.local`. Short forms: `easypj-db`
          (same ns) and `easypj-db.easypj`.
      Q7. The app connects but gets "connection refused". port=80 works elsewhere.
          A. targetPort likely doesn't match the container's listening port. The
          app listens on 8000, so targetPort must be 8000, not 80.
      Q8. How do you serve two ports (e.g. 80 web + 9000 metrics) from one Service?
          A. Define multiple named entries under `ports:` (each with name/port/
          targetPort). Named ports are required when there is more than one.
      Q9. What does sessionAffinity: ClientIP do?
          A. Pins a given client IP to the same backend pod for a timeout window,
          instead of load-balancing every request — useful for sticky sessions.
      Q10. externalTrafficPolicy Local vs Cluster — what's the trade-off?
          A. Cluster (default) load-balances across all nodes but SNATs, hiding
          the client IP and adding a hop. Local preserves the client source IP
          and avoids the extra hop, but only sends to pods on the receiving node.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: 5 service types on one diagram (who reaches them).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 19: DNS & CoreDNS                                            🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Domain: Services & Networking 20% (CoreDNS).
  [B] WHY: "it can't connect" is usually DNS. VISUAL: DNS resolution flow.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain cluster DNS; read/edit CoreDNS config; DNS record
      forms; troubleshoot resolution.
  19.1 How K8s DNS Works 🎯 — CoreDNS Deployment in kube-system; pod resolv.conf
       points at it; auto records for Services. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 DNS
       flow (easypj-web -> easypj-db).
  19.2 CoreDNS Configuration 🎯🏢 — the Corefile ConfigMap; plugins (kubernetes,
       forward, cache); editing & reloading.
  19.3 DNS Record Types 🎯 — A/AAAA for Services & pods; SRV; headless returns
       pod IPs; search domains & ndots.
  19.4 Troubleshooting DNS 🩺 — nslookup kubernetes.default; check CoreDNS pods/
       logs; common failures table.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 19
  [DNS RESOLUTION — how easypj-web finds easypj-db]
   📦 easypj-web  needs  http://easypj-db:5432
        │ 1. read /etc/resolv.conf
        ▼  nameserver 10.96.0.10 (CoreDNS)   search easypj.svc.cluster.local ...
   🔍 CoreDNS (Deployment in kube-system)
        │ 2. match "kubernetes" plugin -> watch API for Services
        ▼ 3. easypj-db.easypj.svc.cluster.local  ⇒  10.96.45.9 (ClusterIP)
   🔌 Service easypj-db ─► 📦 db pod(s)
   📖 Name forms:  easypj-db  (same ns)  ·  easypj-db.easypj  ·  full FQDN
   ⚠️ Most "can't connect" bugs are DNS: wrong namespace, ndots, CoreDNS down.
