  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the kubernetes plugin watches the API to synth records; ndots:5
       and why short names work; cache & negative caching.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the Corefile plugin chain & rewrite; autopath;
      pod dnsPolicy/dnsConfig (ClusterFirstWithHostNet); NodeLocal DNSCache;
      stub domains & upstream forwarding; negative-cache TTL.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Cluster DNS is CoreDNS — a Deployment in kube-system fronted by the
        kube-dns Service (commonly 10.96.0.10). Every pod's /etc/resolv.conf
        points its nameserver at that ClusterIP.
      • The Corefile (a ConfigMap) configures CoreDNS via plugins: kubernetes
        (synthesizes Service/pod records by watching the API), forward (sends
        everything else upstream), cache, errors, health, ready.
      • Service A records resolve to the ClusterIP; headless Services and pods
        resolve to pod IPs. SRV records exist for named ports.
      • Short names work because of search domains + ndots:5 in resolv.conf —
        the resolver tries `<name>.<ns>.svc.cluster.local` etc. automatically.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • A malformed Corefile → CoreDNS CrashLoopBackOff → the WHOLE cluster loses
        name resolution. Edit it carefully; keep a backup.
      • Using the wrong namespace in an FQDN resolves to nothing — `easypj-db`
        from another namespace must be `easypj-db.easypj`.
      • ndots:5 means names with fewer than 5 dots get search domains appended
        first; an external name like `api.example.com` may do extra lookups.
      • Scaling CoreDNS to 0 (or eviction during node pressure) silently breaks
        everything that resolves names.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Sanity check from any pod: `nslookup kubernetes.default` — if that fails,
        DNS itself is broken, not your Service.
      • Use a busybox:1.28 pod for nslookup (newer busybox has a flaky resolver).
      • `kubectl -n kube-system edit configmap coredns` then delete CoreDNS pods
        to reload (or it auto-reloads with the reload plugin).
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl -n kube-system get pods -l k8s-app=kube-dns -o wide
      kubectl -n kube-system get configmap coredns -o yaml      # the Corefile
      kubectl -n kube-system logs -l k8s-app=kube-dns
      kubectl run t --rm -it --image=busybox:1.28 -- nslookup easypj-db.easypj
      kubectl run t --rm -it --image=busybox:1.28 -- nslookup kubernetes.default
      kubectl -n kube-system rollout restart deploy coredns     # reload
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: from a temp pod, resolve easypj-db three ways (short, ns-qualified,
      FQDN). Then introduce a typo into the Corefile, watch CoreDNS CrashLoop and
      DNS break cluster-wide, and restore it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Give the FQDN of Service easypj-db in namespace easypj.
          A. `easypj-db.easypj.svc.cluster.local` — pattern <svc>.<ns>.svc.<clusterDomain>.
      Q2. easypj-web can't resolve easypj-db. How do you debug it step by step?
          A. nslookup kubernetes.default (is DNS up?) → nslookup easypj-db (right
          ns?) → check the Service exists and has endpoints → check CoreDNS pods/
          logs. Most failures are wrong namespace or CoreDNS down.
      Q3. Add an upstream forward for example.internal to CoreDNS. How?
          A. Edit the coredns ConfigMap, add a server block:
          `example.internal:53 { forward . 10.0.0.53 }`, save; CoreDNS reloads.
      Q4. CoreDNS is CrashLoopBackOff after an edit. Cause and recovery?
          A. The Corefile is invalid. `kubectl -n kube-system logs` shows the parse
          error; fix or restore the previous Corefile, then restart the pods.
      Q5. What DNS name does a headless Service hand back?
          A. The pod IPs of its ready members (A records), plus per-pod names like
          `postgres-0.easypj-db.easypj.svc.cluster.local` for StatefulSets.
      Q6. Does a normal pod get a DNS record? In what form?
          A. Optionally, as `<pod-ip-dashed>.<ns>.pod.cluster.local`
          (e.g. 10-244-1-5.easypj.pod.cluster.local). Services are the usual path.
      Q7. What is ndots:5 and why can it cause slow external lookups?
          A. Names with <5 dots get search domains appended and tried first, so an
          external FQDN may incur several failed cluster lookups before the real
          one. Use a trailing dot or dnsConfig to limit it.
      Q8. From namespace web, how do you address Service db in namespace data?
          A. `db.data` (or the full `db.data.svc.cluster.local`). A bare `db`
          would only resolve within namespace web.
      Q9. CoreDNS is overloaded on a large cluster. What do you do?
          A. Scale the CoreDNS Deployment up (more replicas) and/or deploy
          NodeLocal DNSCache to offload per-node lookups and cut latency.
      Q10. How do you confirm CoreDNS is healthy without exec-ing into it?
          A. Check its pods are Running/Ready, its health/ready plugins (8080/8181),
          and run `nslookup kubernetes.default` from a test pod.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: name -> CoreDNS -> ClusterIP -> pods.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 20: Ingress & Network Policies                               🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~3h · Domain: Services & Networking 20%.
  [B] WHY: Ingress is how the world reaches easypj over HTTP(S); NetworkPolicy
      is the firewall that protects the database. VISUAL: north-south +
      east-west traffic map.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: deploy an Ingress controller; write Ingress rules (host/path,
      TLS); write NetworkPolicies (default-deny, allow web->db).
  20.1 Ingress — HTTP/HTTPS routing 🎯 — host/path rules; pathType; TLS via a
       secret; ingressClassName.
       apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
       kind: Ingress
       metadata:
         name: easypj
       spec:
         ingressClassName: nginx
         tls:
         - hosts: [easypj.example.com]
           secretName: easypj-tls          # kubectl create secret tls easypj-tls ...
         rules:
         - host: easypj.example.com
           http:
             paths:
             - path: /jobs
               pathType: Prefix
               backend:
                 service:
                   name: easypj-web
                   port:
                     number: 80
  20.2 Ingress Controller Setup 🎯 — 📖 the rules need a controller (nginx);
       install + class. Install ingress-nginx (Helm or manifest), confirm its
       pods are Running in ingress-nginx ns, and that an IngressClass named
       `nginx` exists; only then does an Ingress object route traffic.
  20.3 Network Policies — pod firewall 🎯 — default allow-all until a policy
       selects a pod; podSelector + ingress/egress + from/to + ports.
       # 1) default-deny ALL ingress to every pod in the namespace
       apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
       kind: NetworkPolicy
       metadata:
         name: default-deny-ingress
         namespace: easypj
       spec:
         podSelector: {}                    # selects every pod
         policyTypes: [Ingress]             # no ingress rules = deny all inbound
       ---
       # 2) allow ONLY easypj-web -> easypj-db on 5432
       apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
       kind: NetworkPolicy
       metadata:
         name: allow-web-to-db
         namespace: easypj
       spec:
         podSelector:
           matchLabels: { app: easypj-db }
         policyTypes: [Ingress]
         ingress:
         - from:
           - podSelector:
               matchLabels: { app: easypj-web }
           ports:
           - protocol: TCP
             port: 5432
  20.4 Policy Types & Patterns 🎯 — ingress vs egress; default-deny; allow-DNS
       egress; namespace selectors. VISUAL: policy decision card.
  20.5 Testing Network Policies 🩺 — exec curl allowed vs blocked; ⚠️ needs a
       policy-aware CNI (Calico/Cilium, NOT Flannel).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 20
  [NORTH-SOUTH + EAST-WEST — traffic into easypj and locked down]
            🌍 internet
                │  HTTPS easypj.example.com/jobs
                ▼
      🌍 Ingress Controller (nginx)  ── reads Ingress rules
                │  host/path -> backend
                ▼
      🔌 easypj-web Service ─► 📦 web pods
                │  app traffic
                ▼  🧯 NetworkPolicy: ONLY easypj-web may reach db:5432
      🔌 easypj-db Service ─► 🐘 postgres
   ⚠️ Ingress needs a CONTROLLER or it does nothing; NetworkPolicy needs a
      policy-aware CNI (Calico/Cilium — NOT Flannel).

  [NETWORKPOLICY — default-deny then allow (the safe pattern)]
   1) default-deny: podSelector:{} , policyTypes:[Ingress]  ─► db rejects ALL
   2) allow web->db:
        to:   podSelector app=easypj-db
        from: podSelector app=easypj-web   ports: 5432
   3) ⚠️ don't forget egress allow-DNS (UDP/TCP 53 to kube-system) or names break.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a NetworkPolicy compiles to CNI rules (iptables/eBPF); why
      "deny" is implicit once any policy selects a pod; how Ingress controllers
      watch Ingress objects to program nginx.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): IngressClass & default class; pathType
      Exact/Prefix/ImplementationSpecific; NetworkPolicy endPort & ipBlock
      except; multiple policies on one pod = UNION; default-deny EGRESS patterns.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Ingress is L7 (HTTP/HTTPS) routing: host + path rules map to backend
        Services. The Ingress OBJECT is inert — an Ingress CONTROLLER (nginx,
        Traefik, etc.) must be installed to actually serve traffic.
      • ingressClassName ties an Ingress to a specific controller. TLS is a
        secret reference (type kubernetes.io/tls) named in spec.tls.
      • NetworkPolicy is the pod firewall. Pods are allow-all UNTIL a policy
        selects them; once selected, only what the policy permits is allowed
        (implicit deny). Multiple policies on a pod are a UNION.
      • NetworkPolicy needs a policy-aware CNI (Calico/Cilium). It has separate
        ingress and egress rules; the safe pattern is default-deny + explicit
        allows — and you must re-allow DNS egress or names stop resolving.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • No Ingress controller installed → the Ingress object exists but nothing
        routes. ADDRESS stays empty; requests time out.
      • Flannel ignores NetworkPolicy entirely — policies appear applied but do
        nothing. Verify your CNI before relying on them.
      • A default-deny ingress+egress policy breaks DNS — you MUST add an egress
        allow to kube-system on UDP/TCP 53, or every name lookup fails.
      • extensions/v1beta1 Ingress is removed — use networking.k8s.io/v1
        (backend shape changed to service.name/service.port).
      • pathType matters: Prefix vs Exact vs ImplementationSpecific route
        differently; omitting it is invalid in v1.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Don't hand-write policies — copy a known-good one from the docs and swap
        the podSelector/ports. Same for Ingress skeletons.
      • Test policies live: exec into an allowed pod and a disallowed pod and
        curl the target; one should succeed, one should hang/refuse.
      • `kubectl get ingress` ADDRESS column tells you if the controller has
        programmed it yet (empty = controller missing or not reconciled).
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get ingress easypj -o wide          # ADDRESS = controller wired up?
      kubectl describe ingress easypj             # rules, TLS, events
      kubectl -n ingress-nginx get pods           # is the controller running?
      kubectl create secret tls easypj-tls --cert=tls.crt --key=tls.key
      kubectl get networkpolicy -A
      kubectl describe networkpolicy default-deny
      kubectl exec web -- curl -m3 easypj-db:5432 ; kubectl exec other -- curl -m3 easypj-db:5432
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: install ingress-nginx, expose easypj-web via a host/path Ingress
      with TLS, then apply a default-deny on the db namespace plus an allow
      web→db:5432 and an allow-DNS egress; verify web reaches db and a third pod
      cannot.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Write an Ingress that routes easypj.example.com/jobs to easypj-web:80.
          A. networking.k8s.io/v1 Ingress, ingressClassName: nginx, rule host
          easypj.example.com, http.paths: path /jobs, pathType: Prefix, backend
          service.name easypj-web service.port.number 80.
      Q2. How do you add TLS to that Ingress?
          A. Create a tls secret (`kubectl create secret tls easypj-tls
          --cert=... --key=...`) and add spec.tls: [{hosts: [easypj.example.com],
          secretName: easypj-tls}].
      Q3. You applied an Ingress but nothing is reachable. First thing to check?
          A. Whether an Ingress CONTROLLER is installed and running (e.g.
          ingress-nginx). Without it the Ingress object does nothing; ADDRESS
          stays empty.
      Q4. Write a default-deny ingress policy for namespace easypj.
          A. NetworkPolicy with podSelector: {} (all pods) and
          policyTypes: [Ingress], no ingress rules → all inbound denied.
      Q5. Allow ONLY easypj-web to reach easypj-db on 5432.
          A. Policy on podSelector app=easypj-db, ingress.from podSelector
          app=easypj-web, ports: [{protocol: TCP, port: 5432}].
      Q6. After a default-deny egress, DNS breaks. Fix?
          A. Add an egress rule allowing UDP and TCP port 53 to the kube-system
          namespace (CoreDNS), or names won't resolve.
      Q7. Two policies select the same pod. How do they combine?
          A. As a UNION — traffic is allowed if ANY selecting policy permits it.
          There's no ordering or deny-override.
      Q8. Allow traffic from an entire namespace (not specific pods). How?
          A. Use a namespaceSelector (matching the namespace's labels) in the
          from/to block instead of (or combined with) a podSelector.
      Q9. Your NetworkPolicy seems ignored — pods still talk freely. Why?
          A. The CNI isn't policy-aware (e.g. Flannel). Switch to Calico/Cilium;
          NetworkPolicy is only enforced by a CNI that implements it.
      Q10. How do you prove a policy works without external tools?
          A. Exec into an allowed pod and curl the target (succeeds), then exec
          into a non-selected pod and curl the same target (times out/refused).
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: traffic entering easypj, firewalled to the db.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 20b: Gateway API — Modern Ingress (2025)                     🟡 🎯 🆕
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Services & Networking 20% (NEW competency).
  [B] WHY: Gateway API is the successor to Ingress and is now on the exam. It
      separates infra (Gateway) from routing (HTTPRoute) by role. VISUAL:
      Ingress vs Gateway API comparison.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain the resource model; deploy a Gateway; route easypj
      with HTTPRoute; compare to Ingress.
  20b.1 Why Gateway API 🎯 — Ingress limits (annotations sprawl, single role);
        role-oriented design. VISUAL: Ingress-vs-Gateway table.
  20b.2 The Resource Model 🎯 — GatewayClass (infra type) -> Gateway (listener/
        IP, platform team) -> HTTPRoute (rules, app team) -> Service. VISUAL:
        layered resource diagram.
  20b.3 Routing easypj 🎯 — header/path/weight matching; traffic splitting.
        apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
        kind: Gateway
        metadata:
          name: easypj-gw
        spec:
          gatewayClassName: nginx          # which controller implements it
          listeners:
          - name: http
            protocol: HTTP
            port: 80
        ---
        apiVersion: gateway.networking.k8s.io/v1
        kind: HTTPRoute
        metadata:
          name: easypj-route
        spec:
          parentRefs:
          - name: easypj-gw                # attach to the Gateway listener
          hostnames: ["easypj.example.com"]
          rules:
          - matches:
            - path:
                type: PathPrefix
                value: /jobs
            backendRefs:
            - name: easypj-web
              port: 80
  20b.4 Ingress vs Gateway API 🧭 — when to use which; migration notes.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 20b
  [GATEWAY API — roles split across resources (vs one Ingress blob)]
   🛠️ Cluster ops   ──►  GatewayClass   (which controller/infra type)
                              │ implemented by
   🏗️ Platform team ──►  Gateway        (listeners: :80/:443, IP, TLS)
                              │ routes attach to a listener
   👩‍💻 App team      ──►  HTTPRoute      (host/path/header/weight rules)
                              │ backendRefs
                              ▼
                         🔌 easypj-web Service ─► 📦 pods
   [Ingress vs Gateway API]
   ┌───────────────┬───────────────────────┬──────────────────────────┐
   │ Ingress       │ one object + annotations│ single role, limited     │
   │ Gateway API   │ GatewayClass/Gateway/   │ role-separated, portable,│
   │               │ HTTPRoute               │ header/weight/traffic-split│
   └───────────────┴───────────────────────┴──────────────────────────┘

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a controller (e.g. nginx-gateway, Envoy Gateway) reconciles
      GatewayClass/Gateway/HTTPRoute; status conditions & attachment.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): listener TLS modes (Terminate/Passthrough);
      ReferenceGrant for cross-namespace routes; route precedence; GRPCRoute/
      TLSRoute; policy attachment; status Accepted/Programmed conditions.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Gateway API is the role-oriented successor to Ingress. It splits one
        Ingress blob into three resources owned by different teams: GatewayClass
        (cluster ops — which controller), Gateway (platform team — listeners/IP/
        TLS), HTTPRoute (app team — host/path/header/weight rules).
      • Routes ATTACH to a Gateway listener via parentRefs; backends are
        referenced by backendRefs (Services). Status conditions (Accepted,
        Programmed) tell you whether attachment succeeded.
      • It's portable (a real K8s API, not nginx annotations) and natively
        supports header matching, weighted traffic splitting, and GRPC/TLS routes.
      • Gateway API ships as CRDs you must install; it is now an exam competency.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • The Gateway API CRDs are NOT built in — install them first or every
        Gateway/HTTPRoute apply fails with "no matches for kind".
      • The gatewayClassName must match a running controller (e.g. nginx-gateway,
        Envoy Gateway); otherwise the Gateway never becomes Programmed.
      • An HTTPRoute with no valid parentRef (wrong Gateway name/namespace) stays
        unattached and silently routes nothing — check its status conditions.
      • Cross-namespace routing requires a ReferenceGrant; without it the
        attachment is refused.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl get gateway easypj-gw -o yaml` → look at status.conditions for
        Accepted/Programmed before debugging anything else.
      • `kubectl describe httproute easypj-route` shows ParentRef attachment
        status — the fastest way to spot an unattached route.
      • Keep the Gateway (platform) and HTTPRoute (app) in separate files so you
        can hand each to the right owner.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get gatewayclass
      kubectl get gateway easypj-gw -o wide
      kubectl get httproute easypj-route -o wide
      kubectl describe httproute easypj-route        # attachment + conditions
      kubectl apply -f https://github.com/kubernetes-sigs/gateway-api/.../crds.yaml
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: install the Gateway API CRDs and a controller, create a Gateway
      with an HTTP :80 listener and an HTTPRoute sending /jobs to easypj-web,
      then confirm the Gateway is Programmed and the route Accepted.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Map the three core Gateway API resources to their owning roles.
          A. GatewayClass = cluster ops (selects the controller/infra). Gateway =
          platform team (listeners, IP, TLS). HTTPRoute = app team (host/path/
          header/weight routing rules).
      Q2. Create a Gateway with an HTTP listener on port 80.
          A. kind Gateway, gatewayClassName: <controller>, listeners: [{name: http,
          protocol: HTTP, port: 80}].
      Q3. Route easypj.example.com/jobs to easypj-web:80 with an HTTPRoute.
          A. HTTPRoute parentRefs: [easypj-gw], hostnames: [easypj.example.com],
          rules.matches path PathPrefix /jobs, backendRefs: [{name: easypj-web,
          port: 80}].
      Q4. How do you match on an HTTP header instead of a path?
          A. In rules.matches add headers: [{name: X-Env, value: canary}] — only
          requests with that header take the rule's backend.
      Q5. Split traffic 90/10 between easypj-web v1 and v2.
          A. One rule with two backendRefs, each given a weight (90 and 10);
          the controller load-balances proportionally.
      Q6. When would you still pick Ingress over Gateway API?
          A. When a needed controller only speaks Ingress, the cluster lacks the
          CRDs, or the routing is trivial single-role. New designs lean Gateway API.
      Q7. `kubectl apply` of a Gateway returns "no matches for kind Gateway". Fix?
          A. The Gateway API CRDs aren't installed. Apply the CRD bundle first,
          then re-apply the Gateway.
      Q8. Your HTTPRoute routes nothing. How do you diagnose it?
          A. `kubectl describe httproute` and check status: parentRef Accepted? If
          unattached, the parentRefs name/namespace or listener is wrong, or a
          ReferenceGrant is missing for cross-namespace.
      Q9. Terminate TLS on a Gateway listener — what's required?
          A. A listener with protocol: HTTPS, port 443, tls.mode: Terminate, and
          certificateRefs pointing at a TLS secret.
      Q10. An HTTPRoute in namespace app targets a Service in namespace data.
           What extra object is needed?
          A. A ReferenceGrant in the data namespace permitting HTTPRoutes from
          namespace app to reference its Services.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: GatewayClass -> Gateway -> HTTPRoute -> easypj Service.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 5 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Flat network: every pod a unique IP, no NAT pod-to-pod; CNI implements│
  │    it; kube-proxy turns Service VIPs into pod endpoints.                 │
  │ ✅ Services give stable VIP+DNS; empty endpoints = selector/readiness    │
  │    problem; headless Services back StatefulSets.                         │
  │ ✅ CoreDNS resolves <svc>.<ns>.svc.cluster.local; most "can't connect"   │
  │    bugs are DNS.                                                         │
  │ ✅ Ingress + a controller do L7 routing; NetworkPolicy firewalls pods    │
  │    (needs a policy-aware CNI); Gateway API is the modern, role-based      │
  │    successor (now on the exam).                                          │
  │ 🎯 EXAM 20%: expose, NetworkPolicy, CoreDNS, and Gateway API are staples.│
  │ 🔑 expose · get ep/endpointslice · get ingress/gateway/httproute · nslookup│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 5 VISUAL SUMMARY]: the complete easypj traffic map, internet -> db.


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 6: STORAGE                                                         🟡 🎯
  Chapters 21-23  ·  CKA DOMAIN WEIGHT: 10%  ·  "Don't lose the data"
  What you'll build: persistent, dynamically-provisioned storage for easypj's
  PostgreSQL, plus clean config/secret injection — data that survives pod
  death, rescheduling, and rollouts.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 6 INTRO]
  Containers are ephemeral — kill a pod and its writable layer is gone. That's
  fine for stateless easypj-web, fatal for the database. This part is the third
  plug point (CSI): how Kubernetes attaches durable storage via the PV/PVC
  model and StorageClasses (dynamic provisioning), plus how ConfigMaps and
  Secrets feed configuration into easypj cleanly.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 6 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch21 VOLUMES (why & types) ─▶ Ch22 PV/PVC/StorageClass (durable, dynamic)│
  │                                          │                              │
  │                                          ▼                              │
  │                 Ch23 CONFIGMAPS & SECRETS (configuration, not bytes)     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 6 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: see easypj lose data on restart -> attach an emptyDir
  (still ephemeral) -> understand the PV/PVC contract -> a StorageClass
  dynamically provisions a PVC for postgres -> the StatefulSet mounts it ->
  config via ConfigMap, credentials via Secret (encrypted at rest). VISUAL:
  the PV->PVC->Pod binding chain (reuse+augment cka_1).
  📖 EXTENSION INTERFACE recap: CSI = the storage plug point (drivers provision
  & attach real disks).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 21: Volumes & Persistent Storage                             🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Storage 10% (volume types/access/reclaim).
  [B] WHY: easypj uploads & db files vanish on restart without volumes. VISUAL:
      ephemeral-storage problem panel.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain ephemeral storage; the volume-type spectrum; use
      emptyDir & hostPath; understand CSI.
  21.1 The Ephemeral Problem 🎯 — writable layer dies with the pod. VISUAL:
       restart loses data.
  21.2 Volume Types Overview 🎯 — emptyDir/hostPath/configMap/secret/PVC/CSI/
       nfs; lifecycle spectrum (pod-bound -> node-bound -> cluster-durable).
       VISUAL: lifecycle spectrum bar.
  21.3 emptyDir 🎯 — temporary shared scratch (sidecar pattern); medium: Memory.
  21.4 hostPath 🎯 — node filesystem; types (Directory/File/DirectoryOrCreate);
       ⚠️ dev/node-agent only, not for app data.
  21.5 CSI & the Storage Plug Point 🎯🏢 — 📖 CSI drivers provision/attach real
       storage; how it replaced in-tree volume plugins.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 21
  [VOLUME LIFECYCLE SPECTRUM — how long does the data live?]
   container-only      pod-scoped         node-scoped      cluster-durable
   (writable layer) ◄─ emptyDir ─►    ◄─ hostPath ─►   ◄─ PVC ─► PV ─►
   dies with         dies with the      tied to ONE      survives pod delete,
   CONTAINER         POD (survives a     node             reschedule & scaling
                     container restart)
   📖 configMap/secret volumes = read-only config files · CSI = real disks.
   easypj: web = stateless (no PVC)   ·   🐘 postgres = PVC (MUST persist!).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the kubelet mounts a volume into a container's mount
      namespace; CSI attach/mount/publish stages.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): projected volumes (cm+secret+token+downwardAPI);
      ephemeral CSI volumes; subPath/subPathExpr; mountPropagation;
      fsGroupChangePolicy; readOnly mounts.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • A container's writable layer is ephemeral — it dies with the CONTAINER.
        Volumes attach storage with longer lifecycles outside that layer.
      • The lifecycle spectrum: emptyDir (lives with the POD, survives container
        restarts) → hostPath (lives on ONE node) → PVC/PV (cluster-durable,
        survives pod delete/reschedule/scale).
      • A `volume` is declared at pod level (the source); a `volumeMount` places
        it at a path inside a container. Many containers can mount the same
        volume — that's how sidecars share files.
      • CSI is the storage extension interface: out-of-tree drivers provision and
        attach real disks (EBS/PD/Ceph), replacing the old in-tree plugins.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • emptyDir survives a container crash/restart but is DELETED when the pod is
        removed or rescheduled — it is not durable storage.
      • hostPath pins a pod to whichever node holds that path and exposes the host
        filesystem — fine for node agents/dev, dangerous for app data.
      • The wrong hostPath `type` (e.g. Directory when the path doesn't exist)
        keeps the pod stuck in ContainerCreating; use DirectoryOrCreate when unsure.
      • medium: Memory emptyDir counts against the pod's memory limit and is lost
        on reboot.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • For a quick scratch share between containers, an emptyDir is one block of
        YAML — no PVC, no provisioner.
      • `kubectl explain pod.spec.volumes` lists every volume source if you forget
        the field name in the exam.
      • Mount the same volume readOnly in consumers and read-write only where
        writes happen, to avoid accidental corruption.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl explain pod.spec.volumes
      kubectl explain pod.spec.containers.volumeMounts
      kubectl exec -it web -c sidecar -- ls /shared
      kubectl get pod web -o jsonpath='{.spec.volumes}'
      kubectl describe pod web | sed -n '/Volumes/,/Events/p'
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: build a pod with two containers sharing one emptyDir — a writer
      that appends to /shared/out.log and a reader that tails it — and prove the
      file is visible to both.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Share a single file between two containers in one pod. How?
          A. Declare an emptyDir volume at pod level and mount it (same name) into
          both containers' volumeMounts at chosen paths; both see the same files.
      Q2. Does an emptyDir survive a container crash? A pod deletion?
          A. It survives a container restart (it's pod-scoped) but is destroyed
          when the pod is deleted or rescheduled to another node.
      Q3. Mount the node's /var/log into a pod. Which volume type?
          A. hostPath with path /var/log. Use it only for node-level agents; it
          ties the pod to that node and exposes host files.
      Q4. What does the hostPath `type` field guard against?
          A. It validates/creates the target: Directory (must exist), File,
          DirectoryOrCreate (create if missing), Socket, etc. Wrong type =
          ContainerCreating failure.
      Q5. Create an in-memory scratch volume. How and what's the cost?
          A. emptyDir with `medium: Memory` (tmpfs). It's fast but counts against
          the pod's memory and is lost on node reboot.
      Q6. easypj-web restarts and the uploaded file is gone. Why and fix?
          A. It was on the container writable layer (or an emptyDir on a
          rescheduled pod). Use a PVC for anything that must persist.
      Q7. What problem does CSI solve?
          A. It lets storage vendors ship drivers out-of-tree that provision and
          attach real volumes, instead of baking every backend into Kubernetes.
      Q8. Difference between a volume and a volumeMount?
          A. The volume (pod.spec.volumes) defines the storage source; the
          volumeMount (container) maps it to a path inside that container.
      Q9. Can one volume be mounted into several containers at once?
          A. Yes — reference the same volume name in each container's
          volumeMounts; that's the basis of sidecar log/file sharing.
      Q10. How do you mount a volume read-only into a container?
          A. Set readOnly: true on that container's volumeMount, so the container
          can read but not modify the shared data.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the volume lifecycle spectrum.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 22: PersistentVolumes, PVCs & StorageClasses                 🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Storage 10% (PV/PVC, dynamic provisioning).
  [B] WHY: this is how easypj's database gets durable, on-demand disk. VISUAL:
      PV/PVC separation-of-concerns (admin vs developer).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: explain the PV/PVC model; create PVs/PVCs; access modes &
      reclaim policies; StorageClasses & dynamic provisioning; give postgres a
      PVC.
  22.1 The PV/PVC Model 🎯 — admin offers PV, dev claims PVC, K8s binds; status
       Available->Bound->Released. VISUAL: separation-of-concerns.
  22.2 Creating PVs 🎯 — capacity; accessModes (RWO/ROX/RWX/RWOP); reclaimPolicy
       (Retain/Delete/Recycle); backend. VISUAL: access-modes table (⚠️ about
       NODES not pods).
  22.3 Creating PVCs 🎯 — must match accessModes + storageClassName + size<=PV.
  22.4 Using PVCs in Pods 🎯 — persistentVolumeClaim.claimName in volumes. VISUAL:
       reuse+augment cka_1 PV->PVC->Pod chain.
  22.5 StorageClasses & Dynamic Provisioning 🎯 — provisioner; volumeBindingMode
       (Immediate vs WaitForFirstConsumer); reclaimPolicy; allowVolumeExpansion;
       default class.
       apiVersion: storage.k8s.io/v1
       kind: StorageClass
       metadata:
         name: easypj-fast
         annotations:
           storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class: "true"
       provisioner: ebs.csi.aws.com        # the CSI driver that creates disks
       parameters:
         type: gp3
       reclaimPolicy: Retain                # keep the disk when the PVC is deleted
       allowVolumeExpansion: true           # PVCs may be grown later
       volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer   # bind once a pod is scheduled
       # A PVC with storageClassName: easypj-fast now auto-creates a matching PV.
  22.6 PostgreSQL Storage for easypj 🧪 — StatefulSet volumeClaimTemplates;
       Retain policy; data survives restart/reschedule/scale. VISUAL: reuse+
       augment cka_1 postgres storage architecture.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 22
  [THE PV / PVC / StorageClass CHAIN — dynamic provisioning for easypj's db]
   👩‍💻 Developer                       🤖 Kubernetes              🛠️ Admin
   ┌──────────────┐  binds to   ┌──────────────┐  provisioned  ┌──────────┐
   │ PVC          │◄───────────►│ PV (10Gi,    │◄──────────────│ 🧱 Storage│
   │ request 10Gi │  if class & │ RWO, Retain) │  on-demand by │ Class    │
   │ RWO, class=  │  size match │              │  the class    │ (provisn)│
   │ easypj-fast  │             └──────┬───────┘               └──────────┘
   └──────┬───────┘                    │ real disk (CSI: EBS/PD/Ceph)
          │ mounted into                ▼
   📦 postgres-0 ─ volumeMounts ─► /var/lib/postgresql/data  🐘
   Status flow:  PVC Pending ─► Bound ─► (PV) Released (after PVC delete)
   ⚠️ PVC stuck Pending = no matching PV / wrong storageClassName / no provisioner.
   Access modes: RWO (one node) · ROX (many read) · RWX (many r/w) · RWOP (one pod)

  [G] 🔧 UTH: the binding controller; WaitForFirstConsumer & topology-aware
      provisioning; how expansion works online.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): volumeMode Block vs Filesystem; CSI VolumeSnapshots
      & clones (snapshot/restore a PVC); online volume expansion; zonal binding
      with WaitForFirstConsumer; RWX via NFS/CephFS; fsGroup/fsGroupChangePolicy
      & file permissions; reclaiming Released PVs; ephemeral CSI volumes;
      mountOptions & subPath pitfalls.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • PV/PVC is separation of concerns: an admin (or a StorageClass) supplies
        the PV (the actual storage); a developer files a PVC (a request). K8s
        binds them: PVC Pending → Bound; after the PVC is deleted the PV goes
        Released (and is cleaned up or kept per reclaimPolicy).
      • A StorageClass enables DYNAMIC provisioning — its provisioner creates a PV
        on demand the moment a matching PVC appears, so you never pre-create PVs.
      • Access modes describe NODES, not pods: RWO = one node read-write, ROX =
        many nodes read-only, RWX = many nodes read-write, RWOP = one pod.
      • reclaimPolicy controls what happens to the disk after the PVC is gone:
        Delete (destroy it) or Retain (keep it, manual cleanup). StatefulSets use
        volumeClaimTemplates to give each replica its own durable PVC.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • PVC stuck Pending = no matching PV, wrong storageClassName, no provisioner
        for that class, or (with WaitForFirstConsumer) no pod scheduled yet.
      • Access-mode mismatch: a PVC asking RWX bound to an RWO backend won't
        attach on multiple nodes. Most block storage is RWO only.
      • Retain leaves PVs in Released state that won't rebind until you manually
        clear claimRef — silent capacity leak.
      • Editing a PVC's size only works if allowVolumeExpansion: true on the class
        (and you can only grow, never shrink).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl get pv,pvc,sc` in one shot shows binding state across all three.
      • describe a Pending PVC first — its Events line usually names the exact
        reason (no provisioner / waiting for consumer / no matching PV).
      • `kubectl get sc` — the one marked (default) is what PVCs without a
        storageClassName use.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get pv,pvc,sc -o wide
      kubectl describe pvc easypj-db-data          # Events explain Pending
      kubectl get sc                               # which is (default)?
      kubectl patch pvc easypj-db-data -p '{"spec":{"resources":{"requests":{"storage":"20Gi"}}}}'
      kubectl get pv -o jsonpath='{range .items[*]}{.metadata.name}{" "}{.status.phase}{"\n"}{end}'
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create a StorageClass-backed PVC, mount it in a pod, write a
      file, delete the pod, recreate it bound to the same PVC, and confirm the
      file survived. Then make the PVC Pending (wrong class) and debug it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Create a static PV, a matching PVC, and a pod that mounts it.
          A. PV with capacity/accessModes/storageClassName; PVC requesting the
          same class, ≤ capacity, compatible accessModes; pod volumes with
          persistentVolumeClaim.claimName → volumeMount.
      Q2. What is dynamic provisioning and what triggers it?
          A. A StorageClass with a provisioner auto-creates a PV when a PVC
          referencing that class is created — no admin pre-provisioning needed.
      Q3. Explain RWO vs ROX vs RWX vs RWOP.
          A. RWO = read-write by one node; ROX = read-only by many nodes; RWX =
          read-write by many nodes; RWOP = read-write by a single pod. Modes are
          node-scoped (except RWOP).
      Q4. reclaimPolicy Delete vs Retain — what happens to the disk?
          A. Delete destroys the underlying volume when the PVC is removed; Retain
          keeps it (PV → Released) for manual recovery/cleanup.
      Q5. A PVC is stuck Pending. List the likely causes.
          A. No PV matches (size/accessModes/class), wrong storageClassName, no
          provisioner for the class, or WaitForFirstConsumer waiting on a pod.
      Q6. How does a PVC bind to a specific PV?
          A. The binder matches storageClassName, requested ≤ capacity, and
          compatible accessModes; you can force it with selectors/volumeName.
      Q7. Grow an existing PVC from 10Gi to 20Gi. Requirements?
          A. The StorageClass must have allowVolumeExpansion: true; patch the
          PVC's requests.storage upward (shrinking is not allowed).
      Q8. How do you set/identify the default StorageClass?
          A. The class with annotation storageclass.kubernetes.io/is-default-class:
          "true" (shown as (default) in `kubectl get sc`) is used by PVCs with no
          storageClassName.
      Q9. Why use volumeBindingMode: WaitForFirstConsumer?
          A. It delays PV creation/binding until a pod is scheduled, so the volume
          is provisioned in the same zone/topology as the pod (avoids cross-zone
          attach failures).
      Q10. Give postgres durable per-replica storage in a StatefulSet.
           A. Use volumeClaimTemplates in the StatefulSet — each replica gets its
           own PVC (e.g. data-postgres-0) bound to its own PV that persists across
           restarts and rescheduling.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: StorageClass -> dynamic PV -> PVC -> postgres pod.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 23: ConfigMaps & Secrets                                     🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Official Workloads competency (config apps).
  [B] WHY: easypj's settings (DB_HOST, ALLOWED_HOSTS) and credentials
      (SECRET_KEY, DB_PASSWORD) must live OUTSIDE the image. VISUAL: ConfigMap
      vs Secret card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: create & consume ConfigMaps and Secrets (env, envFrom,
      volume); secret types; base64 ≠ encryption; immutability.
  23.1 ConfigMaps 🎯 — create (literal/file/env-file); consume (env, envFrom,
       volume); volume updates auto, env does not.
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: ConfigMap
       metadata:
         name: easypj-config
       data:
         DEBUG: "False"
         ALLOWED_HOSTS: "easypj.example.com,localhost"
         DB_HOST: "easypj-db"
         DB_NAME: "easypj"
       # kubectl create configmap easypj-config --from-literal=DEBUG=False \
       #   --from-literal=DB_HOST=easypj-db   (or --from-file / --from-env-file)
  23.2 Secrets 🎯 — 📖 base64 ENCODING not encryption; types (generic/tls/
       docker-registry); stringData vs data; defaultMode.
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: Secret
       metadata:
         name: easypj-secret
       type: Opaque
       stringData:                          # plaintext in; K8s base64-encodes it
         SECRET_KEY: "django-insecure-CHANGE-ME"
         DB_PASSWORD: "s3cr3t-pg-pass"
       # kubectl create secret generic easypj-secret \
       #   --from-literal=SECRET_KEY=... --from-literal=DB_PASSWORD=...
       # base64 is ENCODING, not encryption — enable encryption-at-rest (Ch 11).
  23.3 Best Practices 🏢 — encryption at rest (link Ch 11/Ch 39); RBAC on
       secrets; prefer volume mounts over env; external stores (Vault/External
       Secrets) as 🏢 notes.
  23.4 Immutable ConfigMaps & Secrets 🎯 — immutable: true; perf + safety;
       versioning strategy (config-v2).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 23
  [3 WAYS TO INJECT CONFIG INTO easypj]
   ⚙️ ConfigMap easypj-config   /   🔒 Secret easypj-secret
        │
        ├─ envFrom ─────────► ALL keys become env vars  (DB_HOST, DEBUG, ...)
        ├─ env+valueFrom.*KeyRef ► ONE key, can rename it
        └─ volume mount ────► files at /etc/easypj/...   (hot-reloads ~60s)
   🔁 reload: volume FILES auto-update · ENV VARS do NOT (must restart the pod).
   ⚠️ Secret = base64 ENCODING, not encryption -> turn on encryption at rest.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how envFrom/volume projection works; why mounted files hot-reload
      (~60s) but env vars need a pod restart; subPath caveat.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): immutable cm/secret performance; size limit (1MiB);
      binaryData; secret types (tls/dockercfg/bootstrap); envFrom prefix; how
      the kubelet caches secrets; sealed-secrets / external-secrets rotation.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • ConfigMaps hold non-secret config; Secrets hold sensitive values. Both
        keep configuration OUT of the image so one image runs in every environment.
      • Three injection styles: env (one key, can rename via valueFrom), envFrom
        (all keys become env vars), volume mount (keys become files).
      • Volume-mounted keys hot-reload (~60s); env vars are fixed at pod start and
        only change on a pod restart.
      • Secret data is base64 ENCODING, not encryption. Anyone with read access
        (or etcd access) can decode it — enable encryption-at-rest and lock down
        RBAC on secrets.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Putting plaintext under `data:` fails — `data` must be base64; use
        `stringData` to supply plaintext and let K8s encode it.
      • Changing a ConfigMap/Secret does NOT restart pods; env-var consumers keep
        the old value until the pod restarts (`kubectl rollout restart`).
      • subPath volume mounts do NOT receive live updates — they're copied once.
      • By default secrets sit unencrypted in etcd; a backup or etcd read leaks
        them. Turn on EncryptionConfiguration.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Imperative create is fastest: `kubectl create configmap easypj-config
        --from-literal=DEBUG=False` / `kubectl create secret generic easypj-secret
        --from-literal=DB_PASSWORD=...`.
      • Generate then edit: add `--dry-run=client -o yaml` to any create command
        to get a YAML skeleton.
      • Mark config immutable (`immutable: true`) for hot paths — fewer API watches
        and protection against accidental edits.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl create configmap easypj-config --from-literal=DEBUG=False --from-literal=DB_HOST=easypj-db
      kubectl create secret generic easypj-secret --from-literal=DB_PASSWORD=s3cr3t
      kubectl get secret easypj-secret -o jsonpath='{.data.DB_PASSWORD}' | base64 -d
      kubectl create secret tls easypj-tls --cert=tls.crt --key=tls.key
      kubectl create secret docker-registry regcred --docker-server=... --docker-username=... --docker-password=...
      kubectl rollout restart deploy easypj-web        # pick up new env values
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: inject easypj config three ways into a pod — one env var via
      valueFrom, all keys via envFrom, and the Secret as files under
      /etc/easypj — then exec in and verify each is present.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Create a ConfigMap three different ways.
          A. --from-literal=K=V (inline), --from-file=app.conf (file→key), and
          --from-env-file=app.env (each line a key). All produce the same kind.
      Q2. Difference between envFrom and env+valueFrom?
          A. envFrom imports ALL keys of a cm/secret as env vars; env+valueFrom.
          configMapKeyRef/secretKeyRef pulls ONE key and lets you rename it.
      Q3. Mount easypj-config as files. Where do the keys appear?
          A. As a volume → each key becomes a file under the mountPath
          (e.g. /etc/easypj/DB_HOST containing the value).
      Q4. Create a generic Secret with DB_PASSWORD.
          A. `kubectl create secret generic easypj-secret
          --from-literal=DB_PASSWORD=...` (type Opaque, value base64-encoded).
      Q5. Create a TLS secret for Ingress.
          A. `kubectl create secret tls easypj-tls --cert=tls.crt --key=tls.key`
          (type kubernetes.io/tls).
      Q6. Is a Secret encrypted? Justify.
          A. No by default — values are base64-encoded and stored in etcd in the
          clear. Real protection needs encryption-at-rest + RBAC restrictions.
      Q7. What does immutable: true buy you on a ConfigMap?
          A. The cm/secret can't be updated (only replaced), which reduces kubelet
          watch load and prevents accidental live edits.
      Q8. You edited a ConfigMap but the app still sees the old value. Why?
          A. The app reads it as an env var, which is fixed at pod start. Restart
          the pods (rollout restart) — or mount it as a file for hot-reload.
      Q9. Pull a private image — what Secret type and wiring?
          A. A docker-registry secret referenced via imagePullSecrets on the pod/
          ServiceAccount.
      Q10. Rotate DB_PASSWORD with minimal disruption.
           A. Update the Secret, then trigger a rolling restart of the consumers
           (rollout restart) so new pods pick up the new value; or use a
           file-mounted secret that hot-reloads if the app re-reads it.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: ConfigMap+Secret -> easypj pod (env + files).

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 6 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Containers are ephemeral; volumes add persistence. emptyDir = pod-     │
  │    scratch, hostPath = node-only, PVC = durable cluster storage.         │
  │ ✅ PV/PVC separates admin (offers) from dev (claims); StorageClasses do  │
  │    DYNAMIC provisioning; access modes are about nodes, reclaim controls  │
  │    cleanup. CSI is the storage plug point.                              │
  │ ✅ ConfigMaps (non-secret) & Secrets (base64, encrypt at rest) inject    │
  │    config; volume mounts hot-reload, env vars don't.                    │
  │ 🎯 EXAM 10%: dynamic provisioning + PVC-Pending debugging are classic.   │
  │ 🔑 get pv,pvc · get sc · create cm/secret · describe pvc                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 6 VISUAL SUMMARY]: easypj's durable data + clean config, one diagram.


━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 7: SECURITY — WHO CAN DO WHAT                                      🟡 🎯
  Chapters 24-27  ·  RBAC is a Cluster-Arch competency (25%)  ·  "Lock it down"
  What you'll build: least-privilege access for easypj's team & pods, a clear
  picture of the cluster's certificate web, and pods that run as non-root
  under enforced Pod Security Standards.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 7 INTRO]
  Security in Kubernetes answers two questions: WHO are you (authentication via
  certificates / ServiceAccount tokens) and WHAT may you do (authorization via
  RBAC). On top sits workload security — what a pod itself is allowed to do.
  This part makes easypj's humans and pods least-privileged by default.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 7 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WHO ARE YOU?                         WHAT CAN YOU DO?                   │
  │  Ch26 TLS/PKI (humans) ─┐    ┌─▶ Ch24 RBAC (Roles/Bindings)             │
  │  Ch25 ServiceAccounts ──┴────┘                                          │
  │                                  └─▶ Ch27 POD SECURITY (what pods may do)│
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 7 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: an easypj-web ServiceAccount with a tight Role (read
  its own ConfigMap only) -> a developer kubeconfig signed via a CSR -> Pod
  Security Admission set to "restricted" on the easypj namespace -> easypj pods
  run non-root, read-only-rootfs, no extra capabilities. VISUAL: the AuthN ->
  AuthZ -> Admission request pipeline (reuse+augment cka_1).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 24: RBAC — Role-Based Access Control                         🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% (RBAC). EXAM STAPLE.
  [B] WHY: every "Forbidden" error and every access grant is RBAC. VISUAL:
      WHO(subject)->BINDING->WHAT(role) chain.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: the 4 RBAC objects; Role vs ClusterRole; RoleBinding vs
      ClusterRoleBinding; create & test; least privilege.
  24.1 What is RBAC & Why 🎯 — authorization after authN; deny by default.
  24.2 The Four Objects 🎯 — Role / ClusterRole (permissions) + RoleBinding /
       ClusterRoleBinding (grants). VISUAL: 2x2 scope matrix.
  24.3 Creating Roles & ClusterRoles 🎯 — apiGroups/resources/verbs; "" = core;
       resourceNames to scope to one object.
       apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
       kind: Role
       metadata:
         namespace: easypj
         name: easypj-role
       rules:
       - apiGroups: [""]                    # "" = the core API group
         resources: ["configmaps"]
         resourceNames: ["easypj-config"]   # scope to ONE object (optional)
         verbs: ["get", "list", "watch"]
       # imperative: kubectl create role easypj-role -n easypj \
       #   --verb=get,list,watch --resource=configmaps --resource-name=easypj-config
  24.4 Creating (Cluster)RoleBindings 🎯 — subjects (User/Group/ServiceAccount).
       apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1
       kind: RoleBinding
       metadata:
         namespace: easypj
         name: easypj-web-can-read-config
       subjects:
       - kind: ServiceAccount
         name: easypj-web-sa
         namespace: easypj
       roleRef:
         kind: Role
         name: easypj-role
         apiGroup: rbac.authorization.k8s.io
       # imperative: kubectl create rolebinding easypj-web-can-read-config -n easypj \
       #   --role=easypj-role --serviceaccount=easypj:easypj-web-sa
  24.5 Testing RBAC 🎯 — `kubectl auth can-i VERB RESOURCE --as=...
       [--as-group] [-n ns]`; auth can-i --list.
  24.6 RBAC for easypj 🧪 — practical least-privilege example.
  24.7 🧪 PRACTICE EXERCISES.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 24
  [RBAC — WHO ─► (BINDING) ─► WHAT]
   👤 WHO (subject)            🔗 BINDING                 📜 WHAT (rules)
   ┌──────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────────┐   ┌──────────────────┐
   │ User / Group     │   │ RoleBinding (ns)     │   │ Role (ns)        │
   │ ServiceAccount   │──►│  -or-                │──►│  -or-            │
   │ system:sa:ns:name│   │ ClusterRoleBinding   │   │ ClusterRole      │
   └──────────────────┘   └──────────────────────┘   │ apiGroups+        │
                                                      │ resources+verbs   │
                                                      └──────────────────┘
   Scope matrix:   Role+RoleBinding = one namespace
                   ClusterRole+ClusterRoleBinding = whole cluster
                   ClusterRole + RoleBinding = cluster perms, one namespace
   🔑 Test it:  kubectl auth can-i create deploy --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:web
   💡 Deny by default; permissions are the UNION of all bindings (no deny rules).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the authorizer evaluates rules (union of all bindings, no
      deny rules); aggregated ClusterRoles; the dangerous verbs (escalate/bind/
      impersonate).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): aggregated ClusterRole label selectors; the
      system:* default roles & system:masters; the authz chain (Node -> RBAC ->
      Webhook) & order; subresources in rules (pods/exec, pods/log, */status);
      non-resourceURLs; auditing effective perms (`auth can-i --list --as`);
      impersonation (--as/--as-group) for testing.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • RBAC is authorization (WHAT you may do) — it runs AFTER authentication
        (WHO you are) and denies by default. There are NO deny rules; access is
        the UNION of every binding that applies to you.
      • Four objects: Role and ClusterRole hold permissions (apiGroups +
        resources + verbs); RoleBinding and ClusterRoleBinding grant them to
        subjects (User / Group / ServiceAccount).
      • Scope matrix: Role+RoleBinding = one namespace; ClusterRole+
        ClusterRoleBinding = whole cluster; ClusterRole+RoleBinding = reuse a
        cluster-defined permission set within a single namespace.
      • Cluster-scoped resources (nodes, PVs, namespaces) and non-resource URLs
        can only be granted via a ClusterRole.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • A Role is always namespaced — it cannot grant access to cluster-scoped
        resources (nodes, PVs). Use a ClusterRole for those.
      • The core API group is "" (empty string), not "core" or "v1".
      • Wildcards (`*` verbs/resources) quietly grant far more than intended —
        prefer explicit least privilege.
      • Binding to the wrong subject kind/name (e.g. User vs ServiceAccount, or
        missing the system:serviceaccount prefix) silently grants nothing.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Generate fast: `kubectl create role ... --dry-run=client -o yaml` then
        edit; same for rolebinding with --serviceaccount=ns:name.
      • Always verify with `kubectl auth can-i VERB RESOURCE --as=<subject> -n ns`
        — and `--list` to dump everything a subject can do.
      • For a quick SA grant use the imperative `create rolebinding
        --role=... --serviceaccount=ns:sa`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl create role easypj-role -n easypj --verb=get,list,watch --resource=pods
      kubectl create rolebinding rb -n easypj --role=easypj-role --serviceaccount=easypj:easypj-web-sa
      kubectl auth can-i list pods --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl get rolebinding,clusterrolebinding -A -o wide | grep easypj
      kubectl describe clusterrole view
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create a ServiceAccount that can ONLY get/list/watch pods in the
      easypj namespace (Role + RoleBinding), then prove it with `auth can-i` for
      pods (allowed) and secrets (denied).
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Create a Role allowing read of pods in easypj and bind it to a SA.
          A. Role rules apiGroups:[""] resources:[pods] verbs:[get,list,watch];
          RoleBinding with roleRef→that Role and subject ServiceAccount easypj:sa.
      Q2. You need to grant read of NODES. Role or ClusterRole? Why?
          A. ClusterRole — nodes are cluster-scoped, so a namespaced Role can't
          express them. Grant cluster-wide with a ClusterRoleBinding.
      Q3. Bind the built-in `view` ClusterRole to SA easypj:web in one namespace.
          A. A RoleBinding (namespaced) whose roleRef is the ClusterRole `view`
          and subject is the ServiceAccount — cluster perms scoped to that ns.
      Q4. How do you test what a ServiceAccount can do?
          A. `kubectl auth can-i VERB RESOURCE --as=system:serviceaccount:ns:name
          -n ns`, and `auth can-i --list --as=...` for the full set.
      Q5. Restrict a Role to a single ConfigMap named easypj-config.
          A. Add resourceNames: ["easypj-config"] to the rule (note: list/watch
          can't be name-restricted, only get/update/delete/patch).
      Q6. What's the empty-string apiGroup for?
          A. "" is the CORE group (pods, services, configmaps, secrets, nodes).
          Named groups like apps, rbac.authorization.k8s.io are listed explicitly.
      Q7. A pod gets "Forbidden" calling the API. How do you fix it methodically?
          A. Identify its SA, run `auth can-i --as` to see the gap, then add a
          Role/ClusterRole + Binding granting exactly the missing verb/resource.
      Q8. What is an aggregated ClusterRole?
          A. A ClusterRole with aggregationRule label selectors; K8s auto-merges
          rules from other ClusterRoles carrying matching labels (e.g. how `edit`
          and `view` get extended).
      Q9. Grant a permission to everyone in group "easypj-devs".
          A. A binding with subject kind: Group, name: easypj-devs — RBAC matches
          the group claim from the user's authentication.
      Q10. How do you safely test a binding before handing it out?
           A. Impersonate: `kubectl auth can-i ... --as=<user> --as-group=<group>`
           to confirm the effective permissions without logging in as them.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: subject -> binding -> role -> allowed verbs.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 25: Service Accounts & API Access                            🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch / Workloads.
  [B] WHY: pods that call the API (controllers, easypj jobs) authenticate as a
      ServiceAccount. VISUAL: user vs ServiceAccount card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: SA vs user; default SA; create/use SAs; token projection;
      bind RBAC to SAs; disable auto-mount.
  25.1 User vs Service Accounts 🎯 — humans (external) vs pods (in-cluster).
  25.2 The Default SA 🎯 — every ns has one; auto-mounted token (⚠️ disable if
       unused).
  25.3 Creating & Using SAs 🎯 — `create sa`; spec.serviceAccountName.
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: ServiceAccount
       metadata:
         name: easypj-web-sa
         namespace: easypj
       automountServiceAccountToken: false   # only true if the pod calls the API
       ---
       apiVersion: apps/v1
       kind: Deployment
       metadata: { name: easypj-web, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         template:
           spec:
             serviceAccountName: easypj-web-sa   # pod runs as this identity
             containers:
             - name: web
               image: easypj:v1
       # kubectl create serviceaccount easypj-web-sa -n easypj
  25.4 Token Projection & TokenRequest 🎯🏢 — short-lived projected tokens vs
       legacy secret tokens; audience/expiry.
  25.5 Binding RBAC to SAs 🎯 — system:serviceaccount:<ns>:<name>.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 25
  [SERVICEACCOUNT — a pod's identity to the API]
   👤 User = a HUMAN (external; cert/OIDC) — NOT a K8s object
   🤖 ServiceAccount [N] = a POD's identity (in-cluster)
   📦 easypj-web pod  (serviceAccountName: easypj-web-sa)
        │ auto-mounted PROJECTED token at
        │ /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/token
        ▼
   📡 apiserver ── validates token ──► RBAC checks
        subject = system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa
   ⚠️ set automountServiceAccountToken:false unless the pod calls the API.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the projected token is mounted & rotated; how the apiserver
      validates it; bound vs legacy tokens.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): BoundServiceAccountTokenVolume; audience-scoped
      tokens & TokenRequest expiry; imagePullSecrets on a SA; the
      system:serviceaccounts groups; OIDC discovery for cloud trust (IRSA).
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Users are HUMANS (external; cert/OIDC, not K8s objects). ServiceAccounts
        are POD identities (real namespaced objects). Pods authenticate to the
        API as their SA.
      • Every namespace has a `default` SA, auto-mounted into every pod at
        /var/run/secrets/kubernetes.io/serviceaccount/. Disable the mount unless
        the pod actually calls the API.
      • Modern tokens are short-lived PROJECTED tokens (via TokenRequest), bound
        to the pod with an expiry and audience — not the old long-lived secret.
      • Bind RBAC to a SA using the subject name
        system:serviceaccount:<namespace>:<name>.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • The default SA is mounted everywhere; if you over-grant it, every pod
        inherits those rights. Keep it minimal.
      • Leaving automountServiceAccountToken on for pods that never call the API
        needlessly exposes a credential.
      • Binding a SA with the wrong prefix (forgetting system:serviceaccount: or
        the namespace) grants nothing — the subject won't match.
      • Deleting/recreating a SA referenced by running pods can break their API
        access on token refresh.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Create + attach in two commands: `kubectl create sa easypj-web-sa -n
        easypj` then set `serviceAccountName` (or `kubectl set serviceaccount
        deploy/easypj-web easypj-web-sa`).
      • Test exactly as the pod would: `kubectl auth can-i ... --as=system:
        serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa`.
      • Set automountServiceAccountToken: false on the SA to default-deny the
        mount for all pods using it.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl create serviceaccount easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl set serviceaccount deploy/easypj-web easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl auth can-i list pods --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl create token easypj-web-sa -n easypj            # short-lived token
      kubectl get sa easypj-web-sa -n easypj -o yaml
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create a SA, bind it a Role that can list pods, run a pod as that
      SA, exec in and curl the API with the mounted token (succeeds for pods,
      Forbidden for secrets), and confirm with `auth can-i --as`.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Create a ServiceAccount and run a Deployment as it.
          A. `kubectl create sa easypj-web-sa -n easypj`; set
          spec.template.spec.serviceAccountName: easypj-web-sa on the Deployment.
      Q2. A pod doesn't need API access. How do you reduce its risk?
          A. Set automountServiceAccountToken: false (on the SA or the pod) so no
          token is mounted.
      Q3. Grant a SA permission to read configmaps. Subject string?
          A. Bind a Role to subject kind ServiceAccount; the RBAC subject name is
          system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa.
      Q4. Why is the default SA a risk?
          A. It's mounted into every pod in the namespace, so any privilege you
          give it spreads to all those pods. Keep it unprivileged.
      Q5. What's a projected/bound token and why is it better?
          A. A short-lived token issued via TokenRequest, scoped to an audience
          and expiry and tied to the pod's lifetime — far safer than a static,
          never-expiring secret token.
      Q6. Difference between a User and a ServiceAccount?
          A. Users are external humans not stored in K8s (authenticated by cert/
          OIDC); ServiceAccounts are in-cluster API objects used by pods.
      Q7. What does a token's audience do?
          A. It restricts where the token is valid (e.g. the API server, or an
          external service), preventing token reuse across audiences.
      Q8. Test whether a SA can delete deployments.
          A. `kubectl auth can-i delete deployments
          --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa -n easypj`.
      Q9. A pod gets 403 from the API. SA-side fix?
          A. Its SA lacks RBAC — create a Role/ClusterRole with the needed verb/
          resource and bind it to system:serviceaccount:<ns>:<sa>.
      Q10. Pull from a private registry as a SA. How?
           A. Attach imagePullSecrets to the ServiceAccount; pods using that SA
           inherit the registry credentials automatically.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: pod -> SA token -> apiserver -> RBAC decision.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 26: TLS Certificates & PKI                                   🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25%.
  [B] WHY: the whole control plane talks over mutual TLS; expired certs break
      clusters; the exam tests CSR approval. VISUAL: the cluster PKI web.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: map the PKI; cert locations & purposes; create/approve CSRs;
      check expiry; renew certs.
  26.1 K8s PKI Overview 🎯 — cluster CA; client/server certs for every
       component. VISUAL: PKI web (CA -> apiserver, kubelet, etcd, admins).
  26.2 Certificate Locations 🎯 — /etc/kubernetes/pki/*; what each cert is for.
  26.3 Certificate Signing Requests (CSR) 🎯 — `openssl`/`kubectl certificate
       approve`; embed in a kubeconfig for a new user.
       # 1) generate a key + CSR (CN becomes the username, O the group)
       openssl genrsa -out dev.key 2048
       openssl req -new -key dev.key -out dev.csr -subj "/CN=dev/O=easypj-devs"
       # 2) submit it to the K8s certificates API
       cat <<EOF | kubectl apply -f -
       apiVersion: certificates.k8s.io/v1
       kind: CertificateSigningRequest
       metadata: { name: dev }
       spec:
         request: $(base64 -w0 dev.csr)
         signerName: kubernetes.io/kube-apiserver-client
         usages: ["client auth"]
       EOF
       # 3) approve and fetch the signed cert
       kubectl certificate approve dev
       kubectl get csr dev -o jsonpath='{.status.certificate}' | base64 -d > dev.crt
       # 4) wire dev.crt/dev.key into a kubeconfig (set-credentials/context)
  26.4 Checking Expiry 🎯 — `kubeadm certs check-expiration`; openssl x509.
  26.5 Renewing Certificates 🎯 — `kubeadm certs renew all` + restart kubelet/
       control plane; ⚠️ update kubeconfig.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 26
  [THE CLUSTER PKI — everything speaks mutual TLS]
              🏛️ cluster CA  (/etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt, ~10y)
        ┌──────────┬─────────┴────────┬──────────────────┐
   📡 apiserver  🤖 kubelet      🗄️ etcd (own CA)    👩‍💻 admin
   (server+client)(client)       (peer+server)      CN=user, O=group
                                                     (client cert -> RBAC ids)
   🔑 new user: openssl CSR ─► kubectl certificate approve ─► embed in kubeconfig
   ⏰ check: kubeadm certs check-expiration   ·   renew: kubeadm certs renew all
   ⚠️ leaf certs ~1y -> RESTART the control plane / kubelet AFTER renewing.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: mutual TLS handshake; how the apiserver maps a client cert CN/O
      to user/group; CA validity (10y) vs leaf (1y).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the certificates API signers (kubelet-serving /
      kube-apiserver-client); key usages; external-CA mode; front-proxy CA;
      rotating the cluster CA (hard); cert-manager for app TLS.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Kubernetes runs on mutual TLS. A cluster CA (/etc/kubernetes/pki/ca.crt,
        ~10y) signs client/server certs for the apiserver, kubelet, controller-
        manager, scheduler, and admins; etcd usually has its own CA.
      • A client certificate's CN is the username and O is the group — that's how
        a cert maps to an RBAC identity. No separate "user object" exists.
      • New human access = generate a key+CSR, submit a CertificateSigningRequest,
        `kubectl certificate approve` it, then embed the signed cert in a
        kubeconfig.
      • Leaf certs expire (~1y); `kubeadm certs check-expiration` and `kubeadm
        certs renew all` manage them — and you MUST restart the control plane /
        kubelet after renewing.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Forgetting to restart components after `certs renew` leaves them using the
        old (renewed-but-not-loaded) certs — the cluster still breaks.
      • CN/O drive identity: a typo in CN gives a different (unauthorized) user;
        the group in O must match your RoleBindings.
      • Mixing up cert flags (client vs serving usages, wrong signerName) makes
        the CSR unusable even after approval.
      • An expired apiserver/etcd cert takes the whole control plane down — you
        can't kubectl your way out; fix on the node with kubeadm/openssl.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubeadm certs check-expiration` is the one-command health check for every
        cert and the CA at once.
      • Decode any cert with `openssl x509 -in cert.crt -noout -text` to read its
        CN/O, SANs, and validity.
      • After approving a CSR, pull the cert straight from the object:
        `kubectl get csr <name> -o jsonpath='{.status.certificate}' | base64 -d`.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubeadm certs check-expiration
      kubeadm certs renew all          # then restart control-plane static pods + kubelet
      kubectl get csr
      kubectl certificate approve dev   # or `deny`
      openssl x509 -in /etc/kubernetes/pki/apiserver.crt -noout -dates -subject
      kubectl config set-credentials dev --client-certificate=dev.crt --client-key=dev.key
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: create a key+CSR for user "dev" in group "easypj-devs", approve
      it, fetch the signed cert, build a kubeconfig context, and confirm `kubectl
      --context=dev get pods` is allowed/denied per a matching RoleBinding.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Approve a pending CertificateSigningRequest named "dev".
          A. `kubectl get csr` to see it Pending, then `kubectl certificate
          approve dev`; fetch the cert from .status.certificate.
      Q2. Create a client cert + kubeconfig for a new user.
          A. openssl key+CSR (CN=user/O=group) → submit CSR → approve → decode
          cert → `kubectl config set-credentials/set-context` with the cert+key.
      Q3. How do you check when the cluster certs expire?
          A. `kubeadm certs check-expiration` (all kubeadm-managed certs + CA), or
          `openssl x509 -noout -dates` on a specific cert.
      Q4. Renew all certs and make the cluster use them. Steps?
          A. `kubeadm certs renew all`, then restart the control-plane static pods
          (move/replace manifests or restart kubelet) so they reload the new certs.
      Q5. Where do the cluster certs live and name two.
          A. /etc/kubernetes/pki/ — e.g. ca.crt (cluster CA), apiserver.crt
          (apiserver serving cert), plus etcd/ subdir for etcd certs.
      Q6. How does a client cert become a Kubernetes identity?
          A. The apiserver maps the cert's CN to the username and O to the
          group(s); RBAC then authorizes that user/group.
      Q7. CA vs leaf cert validity — typical and implication?
          A. CA ~10y, leaf certs ~1y. Leaves must be renewed yearly; an expired
          leaf breaks that component even though the CA is still valid.
      Q8. You renewed certs but the apiserver still rejects connections. Why?
          A. The component wasn't restarted, so it's still serving the old cert in
          memory. Restart the static pod / kubelet.
      Q9. etcd uses a different cert chain — why does that matter?
          A. etcd typically has its own CA and peer/server certs; etcd cert expiry
          breaks the datastore independently of the apiserver CA.
      Q10. The whole control plane is down with x509 "certificate has expired".
           Recovery outline?
           A. On the control-plane node, `kubeadm certs renew all` (or regenerate
           the expired cert), restart kubelet/static pods, then update any admin
           kubeconfig that embedded the old cert.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the certificate web of a kubeadm cluster.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 27: Pod Security & Admission Control                         🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Workloads/Cluster Arch.
  [B] WHY: a compromised pod shouldn't own the node. VISUAL: the 3 PSS levels.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: Pod Security Standards & Admission; SecurityContext;
      admission controllers overview.
  27.1 Pod Security Standards 🎯 — Privileged/Baseline/Restricted. VISUAL:
       3-level ladder.
  27.2 Pod Security Admission 🎯 — namespace labels (enforce/warn/audit) +
       version pin.
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: Namespace
       metadata:
         name: easypj
         labels:
           pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce: restricted
           pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce-version: v1.31
           pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn: restricted     # warn before enforcing
           pod-security.kubernetes.io/audit: restricted
       # or: kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
  27.3 Security Contexts 🎯 — runAsNonRoot, readOnlyRootFilesystem,
       allowPrivilegeEscalation:false, capabilities drop ALL, seccomp.
       apiVersion: v1
       kind: Pod
       metadata: { name: easypj-web, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         securityContext:
           runAsNonRoot: true
           runAsUser: 1000
           seccompProfile: { type: RuntimeDefault }
         containers:
         - name: web
           image: easypj:v1
           securityContext:
             allowPrivilegeEscalation: false
             readOnlyRootFilesystem: true
             capabilities: { drop: ["ALL"] }
           volumeMounts:
           - { name: tmp, mountPath: /tmp }    # writable scratch for RO rootfs
         volumes:
         - name: tmp
           emptyDir: {}
  27.4 Admission Controllers (recap) 🎯 — built-in plugins; link to webhooks &
       ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (Ch 40).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 27
  [POD SECURITY STANDARDS — 3 levels, enforced by namespace labels]
   ┌─────────────┬────────────────────────────────────┬──────────────┐
   │ privileged  │ no restrictions                    │ kube-system  │
   │ baseline    │ blocks privileged/hostNet/hostPID  │ general apps │
   │ restricted  │ + non-root, drop ALL caps, seccomp,│ prod / multi-│
   │             │   read-only-ish rootfs             │ tenant 🔒    │
   └─────────────┴────────────────────────────────────┴──────────────┘
   Label the namespace:  pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
   Modes: enforce (block) · warn (stderr) · audit (log) — start with WARN.
   Hardened easypj pod: runAsNonRoot, allowPrivilegeEscalation:false,
   readOnlyRootFilesystem:true (+ emptyDir for writes), capabilities.drop:[ALL].
   ⚠️ PSA is per-namespace and does NOT evict already-running pods.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how PSA evaluates a pod at admission; how SecurityContext maps to
      kernel features (capabilities, seccomp, no_new_privs).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the restricted profile field-by-field; PSA
      exemptions (usernames/runtimeClasses/namespaces); cluster-wide defaults via
      AdmissionConfiguration; NET_BIND_SERVICE nuance; seccomp Localhost profiles.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Pod Security Standards define three levels: privileged (anything),
        baseline (blocks the obvious escapes — hostNet/hostPID/privileged), and
        restricted (also non-root, drop ALL caps, seccomp, no privilege
        escalation).
      • Pod Security Admission enforces them per NAMESPACE via labels, in three
        modes: enforce (reject), warn (stderr message), audit (log). Start with
        warn/audit before flipping to enforce.
      • SecurityContext is where you actually harden a pod: runAsNonRoot,
        readOnlyRootFilesystem, allowPrivilegeEscalation:false, capabilities.drop
        [ALL], seccompProfile RuntimeDefault.
      • PSA is admission-time only — it does NOT evict already-running pods, and
        it complements (doesn't replace) RBAC and NetworkPolicy.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Labeling a namespace restricted does nothing to pods already running —
        only new/updated pods are evaluated.
      • A read-only root filesystem breaks apps that write to disk; add writable
        emptyDir mounts for /tmp, caches, etc.
      • restricted requires runAsNonRoot AND a seccomp profile — images that
        default to root (UID 0) are rejected.
      • Pin enforce-version so a cluster upgrade doesn't silently tighten the
        policy and break admissions.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Roll out safely: set warn+audit=restricted first, watch the warnings, fix
        the pods, THEN set enforce=restricted.
      • `kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted` is
        the one-liner; `--overwrite` to change it.
      • Test a manifest against a level with a dry-run apply into a labeled ns —
        the rejection message names the exact violated control.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
      kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/warn=restricted --overwrite
      kubectl get ns easypj --show-labels
      kubectl apply -f hardened-pod.yaml -n easypj        # see admission verdict
      kubectl run root --image=nginx -n easypj            # rejected under restricted
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: label the easypj namespace enforce=restricted, try to run a stock
      nginx (rejected for running as root), then deploy the hardened pod above and
      watch it admitted.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Enforce the restricted standard on namespace easypj.
          A. `kubectl label ns easypj
          pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted` (optionally pin
          enforce-version).
      Q2. Harden a container to pass restricted. Which fields?
          A. pod: runAsNonRoot, seccompProfile RuntimeDefault; container:
          allowPrivilegeEscalation:false, readOnlyRootFilesystem:true,
          capabilities.drop:[ALL].
      Q3. A stock nginx pod is rejected in the namespace. Why?
          A. It runs as root (UID 0) and doesn't set the required restricted
