          fields, so PSA's enforce=restricted blocks admission.
      Q4. The app needs to write but rootfs is read-only. Fix?
          A. Keep readOnlyRootFilesystem:true and mount writable emptyDir volumes
          at the paths it writes (e.g. /tmp, cache dirs).
      Q5. Drop all Linux capabilities but keep binding to port 80. How?
          A. capabilities.drop:[ALL] then add back only NET_BIND_SERVICE (or run
          on a high port and front it with a Service/targetPort).
      Q6. What does seccompProfile RuntimeDefault do?
          A. Applies the container runtime's default seccomp filter, blocking
          dangerous syscalls — required by the restricted profile.
      Q7. Difference between warn and enforce modes?
          A. warn emits a message to the client but still admits the pod; enforce
          rejects the pod. audit only records to the audit log.
      Q8. Why pin pod-security...enforce-version?
          A. So the policy is evaluated against a fixed Kubernetes version; an
          upgrade won't silently add new restricted controls that break pods.
      Q9. kube-system runs privileged pods. How is that allowed?
          A. Its namespace is labeled (or exempt) at privileged level; PSA is
          per-namespace, so system components aren't held to restricted.
      Q10. Does enforcing restricted kill the pods already running as root?
           A. No — PSA only acts at admission. Existing pods keep running; they're
           only blocked on recreate/update. Evict/recreate to apply.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: a hardened easypj pod under restricted PSS.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 7 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ AuthN (certs / SA tokens) = WHO; RBAC = WHAT (deny by default, union  │
  │    of bindings, no deny rules). Role/RoleBinding (ns) vs ClusterRole/    │
  │    ClusterRoleBinding (cluster).                                        │
  │ ✅ ServiceAccounts are pod identities; disable automount when unused;    │
  │    bind RBAC as system:serviceaccount:<ns>:<name>.                       │
  │ ✅ The control plane runs on mutual TLS; approve CSRs, check & renew      │
  │    certs (restart after renew).                                         │
  │ ✅ Pod Security Standards (privileged/baseline/restricted) via namespace │
  │    labels; SecurityContext makes pods non-root & least-capability.       │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: RBAC Role+Binding and `auth can-i`, plus CSR approval, are      │
  │    high-frequency tasks.                                                 │
  │ 🔑 create role/rolebinding · auth can-i · certificate approve · kubeadm  │
  │    certs renew · label ns pod-security...                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 7 VISUAL SUMMARY]: the AuthN->AuthZ->Admission pipeline guarding easypj.

PART 8: CLUSTER MAINTENANCE & LIFECYCLE                                 🔴 🎯
  Chapters 28-30  ·  Cluster lifecycle competency (25%)  ·  "Keep it alive"
  What you'll build: a safe upgrade of your kubeadm cluster, automated etcd
  backups with a tested restore, and a drain/patch/uncordon node-maintenance
  routine that keeps easypj available throughout.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 8 INTRO]
  Clusters aren't "set and forget." Versions age out, disks fill, nodes need
  kernel patches, and disasters happen. This part is the admin's recurring
  calendar: upgrade Kubernetes safely (control plane first, then workers),
  guarantee recoverability (backup + DR), and take nodes down for maintenance
  WITHOUT taking easypj down (drain + PodDisruptionBudgets).

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 8 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch28 UPGRADE (kubeadm, version skew) ─▶ Ch30 NODE MAINTENANCE (drain/PDB)│
  │                 │                                                        │
  │                 ▼                                                        │
  │ Ch29 BACKUP & DISASTER RECOVERY (etcd snapshots, automation, Velero)     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 8 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: snapshot etcd FIRST -> `kubeadm upgrade plan/apply`
  on the control plane -> drain+upgrade kubelet on each worker -> verify easypj
  unaffected (PDB kept minAvailable). VISUAL: the upgrade order + version-skew
  rules (reuse+augment cka_1).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 28: Cluster Upgrades with kubeadm                            🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% (lifecycle). EXAM TASK.
  [B] WHY: a near-certain exam task and a routine real job. VISUAL: upgrade
      order diagram (CP -> workers, one minor at a time).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: release cycle & version skew; upgrade the control plane;
      upgrade workers; verify.
  28.1 Release Cycle & Version Skew 🎯 — ~3 minor/yr; kubelet may trail
       apiserver by N; never skip a minor. VISUAL: skew rules table.
  28.2 Upgrade Strategy 🎯 — backup etcd first; one minor at a time; CP before
       workers.
  28.3 Upgrade the Control Plane 🎯 — unhold kubeadm -> `kubeadm upgrade plan`
       -> `apply vX` -> unhold+upgrade kubelet/kubectl -> restart.
       # on the control-plane node (example target v1.31.1)
       apt-mark unhold kubeadm
       apt-get update && apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.1-1.1
       apt-mark hold kubeadm
       kubeadm upgrade plan                  # shows the exact upgrade path
       kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.1         # re-renders manifests, rotates certs
       kubectl drain <cp-node> --ignore-daemonsets
       apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
       apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.1-1.1 kubectl=1.31.1-1.1
       apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl
       systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet
       kubectl uncordon <cp-node>
  28.4 Upgrade Workers 🎯 — drain -> unhold -> `kubeadm upgrade node` -> upgrade
       kubelet -> uncordon.
       # from a control-plane/admin context: drain the worker first
       kubectl drain <worker> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
       # then ON the worker node:
       apt-mark unhold kubeadm && apt-get install -y kubeadm=1.31.1-1.1 && apt-mark hold kubeadm
       kubeadm upgrade node                  # updates the local kubelet config
       apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl
       apt-get install -y kubelet=1.31.1-1.1 kubectl=1.31.1-1.1
       apt-mark hold kubelet kubectl
       systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet
       # back on admin:
       kubectl uncordon <worker>
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 28
  [CLUSTER UPGRADE — order & version skew (one minor at a time)]
   0  💾 BACKUP etcd first (always)
   1  CONTROL PLANE node:
        apt-mark unhold kubeadm ; install kubeadm vX
        kubeadm upgrade plan  ─►  kubeadm upgrade apply vX
        apt-mark unhold kubelet kubectl ; install vX ; restart kubelet
   2  EACH WORKER (one at a time):
        kubectl drain <node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
        kubeadm upgrade node ; upgrade kubelet ; restart kubelet
        kubectl uncordon <node>
   ⚠️ NEVER skip a minor (1.30 -> 1.32 ✗). kubelet may TRAIL the apiserver,
      never lead it.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: what `kubeadm upgrade apply` does (re-renders manifests, rotates
      certs, updates the kubeadm-config ConfigMap); how skew is enforced.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): full skew policy (kubelet n-3, kubectl ±1); etcd
      version compatibility; migrating deprecated/removed APIs across versions;
      addon (CoreDNS/kube-proxy) upgrades; rollback considerations.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Upgrade order is fixed: back up etcd → control plane → workers, ONE minor
        version at a time (1.30→1.31, never 1.30→1.32).
      • Version skew: kubelet may TRAIL the apiserver (up to n-3) but must never
        lead it; kubectl is ±1. Upgrade the control plane before the kubelets.
      • Control plane: `kubeadm upgrade plan` then `kubeadm upgrade apply vX`
        (re-renders static-pod manifests, rotates certs, bumps addons); then
        upgrade kubelet/kubectl and restart kubelet.
      • Workers: drain → `kubeadm upgrade node` → upgrade+restart kubelet →
        uncordon. The apt-mark hold/unhold dance pins package versions.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Skipping a minor is unsupported and can corrupt the control plane.
      • Forgetting apt-mark unhold leaves packages pinned (install does nothing);
        forgetting to re-hold lets an unplanned upgrade slip in later.
      • Not draining a worker before upgrading risks disrupting running pods
        abruptly.
      • Forgetting `systemctl restart kubelet` after installing the new kubelet
        means the node keeps running the old version.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubeadm upgrade plan` prints the exact component versions and the target
        — read it before touching anything.
      • `kubectl get nodes` after each step confirms the VERSION column moved and
        the node is Ready.
      • Upgrade the control plane's kubelet too — it's also a node and shows in
        skew checks.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubeadm upgrade plan
      kubeadm upgrade apply v1.31.1            # control plane
      kubeadm upgrade node                     # workers / extra CP nodes
      apt-mark unhold kubeadm kubelet kubectl ; apt-mark hold kubeadm kubelet kubectl
      kubectl drain <node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data ; kubectl uncordon <node>
      kubectl get nodes -o wide                # VERSION column
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: on a 2-node kubeadm cluster, upgrade one minor end to end —
      control plane (plan/apply, kubelet) then the worker (drain, upgrade node,
      kubelet, uncordon) — verifying versions and Ready at each step.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Give the full control-plane upgrade sequence.
          A. Back up etcd → unhold+install new kubeadm → `kubeadm upgrade plan` →
          `kubeadm upgrade apply vX` → drain CP → unhold+install kubelet/kubectl →
          restart kubelet → uncordon.
      Q2. Upgrade a worker node. Steps?
          A. drain (--ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data) → unhold+install
          kubeadm → `kubeadm upgrade node` → install+restart kubelet → uncordon.
      Q3. State the version-skew rule for kubelet vs apiserver.
          A. kubelet may be up to 3 minor versions behind the apiserver but never
          ahead — so always upgrade the control plane first.
      Q4. Why drain a node before upgrading its kubelet?
          A. To evict pods gracefully and reschedule them elsewhere, so the
          kubelet restart/reboot doesn't abruptly kill running workloads.
      Q5. What do apt-mark hold/unhold accomplish here?
          A. hold pins kube* packages so routine `apt upgrade` can't bump them;
          you unhold only to install the chosen version, then re-hold.
      Q6. What does `kubeadm upgrade plan` show?
          A. The current versions, the available target(s), the exact upgrade
          path, and component/addon versions that will change.
      Q7. You upgraded packages but `kubectl get nodes` still shows the old
          version. Why?
          A. kubelet wasn't restarted (or daemon-reload skipped). Run
          `systemctl daemon-reload && systemctl restart kubelet`.
      Q8. Can you jump from 1.29 to 1.31 directly?
          A. No — upgrade one minor at a time (1.29→1.30→1.31). Skipping minors
          is unsupported.
      Q9. What does `kubeadm upgrade apply` actually change on the CP?
          A. Re-renders the static-pod manifests for apiserver/controller-manager/
          scheduler, rotates certs as needed, and updates kubeadm config + addons.
      Q10. After upgrading, how do you verify the cluster is healthy?
           A. `kubectl get nodes` (all Ready, new VERSION), check control-plane
           pods in kube-system Running, and that workloads (easypj) are intact.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the upgrade pipeline, CP then workers.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 29: etcd Backup & Disaster Recovery (Operations)             🔴 🎯🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% + Day-2.
  [B] WHY: Ch 11 taught the commands; this chapter makes backup a RELIABLE,
      automated, tested process — the difference between knowing and surviving.
      VISUAL: backup cadence + DR runbook.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: schedule backups; verify & rotate; full DR drill; broader DR
      (Velero for app/PV state).
  29.1 Why Backups Are Critical 🎯 — RPO/RTO; what etcd does & doesn't capture
       (not PV data!). VISUAL: what's in the snapshot card.
  29.2 etcd Backup (recap + verify) 🎯 — snapshot save + status; store off-node.
  29.3 etcd Restore (full DR drill) 🎯 — restore -> repoint -> verify; 🧪 do it
       end to end.
  29.4 Backup Automation 🏢 — CronJob/systemd timer; retention; off-cluster copy.
       apiVersion: batch/v1
       kind: CronJob
       metadata: { name: etcd-backup, namespace: kube-system }
       spec:
         schedule: "0 */6 * * *"             # every 6 hours
         jobTemplate:
           spec:
             template:
               spec:
                 hostNetwork: true
                 nodeSelector: { node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane: "" }
                 tolerations:
                 - { key: node-role.kubernetes.io/control-plane, operator: Exists, effect: NoSchedule }
                 containers:
                 - name: backup
                   image: registry.k8s.io/etcd:3.5.15-0
                   command: ["/bin/sh","-c"]
                   args:
                   - >
                     etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379
                     --cacert=/pki/ca.crt --cert=/pki/server.crt --key=/pki/server.key
                     snapshot save /backup/etcd-$(date +%F-%H%M).db
                   volumeMounts:
                   - { name: pki, mountPath: /pki, readOnly: true }
                   - { name: backup, mountPath: /backup }
                 restartPolicy: OnFailure
                 volumes:
                 - { name: pki, hostPath: { path: /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd } }
                 - { name: backup, hostPath: { path: /var/backups/etcd } }
       # then ship /var/backups/etcd OFF the node (object store) + prune old files.
  29.5 Beyond etcd — Velero 🏢 — back up namespaces + PV snapshots; restore
       easypj to a new cluster.
  29.6 🧪 PRACTICE EXERCISES (timed restore drills).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 29
  [WHAT etcd DOES (and DOESN'T) BACK UP]
   ✅ in the snapshot: pods, deploys, services, secrets, configmaps, RBAC, ...
   ❌ NOT in it: PersistentVolume DATA (the 🐘 postgres files on disk!)
        └─► back PV data up SEPARATELY: Velero + volume snapshots.

  [BACKUP DISCIPLINE — a backup never restored is not a backup]
   ⏰ scheduled (CronJob / systemd timer)
        ▼
   🔍 verify (etcdctl snapshot status --write-table)
        ▼
   📤 copy OFF the node (object store)
        ▼
   🧪 TEST-restore periodically  ─► document RPO / RTO

  [G] 🔧 UTH: snapshot consistency (point-in-time via MVCC); why PV data needs a
      separate backup; restore implications for in-flight objects.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): consistent multi-resource backups; defrag before/
      after; Velero restic vs CSI snapshots; restoring a single namespace;
      cross-cluster migration; GitOps re-apply as DR.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • etcd holds all cluster STATE (pods, deployments, services, secrets,
        configmaps, RBAC). An etcd snapshot backs that up — but NOT the data
        inside PersistentVolumes (your postgres files on disk).
      • A backup you have never test-restored is not a backup. Define RPO (how
        much data you can lose) and RTO (how fast you must recover) and prove
        them with drills.
      • Automate snapshots (CronJob/systemd timer), VERIFY them (`snapshot status
        --write-table`), and copy them OFF the node to object storage with
        retention/rotation.
      • For app + PV recovery (and cross-cluster migration), use Velero with
        volume snapshots — etcd alone can't restore disk data.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Assuming etcd backs up PV data — it doesn't. Back volumes up separately.
      • Restoring into the LIVE etcd data-dir while etcd is running corrupts it —
        restore to a NEW dir and repoint, with etcd stopped.
      • Missing/incorrect --cacert/--cert/--key (or ETCDCTL_API) makes every
        etcdctl command fail; script the flags.
      • Keeping backups only on the same node — a node loss loses both the cluster
        and its backups.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Save the cert flags once: export ETCDCTL_API=3 and an alias with
        --endpoints/--cacert/--cert/--key so snapshot save/status/restore are short.
      • `etcdctl snapshot status snap.db --write-table` instantly tells you the
        snapshot is readable (hash, revision, size).
      • In the exam, the etcd certs are under /etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ — read the
        static pod manifest for the exact paths.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      ETCDCTL_API=3 etcdctl --endpoints=https://127.0.0.1:2379 \
        --cacert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/ca.crt \
        --cert=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.crt \
        --key=/etc/kubernetes/pki/etcd/server.key snapshot save /backup/snap.db
      etcdctl snapshot status /backup/snap.db --write-table
      etcdutl snapshot restore /backup/snap.db --data-dir /var/lib/etcd-restore
      # then edit the etcd static pod to point hostPath at /var/lib/etcd-restore
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: take a verified snapshot, create a marker object, delete it, then
      restore the snapshot into a new data-dir, repoint the etcd static pod, and
      confirm the marker object is back — timing the whole RTO.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Schedule automated etcd backups. Approach?
          A. A CronJob (or systemd timer) on the control-plane node running
          `etcdctl snapshot save` with the etcd certs, plus an off-node copy and
          retention pruning.
      Q2. Verify a snapshot is valid without restoring it.
          A. `etcdctl snapshot status snap.db --write-table` — shows hash,
          revision, total keys, and size; failure means a corrupt/partial file.
      Q3. Perform a full etcd restore.
          A. Stop the API/etcd, `etcdutl snapshot restore snap.db --data-dir
          /var/lib/etcd-restore`, repoint the etcd static pod's hostPath to it,
          start etcd, verify objects.
      Q4. What does an etcd snapshot NOT contain?
          A. The data inside PersistentVolumes (e.g. postgres files). Only API
          object state is captured.
      Q5. Why have a retention policy?
          A. To bound storage and keep enough history to meet RPO; prune old
          snapshots but keep enough points to recover from a late-discovered issue.
      Q6. Where should backups be stored and why?
          A. OFF the node, in object storage — so losing the node (or cluster)
          doesn't lose the backups too.
      Q7. How do you back up easypj's namespace AND its PV data?
          A. Velero with a volume-snapshot or restic plugin — it captures the API
          objects and the PersistentVolume contents together.
      Q8. Restore easypj into a brand-new cluster. Tool and flow?
          A. Velero restore from the backup in object storage into the new
          cluster, which recreates the namespace objects and PV data.
      Q9. Define RPO and RTO.
          A. RPO = maximum acceptable data loss (drives backup frequency); RTO =
          maximum acceptable downtime (drives how fast restore must run).
      Q10. Give the order of a DR runbook after total control-plane loss.
           A. Provision a node → restore the latest verified etcd snapshot →
           repoint etcd → bring up the control plane → restore PV data (Velero) →
           validate workloads → document actual RPO/RTO achieved.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the backup -> verify -> restore -> validate loop.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 30: Node Maintenance & OS Upgrades                           🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch / Workloads (self-healing).
  [B] WHY: patch nodes without dropping easypj. VISUAL: drain workflow +
      PDB guard.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: cordon/drain/uncordon; PodDisruptionBudgets; OS/kernel
      patching routine.
  30.1 Draining Nodes 🎯 — cordon -> drain (--ignore-daemonsets
       --delete-emptydir-data) -> uncordon. VISUAL: reuse cka_1 maintenance flow.
  30.2 PodDisruptionBudgets 🎯 — minAvailable/maxUnavailable protect easypj
       during voluntary disruption; ⚠️ can block a drain.
       apiVersion: policy/v1
       kind: PodDisruptionBudget
       metadata: { name: easypj-web-pdb, namespace: easypj }
       spec:
         minAvailable: 2                     # keep >=2 web pods up during drains
         selector:
           matchLabels: { app: easypj-web }
       # use EITHER minAvailable OR maxUnavailable, never both.
       # with 3 replicas + minAvailable:2, a drain may evict at most 1 at a time.
  30.3 OS Upgrades & Kernel Patches 🏢 — the safe loop (drain -> patch -> reboot
       -> uncordon), one node at a time.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 30
  [NODE MAINTENANCE — keep easypj up while a node goes down]
   🚫 cordon node2          (no NEW pods land here)
        ▼
   🏗️ drain node2 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
        │  evicts pods -> rescheduled elsewhere
        │  🛡️ PodDisruptionBudget(minAvailable:2) GATES the eviction
        ▼  (drain BLOCKS if it would drop below minAvailable)
   🔧 patch / reboot / kernel update
        ▼
   ✅ uncordon node2        (schedulable again)
   ⚠️ drain also blocks on bare (unmanaged) pods -> --force only if intended.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how eviction respects PDBs; why DaemonSet pods need
      --ignore-daemonsets; graceful termination & terminationGracePeriod.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the Eviction API vs raw deletion; PDB
      unhealthyPodEvictionPolicy; drain timeouts & --grace-period; surge during
      drain; NoExecute taint-based eviction vs drain.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Node maintenance flow: cordon (no new pods) → drain (evict existing pods)
        → patch/reboot → uncordon (schedulable again). Do one node at a time.
      • `kubectl drain` needs --ignore-daemonsets (DaemonSet pods can't move) and
        usually --delete-emptydir-data (acknowledges losing emptyDir scratch).
      • PodDisruptionBudgets (minAvailable / maxUnavailable) gate VOLUNTARY
        disruptions: a drain blocks rather than drop easypj below the budget.
      • drain uses the Eviction API, which respects PDBs and graceful termination
        — unlike a raw pod delete.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • A too-strict PDB (e.g. minAvailable == replicas) makes drain hang forever
        — nothing can be evicted.
      • drain refuses to evict bare/unmanaged pods (no controller) unless --force,
        which deletes them permanently.
      • Forgetting --delete-emptydir-data stops the drain on pods with emptyDir;
        forgetting --ignore-daemonsets stops it on DaemonSet pods.
      • Forgetting to uncordon leaves the node unschedulable after maintenance —
        capacity silently missing.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl drain <node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data` is the
        standard incantation — memorize it.
      • Watch a blocked drain's reason: it prints "cannot evict ... violates PDB";
        scale up or relax the PDB to proceed.
      • `kubectl get nodes` SchedulingDisabled column shows cordoned nodes at a
        glance.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl cordon <node>
      kubectl drain <node> --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
      kubectl uncordon <node>
      kubectl create pdb easypj-web-pdb --selector=app=easypj-web --min-available=2
      kubectl get pdb -n easypj          # ALLOWED DISRUPTIONS column
      kubectl get nodes                  # SchedulingDisabled = cordoned
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: deploy easypj-web with 3 replicas + a minAvailable:2 PDB, drain
      its node, and watch the eviction proceed one pod at a time (never dropping
      below 2), then uncordon.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Safely take node2 down for a kernel patch. Full sequence?
          A. cordon node2 → drain node2 --ignore-daemonsets --delete-emptydir-data
          → patch/reboot → uncordon node2.
      Q2. Difference between cordon and drain?
          A. cordon only marks the node unschedulable (stops NEW pods); drain also
          evicts the pods already running on it.
      Q3. Create a PDB keeping at least 2 easypj-web pods available.
          A. PodDisruptionBudget with selector app=easypj-web and minAvailable: 2
          (or `kubectl create pdb ... --min-available=2`).
      Q4. A drain hangs and won't finish. Likely cause?
          A. A PDB would be violated (or bare/DaemonSet pods block it). Check
          ALLOWED DISRUPTIONS; relax the PDB or scale up replicas.
      Q5. Why is --ignore-daemonsets needed?
          A. DaemonSet pods are pinned one-per-node and can't be rescheduled, so
          drain refuses unless you tell it to skip them.
      Q6. What does --delete-emptydir-data acknowledge?
          A. That evicting pods with emptyDir volumes permanently loses that
          scratch data; drain won't proceed on them without it.
      Q7. After maintenance the node takes no pods. What was missed?
          A. uncordon — the node is still SchedulingDisabled. Run
          `kubectl uncordon <node>`.
      Q8. Maintenance across a 5-node cluster — what order/pace?
          A. One node at a time (cordon/drain/patch/uncordon), verifying workloads
          stay healthy and PDBs are satisfied before moving to the next.
      Q9. How does eviction give pods a clean shutdown?
          A. It sends SIGTERM and honors terminationGracePeriodSeconds (and
          preStop hooks) before SIGKILL, so apps can finish in-flight work.
      Q10. Outline the safe kernel-patch loop for the whole fleet.
           A. For each node: cordon → drain → apply patch → reboot → wait Ready →
           uncordon → confirm pods rescheduled and healthy, then next node.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: a node going down & back up with easypj never dropping.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 8 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Upgrade order: backup etcd -> control plane -> workers; one minor at a │
  │    time; never skip a minor; mind version skew; restart kubelet.         │
  │ ✅ A backup is only real once you've test-RESTORED it; etcd does NOT back │
  │    up PV data (use Velero/volume snapshots for that).                    │
  │ ✅ cordon -> drain -> maintain -> uncordon; PodDisruptionBudgets keep     │
  │    easypj available during voluntary disruptions.                        │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: kubeadm upgrade and node drain are common, etcd restore is gold.│
  │ 🔑 kubeadm upgrade plan/apply/node · drain/cordon/uncordon · etcdctl     │
  │    snapshot save/restore                                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 8 VISUAL SUMMARY]: the maintenance calendar — upgrade, back up, patch.

PART 9: TROUBLESHOOTING                                                 🔴 🎯
  Chapters 31-34  ·  CKA DOMAIN WEIGHT: 30% (THE BIGGEST)  ·  "Fix it fast"
  What you'll build: a repeatable diagnostic method and the reflexes to fix
  any broken easypj pod, node, control-plane component, or network path under
  exam time pressure.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 9 INTRO]
  Troubleshooting is the largest slice of the exam AND the daily reality of the
  job. The secret is METHOD, not memorization: a fixed flow (gather -> read ->
  isolate -> fix -> verify) applied layer by layer (pod -> node -> control
  plane -> network). Every section here is a symptom you'll learn to read on
  easypj and a fix you'll drill until it's reflex.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 9 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch31 PODS/CONTAINERS ─▶ Ch32 NODES/CONTROL PLANE ─▶ Ch34 SERVICES/NET   │
  │       (highest layer)        (the machines/brains)     (connectivity)   │
  │                 └──────────── Ch33 OBSERVE ───────────┘                 │
  │                    logs (output streams) + metrics (top)                │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 9 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: deliberately BREAK easypj in 10 classic ways and fix
  each with the same method. THE METHOD (memorize): 🩺 get -> describe (read
  Events!) -> logs (--previous) -> exec -> isolate layer -> fix -> verify.
  VISUAL: the master troubleshooting decision tree (reuse+augment cka_1).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 31: Debugging Pods & Containers                              🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Troubleshooting 30%.
  [B] WHY: most failures surface as a sick pod. VISUAL: pod-status decision tree.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: read pod phases & container states; exit codes; the debug
      toolkit & order; ephemeral debug containers; fix common easypj failures.
  31.1 Pod Lifecycle & Status Codes 🎯 — phases (Pending/Running/Succeeded/
       Failed/Unknown); container states; exit codes (0/1/137 OOM/143);
       CrashLoopBackOff backoff curve; restartPolicy. VISUAL: lifecycle pills +
       exit-code card.
  31.2 The Debugging Toolkit 🎯 — the order: status -> describe(Events) ->
       logs --previous -> exec -> events. ⚠️ #1 miss: skipping Events.
  31.3 Essential Debug Commands 🎯 — get -o wide; describe; logs [-c][--previous]
       [-f][--since]; exec -it; `kubectl debug` ephemeral container (distroless);
       port-forward; cp.
  31.4 Debugging easypj — common scenarios 🧪 — (1) CrashLoop: db connection
       refused -> initContainer wait; (2) Pending: insufficient memory -> lower
       requests; (3) 502: Django bound to 127.0.0.1 -> bind 0.0.0.0:8000 +
       ALLOWED_HOSTS; (4) ImagePullBackOff: wrong tag/registry secret;
       (5) CreateContainerConfigError: missing ConfigMap/Secret.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 31
  [POD DEBUGGING DECISION TREE — read this top-down]
   kubectl get pod  ─ what STATE?
   ├─ Pending ─────► describe (Events): no resources? taint? unbound PVC?
   ├─ ImagePullBackOff ► describe: wrong image:tag / missing pull secret
   ├─ CreateContainerConfigError ► missing ConfigMap/Secret/key
   ├─ CrashLoopBackOff ► logs --previous: app error? OOM(137)? bad config?
   ├─ Running but bad ► logs + exec -it + check readiness/endpoints
   └─ Completed/Error ► it was a Job? check exit code
   🔁 ALWAYS: describe FIRST (read Events) → logs --previous → exec → fix → verify

  [EXIT CODES & easypj failures]
   ┌──────┬───────────────────┐   easypj real fixes:
   │ 0    │ success           │   • CrashLoop: db refused -> initContainer wait
   │ 1    │ app error         │   • Pending: req 2Gi>free -> lower to 256Mi
   │ 137  │ OOMKilled (mem)   │   • 502: Django bound 127.0.0.1 -> 0.0.0.0:8000
   │ 143  │ graceful SIGTERM  │   • ConfigError: create easypj-config/-secret
   └──────┴───────────────────┘

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the kubelet computes backoff; how probes drive restarts &
      readiness; ephemeral container internals.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): exit code 128+signal; shareProcessNamespace;
      `kubectl debug --target` a process namespace; readinessGates;
      terminationMessagePath; reading cgroup memory for OOM forensics.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Read the pod STATE first, then let it route you: Pending (scheduling),
        ImagePullBackOff (image/secret), CreateContainerConfigError (missing
        cm/secret), CrashLoopBackOff (app/config), Running-but-bad (probes/net).
      • THE METHOD: describe (read Events!) → logs --previous → exec → fix →
        verify. Skipping Events is the #1 wasted-time mistake.
      • Exit codes tell the story: 0 success, 1 app error, 137 OOMKilled (memory),
        143 graceful SIGTERM. 137 is a limits problem, not a code bug.
      • For crashed/distroless containers use `logs --previous` and `kubectl
        debug` ephemeral containers — you can't exec a container that won't stay up.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Forgetting `--previous` shows the NEW container's (empty) logs, not the
        crash you need to see.
      • Ignoring the Events section of describe — it usually states the exact
        cause (FailedScheduling, Failed pull, OOMKilled).
      • In multi-container pods, `logs`/`exec` default to one container — use
        `-c <name>` or you read the wrong one.
      • Misreading OOMKilled (137) as an application bug — it's the memory limit;
        raise limits or fix the leak.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • describe FIRST, always — `kubectl describe pod X | sed -n '/Events/,$p'`
        jumps straight to the cause.
      • `kubectl debug -it pod/X --image=busybox --target=app` attaches a tool
        container sharing the target's namespace — no need to rebuild the image.
      • `kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp` across the namespace shows
        the timeline when one pod's describe isn't enough.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get pod easypj-web -o wide
      kubectl describe pod easypj-web                 # READ Events
      kubectl logs easypj-web --previous -c web
      kubectl exec -it easypj-web -c web -- sh
      kubectl debug -it easypj-web --image=busybox --target=web
      kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp -n easypj
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: deploy an easypj-web that CrashLoops because the DB is refused,
      read the previous logs, add an initContainer that waits for easypj-db, and
      watch it go Running — then break it with a 2Gi request and fix the Pending.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. A pod is CrashLoopBackOff. How do you find the root cause?
          A. `kubectl logs <pod> --previous` to read the dead container's output,
          plus describe Events; common: DB refused, bad config, missing env.
      Q2. A pod is stuck Pending. First command and likely causes?
          A. `kubectl describe pod` → Events. Causes: insufficient cpu/mem, no
          node tolerates a taint, unbound PVC, or nodeSelector/affinity unmet.
      Q3. ImagePullBackOff — how do you fix it?
          A. describe shows the pull error: fix the image:tag, the registry name,
          or add/repair an imagePullSecret for a private registry.
      Q4. How do you see logs from a container that already crashed?
          A. `kubectl logs <pod> -c <container> --previous` — the current instance
          may have no useful logs.
      Q5. Exec into a running pod to inspect its filesystem/env.
          A. `kubectl exec -it <pod> -c <container> -- sh` (or bash), then inspect
          /env, mounted config, connectivity.
      Q6. A pod shows exit code 137. What happened and the fix?
          A. OOMKilled — it exceeded its memory limit. Raise the limit or reduce
          the app's memory use; it is not an application crash per se.
      Q7. The image is distroless (no shell). How do you debug it?
          A. `kubectl debug -it <pod> --image=busybox --target=<container>` to
          attach an ephemeral debug container sharing its namespaces.
      Q8. CreateContainerConfigError — what's wrong?
          A. The pod references a ConfigMap/Secret or key that doesn't exist.
          Create the missing object (easypj-config/easypj-secret) or fix the key.
      Q9. The pod runs but the Service sends no traffic to it. Why?
          A. Its readiness probe is failing, so it's excluded from endpoints. Fix
          the probe path/port or the app so readiness passes.
      Q10. easypj returns 502 through the Service. Likely app fix?
           A. Django bound to 127.0.0.1 — bind 0.0.0.0:8000 (and set ALLOWED_HOSTS)
           so the container accepts traffic from the Service/other pods.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the pod-debugging decision tree, captioned.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 32: Troubleshooting Nodes & Control Plane Components          🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Troubleshooting 30% + Cluster Arch 25%.
  [B] WHY: when kubectl itself fails, you debug from the node with crictl &
      journalctl. VISUAL: "kubectl is down — now what?" flow.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: diagnose NotReady nodes; troubleshoot kubelet; troubleshoot
      control-plane static pods; node conditions.
  32.1 Node NotReady — diagnosis 🎯 — causes (kubelet down, CNI missing, swap on,
       runtime down, disk pressure); SSH -> systemctl status kubelet ->
       journalctl -> fix -> restart. VISUAL: node-conditions card.
  32.2 Troubleshooting kubelet 🎯 — only systemd service; config at
       /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml; cert expiry; can't reach apiserver; swap;
       daemon-reload before restart.
  32.3 Troubleshooting Control Plane Components 🎯 — static pods in
       /etc/kubernetes/manifests/; when apiserver down use `crictl ps -a` +
       `crictl logs`; common bad-manifest failures. VISUAL: component-down matrix.
  32.4 Cluster-Level Failures 🩺 — etcd unhealthy; certs expired; full disk;
       port conflicts; systematic recovery.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 32
  [NODE NotReady — diagnose from the NODE (kubectl may be useless)]
   kubectl get nodes ─ node2 NotReady
        ▼ ssh node2
   systemctl status kubelet ─ running? ──no──► systemctl start kubelet
        ▼ yes                                   (daemon-reload if config changed)
   journalctl -u kubelet -f ─ read the error:
        ├─ "cni plugin not initialized"  ─► install / fix the CNI
        ├─ "failed to run Kubelet: swap" ─► swapoff -a (+ fstab)
        ├─ x509 / certificate expired    ─► renew the kubelet cert
        └─ runtime down                  ─► systemctl restart containerd
   🔧 apiserver itself down? use crictl ps -a · crictl logs <id> (NOT kubectl).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the kubelet reports node conditions; the heartbeat (node-
      lease); how a bad static-pod manifest fails silently (only crictl/
      journalctl show it).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): kubelet flags vs config.yaml precedence; node-
      problem-detector; /var/log/pods; crictl inspect/exec; cgroup-driver
      mismatch symptoms; node-lease update frequency.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • When a node is NotReady, diagnose FROM the node: `systemctl status
        kubelet` → `journalctl -u kubelet -f` → read the error → fix → restart.
      • The kubelet is a systemd service, NOT a pod. Its config is
        /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml; after editing, daemon-reload before restart.
      • Control-plane components are STATIC PODS in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/.
        A typo there fails silently — when the apiserver is down, use `crictl ps
        -a` and `crictl logs`, not kubectl.
      • Common NotReady causes: kubelet down, CNI missing, swap on, container
        runtime down, disk/memory pressure, expired certs.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Reaching for kubectl when the apiserver itself is down — it can't help;
        switch to crictl/journalctl on the node.
      • Editing kubelet config or a unit and forgetting `systemctl daemon-reload`
        before restart — the change doesn't take.
      • A bad static-pod manifest doesn't show an error via kubectl; only
        crictl/journalctl/kubelet logs reveal it.
      • Swap left enabled stops the kubelet from starting (unless explicitly
        allowed) — `swapoff -a` + fix /etc/fstab.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `journalctl -u kubelet -f` streaming the kubelet error is the single most
        useful node-debug command.
      • Editing a file in /etc/kubernetes/manifests/ makes the kubelet recreate
        that static pod within seconds — no restart needed.
      • `crictl ps -a | grep apiserver` then `crictl logs <id>` reads the
        apiserver's own logs when kubectl is dead.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      systemctl status kubelet ; systemctl restart kubelet ; systemctl daemon-reload
      journalctl -u kubelet -f --no-pager
      crictl ps -a ; crictl logs <container-id>
      kubectl describe node <node> | sed -n '/Conditions/,/Events/p'
      swapoff -a
      ls /etc/kubernetes/manifests/        # the static-pod manifests
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: stop the kubelet (or introduce a typo into the apiserver static-
      pod manifest), observe the node/control-plane break, diagnose it with
      journalctl/crictl, and restore service.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. A node is NotReady. Outline the diagnosis.
          A. ssh to it → `systemctl status kubelet` → `journalctl -u kubelet -f` →
          read the error (CNI/swap/cert/runtime) → fix → restart kubelet.
      Q2. The kubelet won't start. Where do you look?
          A. journalctl -u kubelet, /var/lib/kubelet/config.yaml, and the
          container runtime status; common causes are swap, cert expiry, bad config.
      Q3. kubectl times out — the apiserver seems down. How do you debug?
          A. On the control-plane node use `crictl ps -a` to find the apiserver
          container and `crictl logs <id>` to read why it's crashing.
      Q4. You edited the apiserver static-pod manifest and now it won't come up.
          A. A manifest typo. Fix /etc/kubernetes/manifests/kube-apiserver.yaml
          (validate YAML/flags); the kubelet recreates the static pod automatically.
      Q5. Components fail with x509 errors. Cause and fix?
          A. Expired certs. `kubeadm certs check-expiration`, then `kubeadm certs
          renew ...` and restart the affected static pods/kubelet.
      Q6. The kubelet logs "running with swap on ... failed". Fix?
          A. `swapoff -a` and remove the swap entry from /etc/fstab so it stays
          off across reboots; restart kubelet.
      Q7. A node shows DiskPressure. What does that do and how to clear it?
          A. The kubelet taints it and evicts pods; free disk (prune images/logs:
          `crictl rmi --prune`, rotate logs) until pressure clears.
      Q8. Node NotReady with "cni plugin not initialized". Fix?
          A. The CNI isn't installed/healthy on that node — (re)apply the CNI or
          fix its DaemonSet pod, then the kubelet reports Ready.
      Q9. How do you read a node's conditions?
          A. `kubectl describe node <n>` (Conditions section) or `kubectl get node
          <n> -o jsonpath='{.status.conditions}'` — Ready, MemoryPressure, etc.
      Q10. etcd is unhealthy and the control plane is flaky. First checks?
           A. `crictl ps -a | grep etcd` + `crictl logs`, and `etcdctl endpoint
           health` with the etcd certs; look for disk/quorum/cert issues.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the node/control-plane diagnostic ladder.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 33: Logging, Monitoring & Resource Usage                     🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Domain: Troubleshooting 30% (output streams + monitor).
  [B] WHY: official competencies "manage/evaluate container output streams" and
      "monitor cluster & app resource usage." VISUAL: log pipeline + metrics
      pipeline.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: master kubectl logs; cluster logging patterns; metrics-server
      & `kubectl top`; spot resource problems.
  33.1 Application Logging (output streams) 🎯 — logs flags (--previous/-f/
       --tail/--since/-c/--all-containers/-l); where logs live on the node
       (/var/log/containers, /var/log/pods).
  33.2 Cluster-Level Logging 🏢 — node-agent DaemonSet vs sidecar patterns;
       EFK/Loki; log rotation (containerLogMaxSize/Files). VISUAL: log pipeline.
  33.3 Monitoring with Metrics Server 🎯 — cAdvisor -> metrics-server ->
       `kubectl top nodes/pods --sort-by`; install + --kubelet-insecure-tls
       (lab). VISUAL: metrics pipeline.
  33.4 Monitoring easypj 🧪🏢 — top pods -n easypj; requests vs limits vs actual;
       intro Prometheus/Grafana (Day-2).
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 33
  [TWO PIPELINES — container output streams & resource metrics]
   LOGS:  📦 easypj (stdout/stderr) ─► /var/log/containers on the node
          ─► kubectl logs [-f][--previous][-c][--since][-l app=easypj]
          ─► (cluster) DaemonSet agent ─► Loki/Elasticsearch
   METRICS: 🤖 kubelet/cAdvisor ─► 📊 Metrics Server ─► kubectl top nodes/pods
   ⚠️ `kubectl top` fails = Metrics Server not installed.
   ⚠️ logs vanish after pod delete -> use --previous for a crashed container.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how container stdout/stderr becomes a node log file; how metrics-
      server scrapes the kubelet summary API; why `top` needs it installed.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): klog levels & structured logging; container log
      rotation (containerLogMaxSize/Files); the resource/custom/external metrics
      APIs (for HPA); the kubelet summary API.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Container output streams (stdout/stderr) become node log files
        (/var/log/containers, /var/log/pods); `kubectl logs` reads them. Logs
        vanish when the pod is deleted — ship them off-node for retention.
      • `kubectl logs` flags are your scalpel: --previous (crashed instance), -f
        (follow), --tail/--since (windowing), -c/--all-containers, -l (by label).
      • Cluster logging = a node-agent DaemonSet (or sidecars) forwarding to a
        store (EFK/Loki); node-level log rotation is controlled by
        containerLogMaxSize/Files.
      • `kubectl top` (nodes/pods) needs metrics-server installed; it scrapes the
        kubelet/cAdvisor summary API. Use it to compare requests vs actual usage.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • `kubectl top` erroring (Metrics API not available) = metrics-server isn't
        installed/ready, not a usage problem.
      • Logs are gone after a pod is deleted; for a crashed container you must use
        --previous BEFORE it's cleaned up.
      • Default logs target one container; in multi-container pods add -c or
        --all-containers or you miss the relevant stream.
      • In labs metrics-server often needs --kubelet-insecure-tls (self-signed
        kubelet certs) or it stays unavailable.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl top pods -n easypj --sort-by=memory` instantly finds the hog.
      • `kubectl logs -l app=easypj --all-containers --tail=50` reads every easypj
        pod at once by label.
      • `--since=1h` / `--since-time=` scopes noisy logs to the incident window.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl logs easypj-web --previous -c web
      kubectl logs -l app=easypj --all-containers -f --tail=100
      kubectl logs easypj-web --since=1h
      kubectl top nodes --sort-by=cpu
      kubectl top pods -n easypj --sort-by=memory
      kubectl apply -f metrics-server.yaml   # (lab: add --kubelet-insecure-tls)
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: install metrics-server, run `kubectl top pods -n easypj`, compare
      each pod's usage to its requests/limits, and identify which pod would
      OOMKill under load.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Read the logs of a container that already crashed.
          A. `kubectl logs <pod> -c <container> --previous` — the prior instance's
          stdout/stderr, which holds the crash reason.
      Q2. Follow logs of every easypj pod by label.
          A. `kubectl logs -l app=easypj --all-containers -f` streams all matching
          pods' containers together.
      Q3. Show only the last hour of a noisy pod's logs.
          A. `kubectl logs <pod> --since=1h` (or --since-time=RFC3339) to window
          the output.
      Q4. Find the node using the most CPU.
          A. `kubectl top nodes --sort-by=cpu` (requires metrics-server).
      Q5. `kubectl top` returns "Metrics API not available". Fix?
          A. Install metrics-server (and in labs add --kubelet-insecure-tls);
          wait for its Deployment to be Ready, then retry.
      Q6. View logs from a specific container in a multi-container pod.
          A. `kubectl logs <pod> -c <containerName>` (or --all-containers for all).
      Q7. Which easypj pod is consuming the most memory?
          A. `kubectl top pods -n easypj --sort-by=memory` — top row is the hog.
      Q8. Where do container logs live on the node?
          A. /var/log/containers/ (symlinks) and /var/log/pods/ — the kubelet's
          on-disk capture of stdout/stderr.
      Q9. A pod's actual usage far exceeds its requests. Why does that matter?
          A. Requests drive scheduling; if usage >> requests the scheduler
          overcommits the node and pods may get throttled/OOMKilled — adjust
          requests/limits.
      Q10. Describe the sidecar logging pattern.
           A. A second container in the pod tails the app's log file (shared
           volume) and writes it to stdout (or forwards it), so the cluster log
           pipeline captures apps that don't log to stdout.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: stdout -> node -> agent -> store; kubelet -> metrics -> top.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 34: Troubleshooting Services & Networking + Scenario Playbook 🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~3h · Domain: Troubleshooting 30% (services & networking).
  [B] WHY: "it can't connect" is the most common (and most layered) failure.
      Plus a consolidated playbook of the 12 must-know exam scenarios. VISUAL:
      the 4-layer network diagnostic.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: debug Service/endpoints/DNS/NetworkPolicy/kube-proxy; apply
      the universal method to 12 canonical scenarios.
  34.1 Service & Endpoint Issues 🎯 — empty endpoints (selector/readiness);
       targetPort; from a temp pod curl the ClusterIP. VISUAL: 4-layer ladder
       (pod->service->DNS->external).
  34.2 DNS Issues 🎯 — CoreDNS down/misconfig; nslookup; ndots.
  34.3 NetworkPolicy & kube-proxy Issues 🎯 — overly-broad deny; missing allow-
       DNS; Flannel ignores policy; kube-proxy mode/health.
  34.4 The CKA Scenario Playbook 🧪🎯 — 12 canonical broken-cluster scenarios,
       each with method + exact commands + fix:
       (1) Node NotReady · (2) Pod Pending · (3) Service no endpoints ·
       (4) CrashLoopBackOff · (5) DNS failure · (6) etcd unhealthy ·
       (7) certs expired · (8) NetworkPolicy blocking · (9) PVC not binding ·
       (10) kubeconfig/context broken · (11) scheduler down · (12) ImagePullBackOff.
  34.5 Methodology Checklist 🩺 — the symptom -> first-command quick table.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 34
  [THE 4-LAYER NETWORK DIAGNOSTIC — isolate top-down]
   ❓ "easypj-web can't reach easypj-db"
   L1 POD     ─ are both pods Running & Ready?           get pod -o wide
   L2 SERVICE ─ does the Service have ENDPOINTS?         get endpoints easypj-db
   │             (empty = selector/label mismatch or not Ready)
   L3 DNS     ─ does the name resolve?                   exec nslookup easypj-db
   │             (fail = CoreDNS / wrong ns / ndots)
   L4 POLICY  ─ is a NetworkPolicy blocking 5432?        get netpol; exec curl
   └─ still broken? kube-proxy mode/health · MTU · conntrack
   🔁 Method everywhere: GATHER ► READ(events/logs) ► ISOLATE layer ► FIX ► VERIFY

  [THE 12-SCENARIO PLAYBOOK — symptom ▶ first command]
   1 NotReady node ▶ ssh;systemctl kubelet   7 certs expired ▶ kubeadm certs
   2 Pod Pending ▶ describe(Events)           8 NetPol block ▶ get netpol;curl
   3 no endpoints ▶ get ep                     9 PVC Pending ▶ describe pvc
   4 CrashLoop ▶ logs --previous              10 kubeconfig ▶ config view/use
   5 DNS fail ▶ nslookup from temp pod        11 scheduler down ▶ -n kube-system
   6 etcd unhealthy ▶ crictl;etcdctl health   12 ImagePull ▶ describe(Events)

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a Service connection actually fails at each layer; reading
      iptables/ipvs rules; conntrack basics.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): read iptables/IPVS rules & conntrack entries by
      hand; eBPF observability (Cilium Hubble); ephemeral debug containers on
      distroless images (`kubectl debug`); node-level crictl/journalctl
      bisection; MTU & asymmetric-routing bugs; DNS ndots/search-domain traps;
      systematic binary-search isolation across the 4 layers.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • "Can't connect" is layered — isolate top-down: L1 pod (Running/Ready?) →
        L2 Service (has endpoints?) → L3 DNS (name resolves?) → L4 NetworkPolicy/
        kube-proxy (traffic allowed/programmed?).
      • Empty endpoints = selector/label mismatch or no Ready pods. Always confirm
        endpoints before suspecting the network.
      • Most "connection" bugs are actually DNS (wrong namespace, CoreDNS down,
        ndots) or a NetworkPolicy with no allow-DNS egress — not the app.
      • The 12-scenario playbook (NotReady, Pending, no-endpoints, CrashLoop, DNS,
        etcd, certs, NetPol, PVC, kubeconfig, scheduler, ImagePull) covers most of
        the 30% domain — drill symptom → first command.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Blaming the app when it's DNS or a NetworkPolicy — test the network path
        before touching code.
      • Empty endpoints silently break a Service even though it "exists".
      • Wrong namespace in an FQDN resolves to nothing; cross-namespace needs
        <svc>.<ns>.
      • Forgetting that a default-deny egress policy also blocks DNS — names stop
        resolving cluster-wide.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Always test from a temp pod: `kubectl run t --rm -it
        --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash` — curl/nslookup from inside the cluster.
      • `kubectl get endpoints <svc>` is the fastest L2 check; empty = stop and
        fix the selector/readiness.
      • Memorize the symptom→first-command table; in the exam, speed of
        FIRST-command beats deep analysis.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get endpoints easypj-db ; kubectl describe svc easypj-db
      kubectl run t --rm -it --image=nicolaka/netshoot -- bash
      kubectl exec t -- nslookup easypj-db.easypj
      kubectl get netpol -A ; kubectl exec t -- curl -m3 easypj-db:5432
      kubectl -n kube-system get pods -l k8s-app=kube-dns
      kubectl get events --sort-by=.lastTimestamp -A
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: run all 12 playbook scenarios under a timer, naming each symptom's
      first diagnostic command and the fix before you type it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. A Service returns no traffic; endpoints are empty. Fix?
          A. The selector matches no Ready pod. Align the Service selector with
          the pods' labels and ensure the readiness probe passes.
      Q2. Pods can't resolve easypj-db. Diagnose.
          A. From a temp pod: nslookup kubernetes.default (DNS up?), then
          easypj-db / easypj-db.easypj (right ns?); check CoreDNS pods/logs.
      Q3. web→db worked, now it's blocked after adding policies. Cause?
          A. A NetworkPolicy selects db without allowing web (or a default-deny
          with no allow rule). Add an ingress allow web→db:5432.
      Q4. Suspect kube-proxy. What do you check?
          A. kube-proxy DaemonSet pods Running, its mode (iptables/IPVS), and its
          logs; a broken kube-proxy breaks Service VIPs while pod IPs still work.
      Q5. A Service VIP is unreachable but the pod IP works. Meaning?
          A. The app is fine; the problem is Service-layer (endpoints/kube-proxy/
          targetPort), not the pod or app.
      Q6. Connections refused though the Service exists. Common config bug?
          A. targetPort doesn't match the container's listening port — fix the
          Service's targetPort to the real containerPort.
      Q7. From namespace web, reach Service db in namespace data. Name to use?
          A. `db.data` (or db.data.svc.cluster.local) — a bare `db` only resolves
          within web.
      Q8. An Ingress isn't routing. First checks?
          A. Is an Ingress controller installed/running? Is ADDRESS populated? Are
          the host/path/backend and ingressClassName correct?
      Q9. How do you peek at the dataplane rules for a Service?
          A. On a node: `iptables-save | grep <clusterIP>` (iptables mode) or
          `ipvsadm -Ln` (IPVS), and conntrack -L for live connections.
      Q10. Give the universal method you apply to every scenario.
           A. GATHER (get/describe) → READ (Events/logs) → ISOLATE the failing
           layer → FIX → VERIFY — the same loop regardless of symptom.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the universal 5-step method over the 4 network layers.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 9 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ─────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ ONE method everywhere: get -> describe(Events!) -> logs(--previous) -> │
  │    exec -> isolate layer -> fix -> verify.                               │
  │ ✅ Work the layers: pod -> node/kubelet -> control plane (crictl/        │
  │    journalctl when apiserver down) -> network (endpoints/DNS/policy).    │
  │ ✅ Observe with logs (output streams) and `kubectl top` (needs metrics-  │
  │    server); read Events before guessing.                                │
  │ ✅ Memorize the 12-scenario playbook — it covers ~the whole 30% domain.  │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: 30% — the biggest payoff; speed + method win it.                │
  │ 🔑 describe · logs -p · get events --sort-by · crictl · top · auth can-i │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 9 VISUAL SUMMARY]: the master decision tree — symptom to fix.

PART 10: PRODUCTION & HIGH AVAILABILITY                                 🔴 🎯🏢
  Chapters 35-36  ·  HA control plane competency (25%)  ·  "No single point"
  What you'll build: an understanding of (and the steps for) a 3-node HA
  control plane, and how easypj's CKA skills translate to managed cloud
  Kubernetes (EKS/GKE/AKS).
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 10 INTRO]
  A one-node control plane is one crash from a frozen cluster. This part makes
  the cluster itself resilient (HA control plane + etcd quorum + load-balanced
  apiservers) and then shows how everything you've learned maps onto managed
  cloud Kubernetes, where the provider runs the control plane for you.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 10 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch35 HA (self-managed resilience) ─▶ Ch36 CLOUD (managed EKS/GKE/AKS)    │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 10 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: take easypj's cluster from 1 control plane to 3 behind
  a load balancer with quorum etcd; then map the same easypj deployment onto a
  managed cluster (cloud LB Services, cloud CSI storageclasses, IAM identity).

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 35: High Availability Clusters                               🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% (HA control plane).
  [B] WHY: production clusters must survive a control-plane node loss. VISUAL:
      single vs HA control plane (reuse+augment cka_1).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: HA architecture; stacked vs external etcd; HA kubeadm setup;
      quorum & leader election; load balancer in front of apiservers.
  35.1 HA Architecture 🎯 — >=3 control-plane nodes + LB; why odd numbers.
  35.2 Stacked vs External etcd 🎯 — trade-offs table.
  35.3 HA Setup with kubeadm 🎯 — `--control-plane-endpoint` + `--upload-certs`;
       join control-plane nodes; join workers.
  35.4 etcd Quorum & Fault Tolerance 🎯 — N/2+1; 3 tolerate 1, 5 tolerate 2.
  35.5 Leader Election 🔧 — scheduler/controller-manager active-passive via Lease.
  35.6 Load Balancer for HA 🏢 — HAProxy/nginx/keepalived in front of :6443.
  35.7 easypj HA Checklist 🧪 — replicas across nodes (anti-affinity) + PDB +
       multi-AZ storage.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 35
  [HIGH AVAILABILITY — no single point of failure]
                      🌐 Load Balancer (:6443, control-plane-endpoint)
                     ╱            │            ╲
            📡 apiserver   📡 apiserver   📡 apiserver
            🗄️ etcd-0  ◄──►🗄️ etcd-1  ◄──►🗄️ etcd-2     quorum = 2 of 3
            (CP node 1)   (CP node 2)    (CP node 3)
                     ╲            │            ╱
                        💪 worker nodes (easypj pods, spread + PDB)
   Quorum math: 3 ⇒ tolerate 1 · 5 ⇒ tolerate 2 (ALWAYS odd).
   scheduler & controller-manager = active-passive via Lease (leader election).
   Build: kubeadm init --control-plane-endpoint <LB> --upload-certs, then join
   more control-plane nodes, then workers.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how the LB + control-plane-endpoint keeps a stable apiserver
      address; quorum loss behavior (read-only/unavailable).
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): etcd learner members & promotion; LB health checks
      on /readyz; certSANs for the LB endpoint; lease tuning; rebuilding a failed
      control-plane node; multi-AZ etcd latency.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • HA = ≥3 control-plane nodes behind a load balancer (the stable
        --control-plane-endpoint on :6443) + a quorum etcd cluster. Use ODD
        counts.
      • etcd quorum is N/2+1: 3 members tolerate 1 failure, 5 tolerate 2. Lose
        quorum and the cluster goes read-only/unavailable until it's restored.
      • apiservers are active-active (the LB spreads traffic); scheduler and
        controller-manager are active-PASSIVE, electing one leader via a Lease.
      • kubeadm builds it: `kubeadm init --control-plane-endpoint <LB>
        --upload-certs`, then join the other control-plane nodes, then workers.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Even numbers of etcd members buy NO extra fault tolerance (4 still only
        tolerates 1) and raise split-brain risk — stay odd.
      • Losing quorum (e.g. 2 of 3 down) makes the API read-only/unavailable; the
        priority is restoring a quorum member, not adding workers.
      • Forgetting --upload-certs means joining control-plane nodes can't fetch the
        certs and the join fails.
      • The LB must health-check the apiserver (/readyz) and the cert must include
        the LB endpoint in certSANs, or clients reject it.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `etcdctl member list -w table` (with etcd certs) shows the member set and
        who's healthy — first check on an HA etcd issue.
      • Spread easypj with podAntiAffinity + a PDB so a node loss never drops all
        replicas.
      • Reason quorum quickly: tolerated failures = floor(N/2). 3→1, 5→2, 7→3.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubeadm init --control-plane-endpoint "lb.easypj:6443" --upload-certs
      kubeadm join lb.easypj:6443 --control-plane --certificate-key <key>   # CP join
      kubeadm join lb.easypj:6443 ...                                       # worker join
      etcdctl member list -w table --endpoints=... --cacert=... --cert=... --key=...
      kubectl -n kube-system get lease kube-scheduler kube-controller-manager
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: on a 3-control-plane cluster, reason through (and if possible
      simulate) losing 1 node (still works) vs 2 nodes (quorum lost → API
      read-only), and identify the recovery step.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. How many control-plane nodes for HA and why odd?
          A. ≥3, odd, so etcd can form quorum (N/2+1) and tolerate failures without
          split-brain; even counts add risk, not tolerance.
      Q2. Stacked vs external etcd — the trade-off?
          A. Stacked (etcd on the CP nodes) is simpler and fewer machines but
          couples etcd to CP failures; external etcd isolates the datastore for
          resilience at higher ops cost.
      Q3. With 5 etcd members, how many failures are tolerated?
          A. 2 (quorum = 3). General rule: floor(N/2).
      Q4. What does --control-plane-endpoint provide?
          A. A single stable address (the LB) all components/kubeconfigs use, so
          any apiserver can serve and nodes survive a CP node loss.
      Q5. How do additional control-plane nodes get the certs to join?
          A. `kubeadm init --upload-certs` stores them; CP joins use
          --certificate-key to fetch and install them.
      Q6. How are scheduler and controller-manager made HA?
          A. They run on every CP node but only the elected leader acts
          (active-passive) via a Lease object; on leader loss another takes over.
      Q7. What's the load balancer's role?
          A. It fronts the apiservers on :6443, health-checks them, and gives
          clients one endpoint — distributing API traffic and surviving a CP loss.
      Q8. 2 of 3 control planes are down. Cluster state?
          A. etcd lost quorum → the API is read-only/unavailable. Restore a third
          (or recover a member) to regain quorum before anything else.
      Q9. Check the etcd members on an HA cluster.
          A. `etcdctl member list -w table` (and `endpoint health`) with the etcd
          CA/cert/key.
      Q10. How do you make easypj itself HA on top of the cluster?
           A. Multiple replicas spread with podAntiAffinity across nodes/zones, a
           PodDisruptionBudget, and multi-AZ storage so no single failure drops it.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: 3 control planes + LB + quorum etcd.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 36: Cloud Kubernetes — EKS, GKE, AKS                         🟡 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Beyond-exam (how CKA skills apply in the cloud).
  [B] WHY: most real jobs run managed K8s; know what changes and what doesn't.
      VISUAL: managed vs self-managed responsibility split.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: managed vs self-managed; which CKA concepts still apply;
      cloud storage/networking/identity; deploy easypj to a managed cluster;
      cluster autoscaler.
  36.1 Managed vs Self-Managed 🏢 — provider runs the control plane; you keep
       workloads/RBAC/networking/storage.
  36.2 CKA Concepts in the Cloud 🏢 — RBAC, Services, PV/PVC, NetworkPolicy all
       still apply; what's abstracted away (etcd, apiserver upgrades).
  36.3 Cloud Storage & Networking 🏢 — CSI storageclasses, LoadBalancer
       Services, cloud Ingress/Gateway.
  36.4 Cloud Identity 🏢 — IRSA/Workload Identity binding SAs to cloud IAM.
  36.5 Deploy easypj to the cloud 🧪 — same manifests, cloud storageclass + LB.
  36.6 Cluster Autoscaler 🏢 — node autoscaling vs HPA pod autoscaling.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 36
  [MANAGED vs SELF-MANAGED — who owns what]
   ┌──────────────── CLOUD PROVIDER (EKS/GKE/AKS) ───────────────┐
   │ 📡 apiserver  🗄️ etcd  📋 scheduler  🔄 ctrl-mgr  (managed!) │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   ┌──────────────────── YOU still own ──────────────────────────┐
   │ workloads · RBAC · NetworkPolicy · PV/PVC · ingress/gateway  │
   │ LoadBalancer Svc -> cloud LB · storageClass -> cloud CSI     │
   │ SA -> cloud IAM (IRSA / Workload Identity)                   │
   └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
   📖 Same easypj manifests; only storageClass/LB/identity glue changes.
   Cluster Autoscaler scales NODES; HPA scales PODS.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how cloud controllers provision LBs & disks; how IRSA exchanges a
      projected SA token for cloud creds.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): IRSA / Workload-Identity token exchange; cloud LB
      annotations (internal/NLB); per-cloud CSI drivers; managed node pools;
      Cluster Autoscaler vs Karpenter; private clusters & endpoint access.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Managed K8s (EKS/GKE/AKS) runs the control plane (apiserver/etcd/scheduler/
        controller-manager) for you; you still own workloads, RBAC, NetworkPolicy,
        PV/PVC, and ingress/gateway.
      • Your CKA skills transfer directly — the same easypj manifests apply. Only
        the GLUE changes: storageClass → cloud CSI, LoadBalancer Service → cloud
        LB, ServiceAccount → cloud IAM (IRSA / Workload Identity).
      • Two different autoscalers: HPA scales PODS (on metrics); Cluster Autoscaler
        (or Karpenter) scales NODES when pods can't be scheduled.
      • etcd backups, apiserver upgrades, and control-plane HA become the
        provider's job — but you still upgrade node pools and manage app rollout.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Don't assume on-prem behavior — LoadBalancer Services provision (billable)
        cloud LBs; leaving them around costs money.
      • StorageClass names/parameters differ per cloud (gp3 vs pd-ssd vs managed-
        csi); don't hardcode an on-prem class name.
      • IRSA/Workload Identity must be configured or pods can't get cloud creds —
        a SA alone isn't enough.
      • Node-pool upgrades are yours to drive even though the control plane is
        managed.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Keep cloud-specific bits (storageClass, LB annotations, identity) in a
        Kustomize overlay so the base easypj manifests stay portable.
      • Use an internal LB annotation for private Services to avoid exposing them
        publicly (and to cut cost).
      • Right-size with HPA for pods + Cluster Autoscaler for nodes rather than
        over-provisioning fixed capacity.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get sc                          # cloud CSI storageclasses
      kubectl get svc easypj-web -o wide      # EXTERNAL-IP from the cloud LB
      kubectl get nodes -L topology.kubernetes.io/zone   # spread across AZs
      eksctl/gcloud/az ... update cluster     # provider CLI for node pools/upgrade
      kubectl annotate svc easypj-web service.beta.kubernetes.io/aws-load-balancer-internal=true
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: take the easypj manifests and map each object to its managed-cloud
      equivalent — storageClass→cloud CSI, Service type LoadBalancer→cloud LB,
      ServiceAccount→IRSA/Workload Identity — noting what stays identical.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. In managed K8s, what does the provider own vs you?
          A. Provider: control plane (apiserver/etcd/scheduler/controller-manager,
          its upgrades/HA/backup). You: workloads, RBAC, NetworkPolicy, PV/PVC,
          ingress, node pools.
      Q2. Which CKA concepts still apply in the cloud?
          A. Nearly all — Pods/Deployments/Services, RBAC, PV/PVC, NetworkPolicy,
          probes, scheduling. The control-plane internals are abstracted away.
      Q3. How do you get an external IP for easypj in the cloud?
          A. A Service of type LoadBalancer; the cloud controller provisions a
          cloud LB and populates EXTERNAL-IP.
      Q4. Why not hardcode storageClass: standard across clouds?
          A. Class names/provisioners differ per provider; use the cloud's CSI
          class (or templatize it) so PVCs bind correctly.
      Q5. What problem do IRSA / Workload Identity solve?
          A. They bind a Kubernetes ServiceAccount to a cloud IAM role, exchanging
          the pod's projected token for cloud credentials — no static keys in pods.
      Q6. Cluster Autoscaler vs HPA?
          A. HPA adds/removes POD replicas based on metrics; Cluster Autoscaler
          adds/removes NODES when pods are unschedulable or nodes are idle.
      Q7. Deploy easypj to a managed cluster — what changes in the manifests?
          A. Mostly just the glue: storageClass, LoadBalancer/Ingress annotations,
          and SA↔IAM identity; the Deployments/Services stay the same.
      Q8. How are upgrades handled on managed K8s?
          A. The provider upgrades the control plane; you upgrade node pools
          (often a managed rolling replace) and validate workloads.
      Q9. What is a node pool/group?
          A. A managed set of identical worker nodes (instance type, size,
          autoscaling) you can scale/upgrade independently.
      Q10. Name two cloud cost levers for a K8s workload.
           A. Right-size requests + HPA/Cluster Autoscaler (avoid idle nodes), and
           use internal LBs / spot/preemptible nodes where appropriate.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: easypj on managed K8s, responsibility split drawn.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 10 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ HA = >=3 control planes behind a load balancer + quorum etcd (odd      │
  │    counts; N/2+1); scheduler/controller-manager are active-passive.      │
  │ ✅ Stacked vs external etcd is a resilience/ops trade-off.               │
  │ ✅ Managed cloud K8s runs the control plane for you; your CKA workload/  │
  │    RBAC/networking/storage skills transfer directly.                    │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: HA concepts (quorum, leader election) are tested even though you│
  │    won't build a full HA cluster on the clock.                          │
  │ 🔑 kubeadm init --control-plane-endpoint --upload-certs · etcdctl member │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 10 VISUAL SUMMARY]: resilient control plane -> same skills in the cloud.
