PART 11: PACKAGING, DELIVERY & EXTENSIBILITY                            🔴 🎯🏢
  Chapters 37-39  ·  Helm/Kustomize + CRDs/Operators are 25% competencies
  What you'll build: easypj packaged as a Helm chart AND a Kustomize overlay,
  extended with a Custom Resource + operator, and delivered continuously via
  GitOps.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 11 INTRO]
  Hand-applying YAML doesn't scale. This part covers the official 2025 exam
  additions — Helm & Kustomize for templated/overlaid installs, and CRDs &
  operators for extending Kubernetes itself — then the Day-2 delivery layer
  (CI/CD & GitOps) that ships easypj automatically and safely.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 11 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch37 PACKAGE (Helm + Kustomize) ─▶ Ch38 EXTEND (CRDs + Operators)        │
  │                     └──────────────┬──────────────┘                     │
  │                                    ▼                                     │
  │                 Ch39 DELIVER (CI/CD + GitOps: ArgoCD/Flux)               │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 11 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: turn easypj's raw manifests into a Helm chart (values
  for dev/prod) AND a Kustomize base+overlays; define an easypj CRD + a tiny
  operator concept; wire a GitOps pipeline so a git push deploys easypj.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 37: Helm & Kustomize                                         🟡 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% (install components). NEW.
  [B] WHY: official exam competency — "use Helm AND Kustomize to install
      cluster components." VISUAL: Helm (template) vs Kustomize (overlay) card.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: Helm chart anatomy & lifecycle; values & releases; Kustomize
      bases/overlays/patches; choose between them.
  37.1 Helm Basics 🎯 — 📖 chart/release/values/repo; install/upgrade/rollback/
       uninstall; templating.
       helm repo add bitnami https://charts.bitnami.com/bitnami
       helm install easypj ./chart -n easypj -f prod.yaml   # release "easypj"
       helm upgrade easypj ./chart -f prod.yaml --set replicas=4
       helm rollback easypj 1                                # revert to revision 1
       helm uninstall easypj -n easypj
       helm template ./chart -f prod.yaml                    # preview (no apply)
  37.2 Helm Chart Anatomy 🎯 — Chart.yaml, values.yaml, templates/, _helpers;
       package easypj as a chart.
       chart/
        ├ Chart.yaml          # name/version/appVersion
        ├ values.yaml         # defaults: replicas: 2, image: easypj:v1
        ├ templates/
        │   ├ deployment.yaml # spec.replicas: {{ .Values.replicas }}
        │   ├ service.yaml
        │   └ _helpers.tpl    # named templates (labels, fullname)
        └ charts/             # subchart dependencies
  37.3 Kustomize Basics 🎯 — 📖 base + overlays; kustomization.yaml; patches;
       `kubectl apply -k`.
       base/kustomization.yaml:
         resources: [deployment.yaml, service.yaml]
       overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml:
         resources: ["../../base"]
         replicas: [{ name: easypj-web, count: 3 }]
         images:   [{ name: easypj, newTag: v2 }]
         patches:  [{ path: prod-env.yaml }]      # DEBUG=False, ALLOWED_HOSTS
       # apply:  kubectl apply -k overlays/prod   (preview: kubectl kustomize ...)
  37.4 Patches & Generators 🎯 — strategic-merge & JSON6902 patches;
       configMapGenerator/secretGenerator; images/replicas overrides.
  37.5 Helm vs Kustomize 🧭 — templating vs overlaying; when to use which / both.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 37
  [HELM vs KUSTOMIZE — two ways to ship easypj per environment]
   HELM (templating + releases)          KUSTOMIZE (overlays, no templates)
   chart/                                base/
    ├ Chart.yaml                          ├ deployment.yaml  service.yaml
    ├ values.yaml  (defaults)             └ kustomization.yaml
    └ templates/  {{ .Values.replicas }} overlays/
   helm install easypj ./chart \          ├ dev/   (patch: replicas=1, DEBUG)
     -f prod.yaml   (override per env)     └ prod/  (patch: replicas=3, image)
                                          kubectl apply -k overlays/prod
   ┌──────────┬───────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
   │ Helm     │ templated, versioned  │ install 3rd-party components│
   │          │ releases, rollbacks   │ (charts), values per env    │
   │ Kustomize│ template-FREE base +  │ your own manifests, env     │
   │          │ overlays/patches      │ overlays, GitOps-friendly   │
   └──────────┴───────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘
   💡 Preview first: `helm template ./chart` · `kubectl kustomize overlays/prod`.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how Helm renders templates client-side then applies; how Kustomize
      builds a resource list with no templating; release storage as Secrets.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): Helm hooks & weights; subcharts/dependencies;
      values precedence & --set; release history/rollback storage; Kustomize
      components/replacements/transformers; `helm template | kustomize`.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Helm packages manifests as a templated, versioned CHART; installing it
        creates a RELEASE you can upgrade/rollback. values.yaml (+ -f/--set
        overrides) parameterize per environment.
      • Kustomize is template-FREE: a base + overlays that patch it (replicas,
        images, env). Apply with `kubectl apply -k` — it's built into kubectl.
      • Use Helm to install 3rd-party components (charts) and when you need
        releases/rollbacks; use Kustomize for your own manifests and GitOps. They
        can even compose (`helm template | kustomize`).
      • Always PREVIEW before applying: `helm template ./chart` and `kubectl
        kustomize overlays/prod` render the final YAML without touching the cluster.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Helm release-name collisions in a namespace fail the install — names are
        unique per namespace.
      • An overlay's kustomization must reference the base under `resources:`;
        forgetting it produces an empty/partial build.
      • Helm CRDs in the crds/ dir install once and aren't upgraded/deleted with
        the release — manage CRD upgrades separately.
      • `--set` overrides win over `-f values`, which win over chart defaults; mix
        them up and you ship the wrong config.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `helm template` / `kubectl kustomize` to eyeball the rendered manifests is
        the fastest way to catch a templating/patch mistake.
      • `helm history easypj` then `helm rollback easypj <rev>` reverts a bad
        upgrade in one step.
      • `helm upgrade --install` is idempotent — installs if absent, upgrades if
        present.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      helm install easypj ./chart -n easypj -f prod.yaml
      helm upgrade --install easypj ./chart --set replicas=4
      helm history easypj ; helm rollback easypj 1
      helm template ./chart -f prod.yaml
      kubectl apply -k overlays/prod
      kubectl kustomize overlays/prod          # preview
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: package easypj as a Helm chart and install it with a prod values
      file, then ALSO build a Kustomize base + dev/prod overlays (replicas/image/
      DEBUG differ) and `apply -k` prod — compare the two approaches.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Install, upgrade, then roll back an easypj Helm release.
          A. `helm install easypj ./chart -f prod.yaml` → `helm upgrade easypj
          ./chart --set replicas=4` → `helm rollback easypj 1` (see `helm history`).
      Q2. Override a chart value per environment. Precedence?
          A. -f values file or --set on the command line; precedence is --set >
          -f > chart defaults (values.yaml).
      Q3. Describe a Helm chart's anatomy.
          A. Chart.yaml (metadata), values.yaml (defaults), templates/ (manifests
          with {{ .Values }}), _helpers.tpl (named templates), charts/ (subcharts).
      Q4. Create a Kustomize overlay that sets prod replicas and image tag.
          A. overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml with resources: ["../../base"],
          replicas: [{name, count: 3}], images: [{name, newTag: v2}].
      Q5. What is a strategic-merge patch?
          A. A partial manifest merged by field into the base resource (e.g. set
          env or resources on a container) — the default Kustomize patch style.
      Q6. What does configMapGenerator do?
          A. Generates a ConfigMap from literals/files and appends a content hash
          to its name, so changing config triggers a rolling update automatically.
      Q7. Set replicas differently for dev vs prod without templating.
          A. Use Kustomize overlays — base defines the Deployment, each overlay
          patches replicas (and image/env) for its environment.
      Q8. Preview what Helm/Kustomize will apply.
          A. `helm template ./chart -f prod.yaml` or `kubectl kustomize
          overlays/prod` — renders final YAML without applying.
      Q9. Helm vs Kustomize — when each?
          A. Helm for templated/versioned releases and 3rd-party charts; Kustomize
          for template-free overlays of your own manifests and GitOps workflows.
      Q10. Install a cluster component (e.g. ingress-nginx) via Helm.
           A. `helm repo add` the chart repo, then `helm install ingress-nginx
           ingress-nginx/ingress-nginx -n ingress-nginx --create-namespace`.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: raw YAML -> Helm chart / Kustomize overlay -> cluster.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 38: Extending Kubernetes — CRDs & Operators                  🔴 🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Domain: Cluster Arch 25% (CRDs/operators). NEW.
  [B] WHY: official competency — "understand CRDs; install and configure
      operators." VISUAL: CRD + controller = operator diagram.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: register CRDs; create custom resources; the operator pattern;
      install/configure an operator; RBAC for CRs; API aggregation overview.
  38.1 Custom Resource Definitions 🎯 — group/versions/scope/schema (OpenAPIv3);
       `metadata.name = plural.group`; served/storage.
       apiVersion: apiextensions.k8s.io/v1
       kind: CustomResourceDefinition
       metadata:
         name: easypjapps.apps.easypj.io        # MUST be plural.group
       spec:
         group: apps.easypj.io
         scope: Namespaced
         names:
           plural: easypjapps
           singular: easypjapp
           kind: EasypjApp
           shortNames: ["eapp"]
         versions:
         - name: v1
           served: true
           storage: true                         # exactly one version is storage
           schema:
             openAPIV3Schema:
               type: object
               properties:
                 spec:
                   type: object
                   properties:
                     replicas: { type: integer, minimum: 1 }
                     version:  { type: string }
       # now: kubectl get easypjapp  (eapp) works as a first-class API type
  38.2 The Operator Pattern 🎯 — CRD + controller reconcile loop; real operators
       (cert-manager, Prometheus, postgres). VISUAL: watch->compare->act.
  38.3 Installing & Configuring Operators 🎯 — via Helm/manifests/OLM; verify CRDs.
  38.4 API Aggregation Layer 🔧 — when to aggregate vs CRD (e.g. metrics-server).
  38.5 RBAC for Custom Resources 🎯 — apiGroups: ["group"], resources:
       ["plural","plural/status"].
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 38
  [CRD + CONTROLLER = OPERATOR — extend Kubernetes itself]
   1) Install CRD (kind: EasypjApp, group apps.easypj.io) ─► a NEW API type
        ▼  kubectl get easypjapp  now works
   2) Create a custom resource:  EasypjApp{ replicas: 3, version: v2 }
        ▼  stored in etcd at /registry/apps.easypj.io/easypjapps/...
   3) The OPERATOR (a controller) runs the reconcile loop:
        WATCH ─► COMPARE (spec vs cluster) ─► ACT (make Deploy/Svc/...) ─►
        update STATUS ─► repeat
   📖 Real operators: cert-manager, Prometheus, postgres-operator, ArgoCD.
   ⚠️ CRD metadata.name MUST be plural.group · add RBAC for the new resource.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a CRD adds a REST path & /registry key; how the reconcile loop
      + informers drive an operator; finalizers.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): CRD versions & conversion webhooks; structural
      schemas + x-kubernetes-* & CEL validation rules; status/scale subresources;
      additionalPrinterColumns; OLM; controller-runtime reconcile patterns.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • A CRD registers a NEW API type (group/version/kind + OpenAPIv3 schema).
        Once applied, `kubectl get/create <kind>` works like any built-in
        resource and instances are stored in etcd.
      • An OPERATOR = a CRD + a controller running the reconcile loop: WATCH the
        custom resources → COMPARE desired spec vs live state → ACT (create/update
        Deployments/Services/…) → update status → repeat.
      • Real operators (cert-manager, Prometheus, postgres-operator, ArgoCD)
        install via Helm/manifests/OLM; the exam expects you to install/configure
        one and verify its CRDs are present.
      • The API Aggregation Layer is the OTHER extension path — register an
        extension apiserver (e.g. metrics-server) when you need custom storage/
        logic a CRD can't express.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • CRD metadata.name MUST be exactly <plural>.<group> (e.g.
        easypjapps.apps.easypj.io) or it's rejected.
      • Exactly one version may have storage: true; multiple → invalid CRD.
      • A missing/loose schema lets bad data in; define an openAPIV3Schema (and
        ideally validation rules).
      • Forgetting RBAC for the new resource means users/controllers get
        Forbidden — grant verbs on the CRD's apiGroup + plural (and /status).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl get crd` lists installed types; `kubectl explain easypjapp.spec`
        works once the schema is registered.
      • `kubectl api-resources | grep <group>` confirms the new kind and its short
        names.
      • After installing an operator, verify its CRDs exist BEFORE creating custom
        resources, or the apply fails with "no matches for kind".
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl apply -f easypj-crd.yaml
      kubectl get crd ; kubectl api-resources --api-group=apps.easypj.io
      kubectl explain easypjapp.spec
      kubectl get easypjapp -A          # (eapp)
      kubectl create clusterrole eapp-admin --verb=get,list,watch,create,update,delete \
        --resource=easypjapps.apps.easypj.io
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: apply the easypjapp CRD, create an EasypjApp instance with
      replicas/version, list it (`kubectl get eapp`), and `kubectl explain` its
      schema — then add a Role granting access to it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Create a CRD for a new kind EasypjApp. Key fields?
          A. apiextensions.k8s.io/v1 CRD; metadata.name = easypjapps.apps.easypj.io;
          spec.group/scope/names + versions[].schema (openAPIV3Schema); one version
          storage:true.
      Q2. Create an instance of that custom resource.
          A. A manifest apiVersion: apps.easypj.io/v1, kind: EasypjApp, with
          spec.replicas/version; apply it like any object.
      Q3. List all installed custom resource types.
          A. `kubectl get crd` (and `kubectl api-resources` to see kinds + short
          names).
      Q4. Explain the operator pattern.
          A. A controller watches custom resources and reconciles desired spec to
          actual cluster state (creating/updating real objects), updating status —
          CRD + controller = operator.
      Q5. Install an operator. General steps?
          A. Apply its CRDs + controller (Helm/manifests/OLM), verify CRDs with
          `kubectl get crd`, then create its custom resources to configure it.
      Q6. Grant RBAC to manage a custom resource.
          A. A Role/ClusterRole with apiGroups: ["apps.easypj.io"], resources:
          ["easypjapps","easypjapps/status"], the needed verbs, bound to the subject.
      Q7. CRD vs API aggregation — when each?
          A. CRD for declarative custom objects stored by K8s (most cases);
          aggregation when you need a custom apiserver with its own storage/logic
          (e.g. metrics-server).
      Q8. What does a /status subresource give you?
          A. It splits spec and status so controllers update status without
          racing user spec edits, and enables separate RBAC on status.
      Q9. State the CRD naming rule.
          A. metadata.name must equal <plural>.<group>; the kind is CamelCase and
          plural/singular/shortNames live under spec.names.
      Q10. Inspect a custom resource's fields like a built-in.
           A. `kubectl explain <kind>.spec` (works from the registered
           openAPIV3Schema) and `kubectl get <kind> -o yaml`.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: CRD + operator extending easypj's API.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 39: CI/CD & GitOps                                           🟡 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🟡 · ⏱️ ~2h · Beyond-exam (how easypj actually ships).
  [B] WHY: real teams don't `kubectl apply` by hand; git is the source of truth.
      VISUAL: CI (build/test/push) -> CD/GitOps (sync) pipeline.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: a K8s CI/CD pipeline; GitOps with ArgoCD/Flux; deploy easypj
      via GitOps; combine with Helm/Kustomize.
  39.1 CI/CD Pipeline for K8s 🏢 — build image -> scan -> push -> update manifest.
  39.2 GitOps with ArgoCD/Flux 🏢 — git = desired state; controller syncs;
       drift detection/self-heal.
  39.3 easypj via GitOps 🧪 — an Application pointing at the easypj repo/overlay.
  39.4 Helm/Kustomize in GitOps 🏢 — render from chart/overlay in the pipeline.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 39
  [CI/CD + GITOPS — git is the source of truth]
   👩‍💻 git push (easypj code)
        ▼  CI: build -> test -> 🔍 scan -> push image easypj:v2
        ▼  bump image tag in the manifests/overlay repo
   📦 Git repo (desired state)
        ▼  CD/GitOps controller (ArgoCD / Flux) WATCHES the repo
   ☸️ Cluster  ── controller SYNCS: live state -> matches git
        └─ drift? manual kubectl edit gets REVERTED (self-heal)
   ⚠️ don't store raw Secrets in git (sealed-secrets / external-secrets).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how a GitOps controller diff/syncs vs the live cluster; image
      updater patterns.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): app-of-apps; sync waves & hooks; auto-prune &
      drift detection; progressive delivery (Argo Rollouts/Flagger); image
      automation; GitOps secret management; projects & multi-tenancy.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • CI builds/tests/scans/pushes the image and bumps the manifest; CD/GitOps
        SYNCS the cluster to the manifests in git. Git is the single source of
        truth for desired state.
      • A GitOps controller (ArgoCD/Flux) continuously diffs git vs live and
        applies the difference; manual `kubectl edit` drift is detected and
        self-healed (reverted) back to git.
      • easypj ships as an Application/Kustomization pointing at the repo/overlay;
        Helm or Kustomize renders the actual manifests in the pipeline.
      • Rollbacks are just `git revert` — the controller syncs the cluster back to
        the previous known-good commit.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Hand-editing live objects is futile under GitOps — the controller reverts
        them on the next sync. Change git instead.
      • Never commit raw Secrets to git — use sealed-secrets / external-secrets so
        only encrypted/ref data is stored.
      • Resource ordering (CRDs before CRs, namespaces before objects) needs sync
        waves/hooks, or the first sync fails.
      • Auto-prune deletes objects removed from git — powerful but can wipe things
        if the repo is wrong.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Point the GitOps app at a Kustomize overlay (or Helm chart) so per-env
        config is just a different path/values file.
      • Enable self-heal + prune deliberately; start in a "manual sync" mode to
        observe diffs before automating.
      • Treat the image tag bump as a git commit — that commit is your deployment
        record and your rollback point.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      argocd app create easypj --repo <git> --path overlays/prod --dest-namespace easypj
      argocd app sync easypj ; argocd app get easypj
      flux create kustomization easypj --source=easypj-repo --path=./overlays/prod
      git revert <bad-commit>        # rollback = revert + auto-sync
      kubectl apply -k overlays/prod # what the controller does for you
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: sketch (or wire) the easypj git→cluster flow — CI pushes
      easypj:v2 and bumps the overlay; the GitOps controller syncs it; then make a
      manual `kubectl scale` change and watch it get reverted to git.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Difference between CI and CD here?
          A. CI = build/test/scan/push the image (+ bump manifest); CD/GitOps =
          sync the cluster to the manifests. CI produces artifacts; CD deploys them.
      Q2. State the core GitOps principle.
          A. Git holds the declarative desired state; a controller continuously
          reconciles the live cluster to match it (no manual kubectl apply).
      Q3. What is an ArgoCD Application?
          A. An object that points at a git repo/path (and target cluster/ns) and
          tells ArgoCD what to sync — the unit of GitOps deployment.
      Q4. How does GitOps handle configuration drift?
          A. It detects the live/git difference and self-heals by re-applying git,
          reverting unauthorized manual changes.
      Q5. Use a Helm chart in GitOps. How?
          A. The controller renders the chart (with values) from the repo and
          applies the output — ArgoCD/Flux both support Helm sources.
      Q6. How are Secrets handled in GitOps?
          A. Store only encrypted/sealed secrets (sealed-secrets) or references
          (external-secrets) in git; the controller/operator materializes the real
          Secret in-cluster.
      Q7. What are sync waves for?
          A. Ordering — apply CRDs/namespaces before the resources that depend on
          them, using wave annotations/hooks so the sync doesn't fail.
      Q8. Roll back a bad deploy under GitOps.
          A. `git revert` the offending commit; the controller syncs the cluster
          back to the previous state automatically.
      Q9. How does an image update reach the cluster?
          A. CI (or an image-updater) commits the new tag to the manifest repo;
          the GitOps controller syncs the changed Deployment.
      Q10. You edited a live Deployment and it changed back. Why?
           A. GitOps self-heal reverted your manual edit to match git — make the
           change in the repo instead.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: git push -> pipeline -> easypj live.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 11 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Helm = templated, versioned releases (values per env); Kustomize =    │
  │    template-free base + overlays/patches; both can install components.   │
  │ ✅ CRDs add new API types; CRD + a controller = an operator (reconcile   │
  │    loop). Install operators via Helm/manifests; add RBAC for CRs.        │
  │ ✅ GitOps makes git the source of truth; the controller syncs & self-    │
  │    heals drift.                                                          │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: Helm, Kustomize, CRDs & operators are 2025 Cluster-Arch topics. │
  │ 🔑 helm install/upgrade · kubectl apply -k · get crd · kubectl explain   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 11 VISUAL SUMMARY]: package -> extend -> deliver easypj.

PART 12: HARDENING & POLICY                                             🔴 🏢
  Chapters 40-42  ·  Beyond-exam depth + admission/policy mastery
  What you'll build: a hardened easypj namespace governed by layered policy —
  Pod Security Standards, resource governance, CEL admission policies, and an
  external policy engine — plus the admission-webhook machinery underneath.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 12 INTRO]
  Security isn't one switch; it's layers. This part stacks them: harden the
  cluster surface (apiserver flags, audit, supply chain), then ENFORCE rules
  automatically with Pod Security Admission, ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (CEL,
  no webhook), and external engines (OPA Gatekeeper / Kyverno) — and shows the
  admission-webhook plumbing that powers it all.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 12 map]

  │                                                     │                   │
  │                                                     ▼                   │
  │                 Ch42 ADMISSION WEBHOOKS & API EXTENSION (the engine)     │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 12 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: lock the easypj namespace with PSA=restricted +
  ResourceQuota + a CEL policy ("min 2 replicas, approved registries only") +
  a Kyverno rule ("require labels"), and understand the validating/mutating
  webhook flow that enforces them. VISUAL: the layered policy stack.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 40: Security Hardening & Best Practices                      🔴 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Beyond-exam (production security; CKS on-ramp).
  [B] WHY: a passing cluster isn't a secure cluster. VISUAL: 6-layer defense.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: hardening checklist; RBAC hardening; secrets best practice;
      apiserver/audit; runtime (seccomp/AppArmor); supply chain; multi-tenancy.
  40.1 Hardening Checklist 🏢 — 6 layers (infra, authN/Z, admission, workload,
       network, data). VISUAL: layered checklist.
  40.2 RBAC Hardening 🏢 — least privilege; dangerous verbs (escalate/bind/
       impersonate/*); audit bindings.
  40.3 Secrets Management 🏢 — encrypt at rest; volume over env; external stores;
       disable token automount.
  40.4 API Server & Audit 🏢 — --anonymous-auth=false; admission plugins; audit
       policy levels (Metadata for secrets, RequestResponse for RBAC).
  40.5 Runtime Security 🏢 — seccomp RuntimeDefault; AppArmor; read-only rootfs;
       drop capabilities.
  40.6 Supply Chain 🏢 — pin digests; scan (Trivy/Grype); sign (cosign);
       allowlist registries.
  40.7 Multi-Tenancy 🏢 — RBAC + Quota + NetworkPolicy + PSA + LimitRange per ns.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 40
  [DEFENSE IN DEPTH — 6 layers around easypj]
   1 🏗️ Infra      hardened nodes, restricted kubelet, firewall ports
   2 🔐 AuthN/Z    least-privilege RBAC, no anonymous-auth, SA hygiene
   3 🧪 Admission  PodSecurity=restricted, ValidatingAdmissionPolicy
   4 📦 Workload   non-root, read-only rootfs, drop caps, seccomp, no automount
   5 🧯 Network    default-deny NetworkPolicy, egress control
   6 💾 Data       encrypt Secrets at rest, audit log (Metadata for secrets,
                   RequestResponse for RBAC), signed+scanned images
   💡 a breach must defeat EVERY layer — one is never enough.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how audit events flow; how seccomp/AppArmor profiles attach;
      image digest immutability.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): audit policy stages/levels; KMS v2; image-policy
      webhook; Falco runtime detection; SBOM & SLSA provenance; gVisor/Kata
      sandboxing via RuntimeClass; AppArmor profile authoring.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Security is defense-in-depth across 6 layers: infra, authN/Z, admission,
        workload, network, data. A breach must defeat EVERY layer — one is never
        enough.
      • RBAC hardening = least privilege + watching the dangerous verbs (escalate,
        bind, impersonate, and wildcard *) that let a subject grant itself more.
      • Secrets: encrypt at rest (EncryptionConfiguration/KMS), prefer volume
        mounts over env, disable token automount, and consider external stores.
      • apiserver/audit + runtime + supply chain matter: --anonymous-auth=false,
        an audit policy (Metadata for secrets, RequestResponse for RBAC), seccomp/
        AppArmor, and scanned+signed+pinned images from allowlisted registries.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Audit at RequestResponse on Secrets logs their bodies — use Metadata level
        for secrets so you don't leak them into the audit log.
      • `:latest` (or no digest) makes deployments non-reproducible and bypasses
        scan/sign guarantees — pin tags/digests.
      • Broad RBAC (wildcards, cluster-admin handed out) is the most common real
        misconfiguration — audit bindings regularly.
      • Leaving automountServiceAccountToken on across all pods needlessly exposes
        API credentials.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `kubectl auth can-i --list --as=...` audits what a subject can really do;
        the krew `who-can` plugin inverts it (who can do X).
      • Stack per-namespace controls together for tenancy: RBAC + ResourceQuota +
        LimitRange + NetworkPolicy + PSA=restricted.
      • Scan images in CI (Trivy/Grype) and fail the build on criticals — cheaper
        than catching it in the cluster.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl auth can-i --list --as=system:serviceaccount:easypj:easypj-web-sa -n easypj
      kubectl get clusterrolebindings -o wide | grep -i cluster-admin
      kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
      kubectl get secret easypj-secret -o yaml      # verify it's encrypted in etcd
      trivy image easypj:v1                          # supply-chain scan (CI)
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: end-to-end harden easypj — restricted PSA on the namespace, a
      least-privilege Role for its SA (automount off), encryption-at-rest for
      secrets, a default-deny NetworkPolicy, and a scanned/pinned image.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Give easypj's SA the minimum it needs (read its own ConfigMap). How?
          A. A Role scoped to get/list/watch on that ConfigMap (resourceNames for
          get) bound to the SA — no wildcards, nothing cluster-wide.
      Q2. Enable encryption at rest for Secrets.
          A. Configure an EncryptionConfiguration (aescbc/KMS) on the apiserver
          (--encryption-provider-config), then rewrite existing secrets so they're
          re-encrypted.
      Q3. List the controls that harden a workload (pod) itself.
          A. runAsNonRoot, readOnlyRootFilesystem, allowPrivilegeEscalation:false,
          drop ALL capabilities, seccomp RuntimeDefault, automount token off.
      Q4. Set an audit policy that won't leak secret contents.
          A. Log Secrets at Metadata level and sensitive RBAC at RequestResponse;
          never RequestResponse on Secret bodies.
      Q5. What does seccomp RuntimeDefault provide?
          A. The runtime's default syscall filter, blocking dangerous syscalls —
          a baseline runtime-security control required by restricted.
      Q6. Why a read-only root filesystem, and how do apps still write?
          A. It prevents tampering/persistence by attackers; writable paths come
          from explicit emptyDir/PVC mounts.
      Q7. Restrict which registries images may come from.
          A. An admission policy (Kyverno/Gatekeeper or an ImagePolicyWebhook)
          allowlisting approved registries; reject everything else.
      Q8. Reduce the attack surface of the default ServiceAccount.
          A. Set automountServiceAccountToken:false and keep it unprivileged so no
          pod silently inherits API access.
      Q9. Isolate tenants sharing a cluster. Which objects per namespace?
          A. RBAC (scoped), ResourceQuota + LimitRange (fair share), NetworkPolicy
          (default-deny), and PSA=restricted.
      Q10. Find risky cluster bindings.
           A. Audit ClusterRoleBindings for cluster-admin/wildcards and the
           escalate/bind/impersonate verbs (`kubectl get clusterrolebindings`,
           `auth can-i --list`, who-can).
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the 6-layer defense around easypj.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 41: Policies Deep Dive                                       🔴 🎯🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · PSS/Quota are exam-relevant; engines are Day-2.
  [B] WHY: enforce standards automatically instead of trusting humans. VISUAL:
      policy-engine decision tree 🧭.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: PSS levels; LimitRange vs ResourceQuota; PID limits; topology
      spread as policy; ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (CEL); OPA Gatekeeper vs Kyverno.
  41.1 Pod Security Standards — 3 levels 🎯 — privileged/baseline/restricted;
       enforce/warn/audit; version pin (recap + deeper).
  41.2 LimitRange vs ResourceQuota 🎯 — per-pod defaults vs ns totals (recap +
       interplay).
  41.3 PID Limits 🏢 — podPidsLimit; protect nodes from fork bombs.
  41.4 Topology Spread as Policy 🎯 — even distribution as a governance control.
  41.5 ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (CEL) 🎯🏢 — in-cluster, no webhook; example
       "deny <2 replicas".
       apiVersion: admissionregistration.k8s.io/v1
       kind: ValidatingAdmissionPolicy
       metadata: { name: require-min-replicas }
       spec:
         failurePolicy: Fail
         matchConstraints:
           resourceRules:
           - apiGroups: ["apps"]
             apiVersions: ["v1"]
             operations: ["CREATE","UPDATE"]
             resources: ["deployments"]
         validations:
         - expression: "object.spec.replicas >= 2"
           message: "easypj Deployments must have at least 2 replicas"
       ---
       apiVersion: admissionregistration.k8s.io/v1
       kind: ValidatingAdmissionPolicyBinding
       metadata: { name: require-min-replicas-binding }
       spec:
         policyName: require-min-replicas
         validationActions: ["Deny"]
         matchResources:
           namespaceSelector:
             matchLabels: { kubernetes.io/metadata.name: easypj }
  41.6 External Engines — OPA Gatekeeper & Kyverno 🏢 — Rego vs YAML; validate/
       mutate/generate; when to use which.
  41.7 The Complete Policy Stack 🧭 — PSA + VAP + LimitRange + Quota + NetPol +
       PDB working together; enforcement order.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 41
  [THE LAYERED POLICY STACK — enforced on the easypj namespace]
   request to create a pod
        ▼
   🛡️ Pod Security Admission    (enforce=restricted: non-root, drop caps)
        ▼
   🧮 ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (CEL, no webhook: e.g. "replicas >= 2")
        ▼
   📏 LimitRange                (inject default cpu/mem if missing)
        ▼
   📦 ResourceQuota             (namespace totals not exceeded)
        ▼  scheduled
   🧯 NetworkPolicy (who may talk)      🛟 PDB (survive disruptions)
   🧭 engines: built-in CEL · OPA Gatekeeper (Rego) · Kyverno (YAML).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how PSA evaluates at admission; how CEL expressions run in-process;
      how Gatekeeper/Kyverno hook via webhooks.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): CEL variables/functions & messageExpression; VAP
      param resources & bindings; Gatekeeper ConstraintTemplates (Rego) &
      mutation; Kyverno validate/mutate/generate/verifyImages; PolicyReport CRD.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Policy is layered at admission: PSA (pod security) → ValidatingAdmission-
        Policy (CEL rules, in-cluster, NO webhook) → LimitRange (inject defaults)
        → ResourceQuota (namespace totals) → then NetworkPolicy + PDB at runtime.
      • LimitRange sets per-pod/container defaults & bounds; ResourceQuota caps
        the namespace's TOTAL requests/limits/object counts. They work together.
      • ValidatingAdmissionPolicy runs CEL expressions in-process (e.g. "replicas
        >= 2") — no external webhook to deploy or keep alive.
      • External engines (OPA Gatekeeper = Rego, Kyverno = YAML) add validate/
        mutate/generate via webhooks when built-in CEL isn't enough.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • PSA is per-namespace and doesn't evict already-running pods — only new/
        updated ones are checked.
      • Roll policies out as warn/audit BEFORE enforce, or you instantly block
        legitimate deploys.
      • CEL syntax errors (or referencing missing fields) make a VAP misbehave;
        test the expression first.
      • A too-tight ResourceQuota with no LimitRange defaults rejects pods that
        omit requests/limits — pair them.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Use a ValidatingAdmissionPolicy for simple cluster-native rules before
        reaching for a full Gatekeeper/Kyverno install.
      • LimitRange with default + defaultRequest means pods without resources
        still schedule sanely under a Quota.
      • `kubectl get resourcequota -o yaml` shows used vs hard — instantly see
        what's exhausted.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl label ns easypj pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted
      kubectl create quota easypj-quota -n easypj --hard=cpu=4,memory=8Gi,pods=20
      kubectl get resourcequota -n easypj -o yaml      # used vs hard
      kubectl get limitrange -n easypj -o yaml
      kubectl apply -f vap.yaml ; kubectl get validatingadmissionpolicy
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: on the easypj namespace stack PSA=restricted + a LimitRange
      (default cpu/mem) + a ResourceQuota + the "replicas >= 2" VAP, then try to
      create a 1-replica root pod and watch each layer reject/adjust it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Apply the restricted Pod Security Standard to a namespace.
          A. `kubectl label ns easypj
          pod-security.kubernetes.io/enforce=restricted` (warn/audit first).
      Q2. What does a LimitRange do vs a ResourceQuota?
          A. LimitRange sets per-container defaults/min/max; ResourceQuota caps the
          namespace's aggregate requests/limits and object counts.
      Q3. Create a ResourceQuota capping the namespace.
          A. `kubectl create quota easypj-quota --hard=cpu=4,memory=8Gi,pods=20 -n
          easypj` (pods then must declare requests/limits within it).
      Q4. What are PID limits for?
          A. podPidsLimit bounds processes per pod to protect the node from fork
          bombs / runaway process creation.
      Q5. Use topology spread as a policy. How?
          A. topologySpreadConstraints to force even distribution across zones/
          nodes (maxSkew), governing placement as a reliability control.
      Q6. Write a VAP that requires ≥2 replicas on Deployments.
          A. A ValidatingAdmissionPolicy with CEL `object.spec.replicas >= 2` on
          apps/deployments + a Binding with validationActions:[Deny] scoped to the ns.
      Q7. OPA Gatekeeper vs Kyverno — key difference?
          A. Gatekeeper uses Rego (ConstraintTemplates); Kyverno uses YAML policies
          and also does mutate/generate/verifyImages — pick by team familiarity.
      Q8. Why warn before enforce?
          A. To surface violations without blocking deploys, fix them, then flip
          to enforce — avoids an outage from a too-strict rollout.
      Q9. Combine multiple policies on one namespace — do they conflict?
          A. They compose: PSA + VAP + LimitRange + Quota + NetworkPolicy + PDB
          each guard a different aspect; a request must satisfy all that apply.
      Q10. What is the admission enforcement order?
           A. authN → authZ → mutating webhooks → schema validation → validating
           (PSA/VAP/validating webhooks) → persist; defaults (LimitRange) are
           applied during admission before quota is checked.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the layered policy stack over easypj.

  Chapter 42: Admission Webhooks & API Extension                       🔴 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2h · Beyond-exam (the machinery under policy).
  [B] WHY: understand the engine that mutates/validates every request. VISUAL:
      mutating-then-validating webhook flow.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: mutating vs validating webhooks; the request flow; failure
      policy; kubectl plugins & krew; aggregation recap.
  42.1 Mutating vs Validating Webhooks 🏢 — order, purpose, AdmissionReview.
  42.2 The Admission Flow 🏢 — authN -> authZ -> mutating -> schema ->
       validating -> persist. VISUAL: reuse+augment cka_1 admission pipeline.
  42.3 Failure Policy & TLS 🏢 — Fail vs Ignore; caBundle; timeouts.
  42.4 kubectl Plugins & krew 🏢 — extend the CLI (ctx, ns, neat, tree, who-can).
  42.5 API Aggregation (recap) 🔧 — apiservices; extending the API surface.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 42
  [ADMISSION WEBHOOKS — mutate THEN validate]
   request ─► authN ─► authZ
        ▼
   🔧 MutatingWebhookConfiguration   (can MODIFY: inject sidecar, set defaults)
        ▼
   📋 schema validation
        ▼
   ✅ ValidatingWebhookConfiguration (accept / REJECT only)
        ▼
   🗄️ persist in etcd
   ⚠️ failurePolicy: Fail + webhook DOWN ⇒ requests blocked (can wedge the
      cluster). Webhooks need TLS + caBundle.
   🧰 kubectl plugins (krew): ctx · ns · neat · tree · who-can.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: the AdmissionReview request/response JSON; how a mutating webhook
      patches an object; why mutating runs before validating.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): matchPolicy Equivalent/Exact; object/namespace
      Selector; reinvocationPolicy; sideEffects & dryRun support; timeoutSeconds;
      caBundle rotation; webhook vs ValidatingAdmissionPolicy trade-offs.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • The admission chain: authN → authZ → MUTATING webhooks → schema validation
        → VALIDATING webhooks → persist. Mutating runs BEFORE validating so the
        final (mutated) object is what gets validated.
      • Mutating webhooks MODIFY objects (inject sidecars, set defaults);
        validating webhooks only ACCEPT or REJECT. Both speak the AdmissionReview
        request/response JSON.
      • failurePolicy decides what happens when the webhook is unreachable: Fail
        (block the request) or Ignore (allow it). Fail + a down webhook can wedge
        the whole cluster.
      • Webhooks require TLS with a caBundle the apiserver trusts. krew extends
        kubectl itself (ctx, ns, neat, tree, who-can).
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • failurePolicy: Fail + the webhook pod down = every matching request is
        rejected — including the ones you'd use to fix it. Scope match narrowly
        and consider Ignore for non-critical webhooks.
      • A stale/incorrect caBundle (cert rotation) breaks the webhook with TLS
        errors.
      • Webhook ORDER matters: a mutating webhook's output feeds validation; design
        for it.
      • Webhooks that don't declare sideEffects/dryRun support break `--dry-run`
        and diff.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • If the cluster suddenly rejects everything, suspect a webhook: `kubectl get
        validatingwebhookconfigurations,mutatingwebhookconfigurations` and delete/
        fix the offender.
      • Prefer ValidatingAdmissionPolicy (CEL) over a validating webhook when you
        can — no pod to keep alive, no TLS to rotate.
      • Use namespaceSelector/objectSelector to scope a webhook so it can't affect
        kube-system and lock you out.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl get mutatingwebhookconfigurations
      kubectl get validatingwebhookconfigurations
      kubectl delete validatingwebhookconfiguration <bad-one>   # emergency unwedge
      kubectl krew install ns ctx neat tree who-can
      kubectl get apiservices                                   # aggregation layer
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: trace a pod-create request through the admission chain (authN →
      authZ → mutating → schema → validating → persist), then simulate a down
      webhook with failurePolicy:Fail and recover by deleting the configuration.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Mutating vs validating webhook — purpose of each?
          A. Mutating MODIFIES the object (defaults, sidecar injection); validating
          only ACCEPTS or REJECTS it. Mutating runs first.
      Q2. Give the full admission order.
          A. authN → authZ → mutating webhooks → schema validation → validating
          webhooks (and PSA/VAP) → persist to etcd.
      Q3. What does failurePolicy: Fail do when the webhook is down?
          A. Matching requests are rejected (blocked). Ignore would let them
          through. Fail can wedge the cluster if the webhook is broadly scoped.
      Q4. Why do webhooks need TLS/caBundle?
          A. The apiserver calls the webhook over HTTPS and must trust its cert;
          the caBundle in the configuration validates the webhook's server cert.
      Q5. The cluster rejects all creates after a webhook broke. Recovery?
          A. Identify and delete/fix the offending Validating/Mutating-
          WebhookConfiguration (or restore its webhook pod / caBundle).
      Q6. What is krew and give two useful plugins.
          A. The kubectl plugin manager; e.g. `ctx`/`ns` (fast context/namespace
          switching) and `who-can`/`neat`/`tree`.
      Q7. CRD vs API aggregation — recap the distinction.
          A. CRD adds declarative types stored by K8s; aggregation registers a
          separate apiserver (APIService) for custom logic/storage (e.g.
          metrics-server).
      Q8. What's in an AdmissionReview?
          A. The request (object, oldObject, operation, user) sent to the webhook,
          and the response (allowed, optional patch for mutating, status message).
      Q9. Give an example of a mutating webhook in practice.
          A. A service-mesh injector adding an Envoy sidecar to every pod, or
          defaulting securityContext/labels on creation.
      Q10. Give an example of a validating webhook in practice.
           A. Rejecting pods using :latest images or images from non-allowlisted
           registries (Gatekeeper/Kyverno enforce exactly this).
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: a request flowing through the admission chain.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 12 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Security is layered: infra -> authN/Z -> admission -> workload ->      │
  │    network -> data; harden each.                                        │
  │ ✅ Enforce automatically: PSS (namespace labels), ValidatingAdmission-   │
  │    Policy (CEL, no webhook), OPA Gatekeeper/Kyverno (webhook engines).   │
  │ ✅ Resource governance: LimitRange (per-pod) + ResourceQuota (ns total). │
  │ ✅ Webhooks: mutating runs before validating; failurePolicy=Fail can     │
  │    lock the cluster if the webhook is down.                             │
  │ 🎯 EXAM: PSS, LimitRange, ResourceQuota appear; the rest is Day-2 depth. │
  │ 🔑 label ns pod-security · ValidatingAdmissionPolicy · get quota/limits  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 12 VISUAL SUMMARY]: the policy & admission engine guarding easypj.

PART 13: OBSERVABILITY & DAY-2 SRE                                      🔴 🏢

  alerts, SLOs) and the operational practices for running many clusters

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 13 INTRO]
  Passing the exam means you CAN operate a cluster; SRE practice means you
  operate it WELL. This part goes beyond `kubectl top`: a real observability
  stack (the three pillars), alerting and SLOs, and the multi-cluster /
  capacity / cost concerns of running easypj at scale.

  [HOW THE CHAPTERS FIT — Part 13 map]
  ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ Ch43 OBSERVE WELL (metrics/logs/traces/alerts/SLOs) ─▶ Ch44 OPERATE     │
  │                                                  AT SCALE (multi/cost)   │
  └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

  [PART 13 SUMMARY + IMPLEMENTATION]
  Implementation thread: instrument easypj with Prometheus metrics + Grafana
  dashboards + alerts on SLOs (latency/error rate), then reason about running
  it across clusters/regions within a budget.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 43: Observability — Metrics, Logs, Traces, Alerts            🔴 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~2.5h · Beyond-exam (the SRE toolkit).
  [B] WHY: you can't operate what you can't see. VISUAL: the three pillars
      (metrics/logs/traces) feeding alerts.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: the observability stack; Prometheus & PromQL basics; Grafana
      dashboards; alerting & SLO/SLI/error budgets; tracing overview.
  43.1 The Three Pillars 🏢 — metrics vs logs vs traces; what each answers.
  43.2 Prometheus 🏢 — pull model; exporters; ServiceMonitor; basic PromQL on
       easypj (request rate, error rate, p95 latency).
  43.3 Grafana 🏢 — dashboards; key easypj panels (RED/USE methods).
  43.4 Alerting & SLOs 🏢 — Alertmanager; SLI/SLO/error budget; alert on symptoms
       not causes.
  43.5 Tracing 🏢 — OpenTelemetry; following a /jobs/ request across services.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 43
  [THE THREE PILLARS — observe easypj in production]
   📈 METRICS (Prometheus)  what & how much?  rate · p95 latency · error %
   📜 LOGS    (Loki/EFK)     what happened?    structured stdout/stderr
   🔗 TRACES  (OTel)         where's the time? a /jobs/ request across services
        └──────────────┬──────────────┘
                       ▼
        🚨 Alerts (Alertmanager) on SLOs/symptoms ─► error budget
   📖 RED (Rate/Errors/Duration) for services · USE (Util/Sat/Errors) for nodes.
   ⚠️ alert on SYMPTOMS not causes · watch metric CARDINALITY.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how Prometheus discovers targets; cardinality pitfalls; recording
      vs alerting rules.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): PromQL recording rules & histogram_quantile;
      exemplars & native histograms; OTel collector pipelines; SLO burn-rate
      alerts; remote-write & long-term storage (Thanos/Mimir).
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Three pillars: METRICS (Prometheus — what & how much, rate/latency/errors),
        LOGS (Loki/EFK — what happened), TRACES (OpenTelemetry — where the time
        went across services). Together they feed ALERTS.
      • Prometheus is pull-based: it scrapes exporters/ServiceMonitors. Core
        PromQL: rate() for per-second rates, histogram_quantile() for p95 latency.
      • Use RED (Rate/Errors/Duration) for services and USE (Utilization/
        Saturation/Errors) for resources/nodes to decide what to dashboard.
      • Alert on SYMPTOMS/SLOs (user-visible: error rate, latency, budget burn),
        not raw causes — and watch metric cardinality so Prometheus stays healthy.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • High-cardinality labels (user id, request id) explode Prometheus memory —
        keep label sets bounded.
      • Alerting on every cause produces alert fatigue; alert on a few meaningful
        SLO/symptom conditions instead.
      • Dashboards without SLOs are pretty but don't tell you if you're OK — define
        objectives, not just graphs.
      • histogram_quantile needs proper bucket boundaries; bad buckets give
        meaningless p95s.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • RED for services, USE for resources — a fast mental template for "what do I
        measure here?".
      • `rate(http_requests_total{status=~"5.."}[5m]) / rate(http_requests_total[5m])`
        is the canonical error-rate query.
      • Start alerts from the SLO (e.g. 99.9% success) and a burn-rate rule, not
        from arbitrary thresholds.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS/QUERIES
      kubectl top pods -n easypj                       # quick, no Prometheus
      # PromQL — easypj request rate:
      sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj"}[5m]))
      # error rate:
      sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj",status=~"5.."}[5m]))
        / sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj"}[5m]))
      # p95 latency:
      histogram_quantile(0.95, sum by (le)(rate(http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])))
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: write the PromQL for easypj's error rate and p95 latency, build a
      Grafana panel for each, and define one SLO-based alert (e.g. error rate
      > 1% for 5m) on it.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Name the three pillars and what each answers.
          A. Metrics (what/how much), logs (what happened), traces (where the time
          went across services) — together feeding alerts.
      Q2. Write PromQL for easypj's request rate.
          A. `sum(rate(http_requests_total{app="easypj"}[5m]))` — per-second rate
          averaged over 5m, summed across pods.
      Q3. Compute p95 latency in PromQL.
          A. `histogram_quantile(0.95, sum by (le)(rate(
          http_request_duration_seconds_bucket[5m])))`.
      Q4. RED vs USE — when each?
          A. RED (Rate/Errors/Duration) for request-driven SERVICES; USE
          (Utilization/Saturation/Errors) for RESOURCES like nodes/disks.
      Q5. What is a ServiceMonitor?
          A. A Prometheus-Operator CRD that tells Prometheus which Services/
          endpoints to scrape (label selector + port/path).
      Q6. Define SLI, SLO, and error budget.
          A. SLI = a measured indicator (e.g. success ratio); SLO = the target for
          it (99.9%); error budget = 1−SLO, the allowable failure you can "spend".
      Q7. Why alert on symptoms not causes?
          A. Symptoms (latency/errors) map to user impact and catch unknown
          failure modes; cause-alerts are noisy and miss novel problems.
      Q8. Build a useful easypj Grafana panel. What goes on it?
          A. RED signals — request rate, error %, and p95/p99 latency for /jobs —
          ideally with the SLO line drawn.
      Q9. Trace a slow /jobs/ request across services. Tool and idea?
          A. OpenTelemetry traces — a trace id follows the request through web →
          db, showing per-span time so you find the bottleneck.
      Q10. What is a cardinality risk and how do you avoid it?
           A. Labels with unbounded values (user/request ids) create millions of
           series and OOM Prometheus; keep labels low-cardinality / drop them.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: easypj observability stack, one diagram.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 44: Multi-Cluster, Capacity & Cost                           🔴 🏢
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🔴 · ⏱️ ~1.5h · Beyond-exam (running at scale, on a budget).
  [B] WHY: real platforms are many clusters with real bills. VISUAL: multi-
      cluster topology + cost levers.
  [C] OBJECTIVES: multi-cluster patterns; capacity planning; right-sizing & cost;
      autoscaling layers together.
  44.1 Multi-Cluster Patterns 🏢 — per-env/per-region/per-tenant; fleet mgmt.
  44.2 Capacity Planning 🏢 — requests as the unit; headroom; bin-packing.
  44.3 Right-Sizing & Cost 🏢 — requests vs usage; over-provisioning; spot nodes;
       cost tools (OpenCost/Kubecost).
  44.4 Autoscaling Layers 🧭 — HPA (pods) + VPA (sizing) + Cluster Autoscaler
       (nodes) together.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 44
  [THREE AUTOSCALERS — different axes, used together]
   📈 HPA  ─ more/fewer PODS        (on CPU/mem/custom metrics)
   📐 VPA  ─ bigger/smaller REQUESTS (right-size one pod)
   🖥️ Cluster Autoscaler ─ more/fewer NODES (when pods can't fit)
   easypj-web: HPA 3->10 on CPU · VPA tunes requests · CA adds nodes if Pending
   ⚠️ HPA + VPA on the SAME metric conflict — split their signals.

  [REQUESTS = the unit of BOTH scheduling AND cost]
   requests too HIGH ─► poor bin-packing, idle $$$   (over-provisioned)
   requests too LOW  ─► evictions, noisy neighbors    (under-provisioned)
   🧭 right-size from real usage (kubectl top / VPA recommendations).

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how requests drive both scheduling and cost; bin-packing & node
      utilization math.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): Karpenter consolidation; topology-aware bin-
      packing; spot/preemptible handling (PDB + interruption); VPA recommender
      right-sizing; Kubecost/OpenCost allocation; fleet GitOps.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • Multi-cluster patterns split by environment, region, or tenant; fleets are
        managed with GitOps + a control plane (Argo/Flux/Fleet) rather than by hand.
      • Requests are the unit of BOTH scheduling and cost. Too high = idle, wasted
        spend (bad bin-packing); too low = evictions and noisy neighbors. Right-
        size from real usage.
      • Three autoscalers on different axes: HPA scales POD COUNT (metrics), VPA
        right-sizes a pod's REQUESTS, Cluster Autoscaler scales NODE COUNT (when
        pods can't fit).
      • Cost levers: right-size requests, autoscale (avoid idle nodes), use spot/
        preemptible nodes (with PDBs), and measure allocation (OpenCost/Kubecost).
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Pods without requests wreck both scheduling (bin-packing) and cost
        accounting — always set requests.
      • HPA and VPA acting on the SAME metric fight each other (VPA changes
        requests while HPA scales on them) — separate their signals or use VPA in
        recommend-only mode.
      • Spot/preemptible nodes can vanish with short notice; protect workloads with
        PDBs and spread, and keep critical pods on on-demand.
      • Over-provisioned headroom feels safe but is pure cost — size it
        deliberately.
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • Compare `kubectl top pods` to the declared requests to spot over/under-
        provisioning instantly.
      • Let VPA's recommender suggest requests even if you don't auto-apply them.
      • Put per-env differences (replicas/requests) in Kustomize overlays so the
        same base scales across clusters.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS
      kubectl top pods -n easypj
      kubectl get deploy easypj-web -o jsonpath='{.spec.template.spec.containers[0].resources}'
      kubectl get hpa -n easypj
      kubectl describe node <n> | sed -n '/Allocated resources/,/Events/p'
      kubectl get nodes -L node.kubernetes.io/instance-type,topology.kubernetes.io/zone
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: pull easypj-web's actual usage with `kubectl top`, compare it to
      its requests/limits, set right-sized requests, add an HPA, and reason about
      when Cluster Autoscaler would add a node.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each:
      Q1. Name a multi-cluster pattern and its driver.
          A. Per-environment (dev/stage/prod), per-region (latency/DR), or
          per-tenant (isolation) — chosen for blast-radius, locality, or compliance.
      Q2. How do you estimate cluster capacity?
          A. Sum pod REQUESTS (the scheduling unit) + headroom, then size node
          count/type so requests bin-pack with utilization targets.
      Q3. Right-size easypj-web. Method?
          A. Compare `kubectl top` actual usage vs requests/limits (or VPA
          recommendations) and set requests near real usage with sane limits.
      Q4. HPA vs VPA vs Cluster Autoscaler — roles?
          A. HPA = more/fewer pods (on metrics); VPA = bigger/smaller pod requests;
          Cluster Autoscaler = more/fewer nodes when pods are unschedulable/idle.
      Q5. Trade-offs of spot/preemptible nodes?
          A. Much cheaper but can be reclaimed anytime; safe only for interruption-
          tolerant workloads with PDBs/spread and on-demand fallback.
      Q6. Why do requests drive bin-packing?
          A. The scheduler places pods by their requests against node allocatable;
          inflated requests waste capacity, tiny ones overcommit and cause evictions.
      Q7. Give two concrete cost levers.
          A. Right-size requests + autoscale to remove idle nodes; use spot nodes
          and internal LBs where appropriate.
      Q8. What is headroom and why have it?
          A. Spare capacity reserved for spikes/failover so the cluster can absorb
          load or a node loss without immediate Pending pods — sized deliberately.
      Q9. Symptoms of over-provisioning?
          A. Low node utilization, requests >> usage, idle nodes the autoscaler
          can't remove — paying for capacity you don't use.
      Q10. Why might per-environment clusters be preferred over namespaces?
           A. Stronger isolation (separate control planes/blast radius), independent
           upgrades, and clearer cost/security boundaries than namespace separation.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: easypj across clusters with the 3 autoscalers.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 13 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Three pillars: metrics (Prometheus), logs, traces (OTel) -> alerts.   │
  │    Alert on symptoms/SLOs, not raw causes; RED for services, USE for     │
  │    resources.                                                           │
  │ ✅ Requests are the unit of both scheduling AND cost; right-size from    │
  │    real usage; combine HPA (pods) + VPA (size) + Cluster Autoscaler.     │
  │ 🏢 BEYOND: this is the SRE layer that turns "can operate" into "operate  │
  │    well" — not exam-graded but career-defining.                         │
  │ 🔑 kubectl top · PromQL rate()/histogram_quantile · get hpa             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 13 VISUAL SUMMARY]: see easypj, alert on it, scale it, afford it.

PART 14: EXAM MASTERY & FINAL PREP                                      🎯
  Chapter 45  ·  Pass on the first attempt  ·  "Prove it"
  What you'll build: a battle plan — setup ritual, 3-pass time strategy, the
  do's/don'ts, the top-10 point-losers, and a full timed simulator on easypj.
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

  [PART 14 INTRO]
  Knowing Kubernetes is necessary but not sufficient: the CKA is a SPEED +
  ACCURACY test under a 2-hour clock. This part converts knowledge into points.

  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  Chapter 45: CKA Exam Strategy & Simulator                            🎯
  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [A] META: 🎯 · ⏱️ ~3h (practice) · The capstone.
  [B] WHY: most failures are time/accuracy, not knowledge. VISUAL: time-budget
      bar (setup 5m · pass1 35m · pass2 50m · pass3 30m).
  [C] OBJECTIVES: run the setup ritual; apply the 3-pass strategy; avoid the
      top point-losers; complete a full simulator at exam pace.
  45.1 The First 5 Minutes — setup ritual 🎯 — aliases (k, kgp, $do, $now),
       bash completion, vim (ts=2/sw=2/expandtab), bookmark docs, verify env.
       # ~/.bashrc  (source it: `source ~/.bashrc`)
       alias k=kubectl
       source <(kubectl completion bash)
       complete -o default -F __start_kubectl k
       export do="--dry-run=client -o yaml"     # k create deploy x $do > x.yaml
       export now="--force --grace-period=0"     # k delete pod x $now (fast delete)
       alias kgp='kubectl get pods -o wide'
       alias kn='kubectl config set-context --current --namespace'
       # ~/.vimrc
       set tabstop=2 shiftwidth=2 expandtab
       set number autoindent
       # paste-safe YAML: :set paste before pasting blocks
  45.1b Verify env each task 🎯 — ALWAYS `kubectl config use-context <c>` and set
       the namespace before working; the exam switches clusters per question.
  45.2 The 3-Pass Time Strategy 🎯 — Pass1 quick wins (<4m), Pass2 medium (5-8m),
       Pass3 hard + verify; never >10m on one task. VISUAL: 3-pass roadmap.
  45.3 Imperative vs Declarative on the Clock 🧭 — imperative for speed; generate
       YAML with $do then edit; copy NetworkPolicy/Ingress/Gateway from docs.
  45.4 Domain-by-Domain Strategy 🎯 — how to attack each of the 5 domains;
       key commands per domain.
  45.5 Top 10 Point-Losers ⚠️ — wrong context/namespace, typos, indentation,
       not verifying, hand-writing YAML, forgetting $now, etcd restore order,
       deprecated APIs, over-time on one task, leaving blanks.
  45.6 Scoring & Partial Credit 🎯 — weights; partial credit; auto-grading;
       66% to pass; verify everything.
  45.7 Exam-Day Checklist 🎯 — before/during/after (ID, room scan, PSI browser,
       context switch every question, results in 24h, free retake, valid 3y).
  45.8 THE SIMULATOR 🧪🎯 — 20 timed performance tasks on easypj, weighted to
       the real domains, with Show Answer + Show Explanation (+YAML/diagram):
         Cluster Arch (25%): H1 RBAC SA+Role+Binding · H2 kubeadm upgrade ·
           H3 etcd backup+restore · H4 static pod · H5 CSR approve
         Workloads (15%): H6 rolling update+rollback · H7 HPA · H8 taint+toleration
         Services/Net (20%): H9 expose NodePort · H10 NetworkPolicy default-deny ·
           H11 Ingress · H12 Gateway API HTTPRoute
         Storage (10%): H13 dynamic PVC · H14 mount PVC in pod
         Troubleshooting (30%): H15 NotReady node · H16 CrashLoop · H17 Service
           no endpoints · H18 DNS · H19 PVC Pending · H20 broken kubeconfig
       Target: 20 tasks in <120 min; self-score; 75%+ = ready.
  📊 KEY DIAGRAMS — Chapter 45
  [THE 120-MINUTE BATTLE PLAN — maximize points per minute]
   ⚡ Setup  (5m)  ████  aliases · completion · vim · bookmarks · context
   🟢 Pass 1 (35m) ████████████        every task < 4 min  (quick wins)
   🟡 Pass 2 (50m) ██████████████████  5-8 min tasks       (bulk of score)
   🔴 Pass 3 (30m) ██████████          hardest + VERIFY everything
   Rules: switch CONTEXT every question · generate YAML with $do · never camp
   >10 min on one task · 66% to pass · NEVER leave a question blank.

  [G] 🔧 UTH: how exam tasks are auto-graded (cluster-state checks) — why exact
      names/namespaces/labels matter.
      🔬 DEEPER (exam+beyond): the PSI remote-desktop quirks; killer.sh sessions;
      tmux & copy-paste; vimrc for YAML; fast ctx/ns aliases; docs-search
      workflow; flagging questions; the 2025 syllabus deltas to drill.
  [H] 🔑 KEY TAKEAWAYS
      • The CKA is a 2-hour, hands-on SPEED + ACCURACY test (66% to pass). Most
        failures are time/process, not knowledge.
      • Spend the first 5 minutes on the setup ritual: k alias + completion, $do
        (dry-run YAML) + $now (fast delete), vim 2-space expandtab, doc bookmarks.
      • Work 3 passes: quick wins (<4m) → medium (5–8m) → hardest + verify. Never
        camp >10 min on one task; never leave a question blank (partial credit).
      • Switch CONTEXT/namespace every question, generate YAML instead of hand-
        writing it, copy NetworkPolicy/Ingress/Gateway skeletons from the docs,
        and VERIFY after every task.
  [I] ⚠️ GOTCHAS
      • Skipping the setup ritual costs minutes on every single task afterward.
      • Forgetting `kubectl config use-context` (or the namespace) → you do correct
        work in the WRONG cluster and score zero.
      • Perfectionism: polishing one task while three easy ones go untouched.
      • Hand-writing YAML (typos/indentation) instead of generating it with $do.
      • Forgetting $now on deletes, or deprecated API versions (extensions/v1beta1).
  [J] ⚡ SPEED TIPS
      • `k create deploy easypj-web --image=easypj:v1 $do > d.yaml` then edit — far
        faster and safer than writing from scratch.
      • Bookmark kubernetes.io/docs for NetworkPolicy, Ingress, and Gateway API —
        copy-paste-adjust beats memorizing YAML.
      • Verify with the same command set every time: get -o wide, describe, auth
        can-i, and a curl from a temp pod.
  [K] 🔑 COMMANDS (the exam alias/dry-run kit)
      alias k=kubectl ; source <(kubectl completion bash) ; complete -F __start_kubectl k
      export do='--dry-run=client -o yaml' ; export now='--force --grace-period=0'
      kubectl config get-contexts ; kubectl config use-context <c>
      kubectl config set-context --current --namespace=<ns>
      k create deploy web --image=nginx $do > web.yaml ; k apply -f web.yaml
      kubectl explain <kind>.spec --recursive | less     # field lookup, no docs
  [L] 🧪 DRILL: run the full 20-task simulator (§45.8) under a 120-minute clock
      using the 3-pass strategy; self-score; 75%+ means book the exam.
  [M] 🧪 LAB Q&A (10) — full answer + explanation each (the simulator condensed):
      Q1. First thing you do when the exam starts?
          A. The setup ritual: k alias + bash completion, $do/$now exports, vim
          config, doc bookmarks, and confirm the current context.
      Q2. How do you avoid working in the wrong cluster?
          A. Run `kubectl config use-context <c>` (and set the namespace) at the
          START of every question — the exam switches clusters per task.
      Q3. Outline the 3-pass time strategy.
          A. Pass 1: all <4-min quick wins. Pass 2: 5–8-min tasks (bulk of score).
          Pass 3: hardest tasks + verify everything. Never >10 min on one.
      Q4. Create a Deployment fast without writing YAML by hand.
          A. `k create deploy easypj-web --image=easypj:v1 $do > d.yaml`, edit,
          `k apply -f d.yaml`.
      Q5. RBAC task: SA + Role + Binding to read pods. Fast path?
          A. `k create sa web -n easypj`; `k create role r --verb=get,list,watch
          --resource=pods -n easypj`; `k create rolebinding rb --role=r
          --serviceaccount=easypj:web -n easypj`; verify with `auth can-i`.
      Q6. etcd backup+restore — the must-not-miss order?
          A. snapshot save (with certs) → verify status → restore to a NEW
          data-dir → repoint the etcd static pod → verify objects.
      Q7. A Service has no endpoints in a troubleshooting task. Fix approach?
          A. Compare the Service selector to the pods' labels and check readiness;
          align them so ready pods populate the EndpointSlice.
      Q8. How do you handle a task you can't finish in 10 minutes?
          A. Flag it, leave a best-effort partial answer (partial credit), move on,
          and return in Pass 3 if time remains — never leave it blank.
      Q9. Why generate YAML and copy policy/ingress skeletons from docs?
          A. To avoid indentation/typo errors and save time; the exam allows
          kubernetes.io docs — adjust a known-good template instead of authoring.
      Q10. What's the single highest-value habit during the exam?
           A. Verify after EVERY task (get/describe/curl) so you never lose points
           to a silent mistake — accuracy compounds across 15–20 tasks.
  [N] VISUAL SUMMARY: the exam battle plan on one page.

  ┌──────────────────────── PART 14 — KEY TAKEAWAYS ────────────────────────┐
  │ ✅ Spend the first 5 minutes on aliases/completion/vim/bookmarks.        │
  │ ✅ 3 passes: quick wins -> medium -> hard; never camp on one task; never │
  │    leave a question blank (partial credit is real).                     │
  │ ✅ Switch context EVERY question; verify after EVERY task; generate YAML.│
  │ ✅ 66% to pass — maximize points/minute, not perfection.                │
  │ 🎯 The simulator on easypj is your readiness gate: 75%+ = book the exam. │
  │ 🔑 k · $do · $now · config use-context · auth can-i · explain           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  [PART 14 VISUAL SUMMARY]: setup -> 3 passes -> verify -> pass.
